But he was above all things a romantic and sentimental youth, and he was determined to see this country as he had expected to see it; so he plodded on, his coat-collar up, British obstinacy in his eyes and a little excited flutter in his heart whenever a bright colour, an Eastern face, a street pedlar, a bunched-up, high-backed coachman, anything or anyone unusual presented itself.
He saw on his right a great church; it stood back from the street, having in front of it a desolate little arrangement of bushes and public seats and winding paths. The church itself was approached by flights of steps that disappeared under the shadow of a high dome supported by vast stone pillars. Letters in gold flamed across the building above the pillars.
Henry passed the intervening ground and climbed the steps. Under the pillars before the heavy, swinging doors were two rows of beggars; they were dirtier, more tousled and tangled, fiercer and more ironically falsely submissive than any beggars that, he had ever seen. He described one fellow to me, a fierce brigand with a high black hat of feathers, a soiled Cossack coat and tall dirty red leather boots; his eyes were fires, Henry said. At any rate that is what Henry liked to think they were. There was a woman with no legs and a man with neither nose nor ears.

===Chinese===

洗了澡，刮了脸，吃了早饭，他觉得又快活又宽慰。照我看，他一向对身体的感受起反应起得极快，何况他还太年轻，不知道鬼魂不是梳梳头、擦擦脸就能安顿的。早饭后他往床上一躺又睡着了，这回没有梦；他睡得像个英国人，无梦，健康，干净。醒来时，他同先前一样自信满满。……你瞧，头一道咒语还不够。……
他一头扎进这座城。天正下着那种又浓又黑的雨，仿佛没落地就先带了泥。全城罩在薄雾里，人影现了又没，电车叮当，俄语里那些狂野的古怪叫喊——初听那么罗曼蒂克、那么惊心——又尖利又懒散地升上空中。他一路闯去，闯到了涅瓦大街——那气派和笃定不会认错，尽管在泥泞雨水中显得灰扑扑的。
可他到底是个罗曼蒂克的、伤感的孩子，打定了主意要按自己料想的样子来看这个国度；于是他埋头往前走，衣领竖着，眼里是英国人的执拗，心口一小阵兴奋的扑腾——每逢一点鲜亮的颜色、一张东方的脸、一个街头小贩、一个缩着脖子的、高背的马车夫，任何不寻常的东西或人。
他看见右手边一座大教堂；它退在街后，前面是一小片荒凉的灌木、公用的座椅和弯弯的小径。教堂本身要登几段台阶才到，台阶没入高高的穹顶投下的影子，穹顶撑在巨大的石柱上。金色的字母在柱子上方的建筑上燃烧。
亨利穿过空地，登上台阶。沉重的摆动的门前的廊柱下，立着两排乞丐；比他生平见过的任何乞丐都更脏、更蓬头垢面、更凶，也更讥诮地、更假模假样地卑顺。他向我形容过其中一个，一条凶悍的响马，戴一顶高高的羽毛黑帽，穿一件油污的哥萨克外衣，一双脏兮兮的红色高统皮靴；他的眼睛是两团火，亨利说。至少亨利愿意这么想。还有一个没有腿的女人，一个鼻子耳朵都没有的男人。

===Original===

I am sure that they watched Henry with supplicating hostility. He entered the church and was instantly swallowed up by a vast multitude.
He described to me afterwards that it was as though he had been pushed (by the evil, eager fingers of the beggars no doubt) into deep water. He rose with a gasp, and was first conscious of a strange smell of dirt and tallow and something that he did not know, but was afterwards to recognise as the scent of sunflower seed. He was pushed upon, pressed and pulled, fingered and crushed. He did not mind﻿—he was glad﻿—this was what he wanted. He looked about him and found that he and all the people round him were swimming in a hazy golden mist flung into the air from the thousands of lighted candles that danced in the breeze blowing through the building. The whole vast shining floor was covered with peasants, pressed, packed together. Peasants, men and women﻿—he did not see a single member of the middle-class. In front of him under the altar there was a blaze of light, and figures moved in the blaze uncertainly, indistinctly. Now and then a sudden quiver passed across the throng, as wind blows through the corn. Here and there men and women knelt, but for the most part they stood steadfast, motionless, staring in front of them. He looked at them and discovered that they had the faces of children﻿—simple, trustful, unintelligent, unhumorous children﻿—and eyes, always kindlier than any he had ever seen in other human beings. They stood there gravely, with no signs of religious fervour, with no marks of impatience or weariness and also with no evidence of any especial interest in what was occurring. It might have been a vast concourse of sleepwalkers.
He saw that three soldiers near to him were holding hands.﻿ ﻿…
From the lighted altars came the echoing whisper of a monotonous chant. The sound rose and fell, scarcely a voice, scarcely an appeal, something rising from the place itself and sinking back into it again without human agency.
After a time he saw a strange movement that at first he could not understand. Then watching, he found that unlit candles were being passed from line to line, one man leaning forward and tapping the man in front of him with the candle, the man in front passing it, in his turn, forward, and so on until at last it reached the altar where it was lighted and fastened into its sconce.

===Chinese===

我敢说他们望着亨利，眼里是恳求的敌意。他进了教堂，立刻被巨大的人群吞没。
他后来向我形容，那就像被人一推（多半是乞丐们那些恶毒而急切的手指），推进了深水。他喘着冒出头，先闻到一股奇怪的气味，污垢、油脂，还有一样他叫不出、后来才认出的——葵花子的香。他被挤、被压、被拽、被摸索、被夹。他不在乎——他高兴——这正是他要的。他四下张望，发现他同周围所有的人都游在一层雾蒙蒙的金霭里，那霭是成千支点着的蜡烛抛上空中的，烛火在穿堂的风里舞。整片发亮的大地面铺满了农民，挤紧，压实。农民，男人和女人——中产阶级他一个也没看见。他前面的圣坛下一片光焰腾腾，人影在光焰里晃动，看不真切。人群不时掠过一阵突来的颤，像风过麦田。到处有男男女女跪着，但大半都笔直地站着，一动不动，望着前面。他打量他们，发现他们长着孩子的脸——单纯、轻信、不聪明、不苟言笑的孩子——眼睛呢，永远比他在旁人身上见过的更和善。他们严肃地立在那里，没有宗教狂热的迹象，没有不耐或疲倦的痕迹，对眼前进行的事也没有特别的兴致。那仿佛是一场大规模的梦游者的集会。
他看见近旁三个士兵手拉着手。……
从灯烛辉煌的祭坛传来单调的圣咏的回声似的低吟。那声音起起落落，几乎不成声，几乎不成为呼求，像是从这个地方本身升起来，又沉回它里面去，不经人手。
过了一阵他看见一种起初看不懂的奇怪动作。细看之下，才发现是没点的蜡烛在一排排人手里传递，一个人俯身用蜡烛点点前面那人的背，前面的人接过去再往前传，如此往下，最后传到祭坛，点亮，插进烛座。

===Original===

This tapping with the candles happened incessantly throughout the vast crowd. Henry himself was tapped, and felt suddenly as though he had been admitted a member of some secret society. He felt the tap again and again, and soon he seemed to be hypnotised by the low chant at the altar and the motionless silent crowd and the dim golden mist. He stood, not thinking, not living, away, away, questioning nothing, wanting nothing.﻿ ﻿…
He must of course finish with his romantic notion. People pushed around him, struggling to get out. He turned to go and was faced, he told me, with a remarkable figure. His description, romantic and sentimental though he tried to make it, resolved itself into nothing more than the sketch of an ordinary peasant, tall, broad, black-bearded, neatly clad in blue shirt, black trousers, and high boots. This fellow stood apparently away from the crowd, apart, and watched it all, as you so often may see the Russian peasant doing, with indifferent gaze. In his mild blue eyes Bohun fancied that he saw all kinds of things﻿—power, wisdom, prophecy﻿—a figure apart and symbolic. But how easy in Russia it is to see symbols and how often those symbols fail to justify themselves! Well, I let Bohun have his fancies. “I should know that man anywhere again,” he declared. “It was as though he knew what was going to happen and was ready for it.” Then I suppose he saw my smile, for he broke off and said no more.
And here for a moment I leave him and his adventures.

===Chinese===

这用蜡烛点人的动作，在浩大的人群里不停进行。亨利自己也被点了一下，忽然觉得自己被某个秘密会社接收为会员了。他一次又一次被点，不久他仿佛被祭坛前低低的吟唱、静止无声的人群和昏金色的雾催了眠。他站着，不想，不活，远去，远去，什么也不问，什么也不要。……
他的罗曼蒂克念头当然得分个了结。人们在他四围挤着往外拥。他转身要走，同——他告诉我——一个异样的人物打了照面。他那段形容，尽管竭力往浪漫伤感里描，归结起来不过是普通一个农民的素描：高个子，宽肩膀，黑胡子，蓝衬衫、黑裤子、高统靴，穿得干净齐整。这人仿佛立在人群之外，隔开一点，冷眼看着这一切——俄国农民这样子你常常看得见。博亨在他那双温和的蓝眼睛里，自以为看见了种种东西——力量、智慧、预言——一个孤立的、象征的身影。可在俄国，看见象征多么容易，而那些象征又是多么经常兑现不了！好吧，我由着博亨去想他的。“那个人我到哪儿都再认得出来，”他宣布，“他那样子，仿佛知道要出什么事，而且在等着它。”随后我猜他看见了我在笑，便收住不说了。
这里，我把他和他的奇遇暂时放下。

## Part / 第一部 薇拉与妮娜
===Original===

I Vera and Nina

===Chinese===

I 薇拉与妮娜

### VI / 六
===Original===

I must speak, for a moment, of myself. Throughout the autumn and winter of 1914 and the spring and summer of 1915 I was with the Russian Red Cross on the Polish and Galician fronts. During the summer and early autumn of 1915 I shared with the Ninth Army the retreat through Galicia. Never very strong physically, owing to a lameness of the left hip from which I have suffered from birth, the difficulties of the retreat and the loss of my two greatest friends gave opportunities to my arch-enemy Sciatica to do what he wished with me, and in October 1915 I was forced to leave the Front and return to Petrograd. I was an invalid throughout the whole of that winter, and only gradually during the spring of 1916 was able to pull myself back to an old shadow of my former vigour and energy. I saw that I would never be good for the Front again, but I minded that the less now in that the events of the summer of 1915 had left me without heart or desire, the merest spectator of life, passive and, I cynically believed, indifferent. I was nothing to anyone, nor was anyone anything to me. The desire of my heart had slipped like a laughing ghost away from my ken﻿—men of my slow warmth and cautious suspicion do not easily admit a new guest.﻿ ﻿…
Moreover during this spring of 1916 Petrograd, against my knowledge, wove webs about my feet. I had never shared the common belief that Moscow was the only town in Russia. I had always known that Petrograd had its own grace and beauty, but it was not until, sore and sick at heart, lonely and bitter against fate, haunted always by the face and laughter of one whom I would never see again, I wandered about the canals and quays and deserted byways of the city that I began to understand its spirit. I took, to the derision of my few friends, two tumbledown rooms on Pilot’s Island, at the far end of Ekateringofsky Prospect. Here amongst tangled grass, old, deserted boats, stranded, ruined cottages and abraided piers, I hung above the sea.

===Chinese===

我得说一会儿我自己。1914年秋冬和1915年春夏，我随俄国红十字会待在波兰和加利西亚前线。1915年夏末初秋，我又同第九集团军一道经历了加利西亚大撤退。我身体向来不强，先天左胯的残疾让我吃了一辈子苦，撤退的艰辛加上两位至友的丧生，给我的死敌坐骨神经痛大开了方便之门，它爱拿我怎样就怎样，1915年10月我被迫离开前线，回到彼得格勒。那一整个冬天我是个病人，直到1916年春才慢慢拽回来几分旧日精力的影子。我明白自己再上也顶不了用了，可我也不那么在意了，因为1915年夏天的那些事已把我掏得没了心气、没了欲望，只剩一个生活的旁观者，被动的，而且——我犬儒地相信——冷漠的。我对谁都不是什么，谁对我也不是什么。我心中的愿望像个笑着的鬼影，溜出了我的视野——我这种温吞而多疑的人，是不轻易再放新客进门的。……
再说，1916年这个春天，彼得格勒背着我，在我脚下结起了网。我从不信莫斯科是俄国唯一的城那种通说。我一向知道彼得格勒自有它的风致和美，可直到我心痛身虚、孤单而怨命、永远被一张再不能见的脸和一阵笑声纠缠着，在这座城的运河、河岸和僻巷里乱走时，我才开始懂它的魂。我不顾寥寥几个朋友的嗤笑，在皮洛特岛上、叶卡捷琳戈夫斯基大街的尽头，赁下了两间破屋。这里，乱草丛中，废旧的老船，搁浅的破屋，剥蚀的木桩之间，我悬在海的上空。

===Original===

Not indeed the sea of my Glebeshire memories; this was a sluggish, tideless sea, but in the winter one sheet of ice, stretching far beyond the barrier of the eye, catching into its frosted heart every colour of the sky and air, the lights of the town, the lamps of imprisoned barges, the moon, the sun, the stars, the purple sunsets, and the strange, mysterious lights that flash from the shadows of the hovering snow-clouds. My rooms were desolate perhaps, bare boards with holes, an old cracked mirror, a stove, a bookcase, a photograph, and a sketch of Rafiel Cove. My friends looked and shivered; I, staring from my window on to the entrance into the waterways of the city, felt that any magic might come out of that strange desolation and silence. A shadow like the sweeping of the wing of a great bird would hover above the ice; a bell from some boat would ring, then the church bells of the city would answer it; the shadow would pass and the moon would rise, deep gold, and lie hard and sharp against the thick, impending air; the shadow would pass and the stars come out, breaking with an almost audible crackle through the stuff of the sky﻿ ﻿… and only five minutes away the shop-lights were glittering, the *isvostchicks* crying to clear the road, the tram-bells clanging, the boys shouting the news. Around and about me marvellous silence.﻿ ﻿…
In the early autumn of 1916 I met at a dinner-party Nicolai Leontievitch Markovitch. In the course of a conversation I informed him that I had been for a year with the Ninth Army in Galicia, and he then asked me whether I had met his wife’s uncle Alexei Petrovitch Semyonov, who was also with the Ninth Army. It happened that I had known Alexei Petrovitch very well and the sound of his name brought back to me so vividly events and persons with whom we had both been connected that I had difficulty in controlling my sudden emotion. Markovitch invited me to his house. He lived, he told me, with his wife in a flat in the Anglisky Prospect; his sister-in-law and another of his wife’s uncles, a brother of Alexei Petrovitch, also lived with them. I said that I would be very glad to come.
It is impossible to describe how deeply, in the days that followed, I struggled against the attraction that this invitation presented to me.

===Chinese===

自然不是我记忆里格勒本郡的海；这是一片滞缓的、无潮的海，可一到冬天便是一整幅冰，铺到目力的界桩之外，把它霜结的心窝里收进天空和空气的每一种颜色，全城的灯火，被困驳船的灯，月亮，太阳，星星，紫色的落日，还有从盘旋雪云的暗影里闪出的那些古怪、神秘的光。我的房间也许荒凉，光地板带着窟窿，一面裂纹的旧镜子，一只炉子，一只书架，一张照片和一幅拉菲尔小湾的素描。朋友们看了打寒噤；我却望着窗外这座城市水路的入海口，觉得任何魔法都可能从那奇怪的荒凉和寂静里出来。一道影子，像大鸟掠过的翅，会悬在冰上；哪条船上一口钟响，城里的教堂钟便应它；影子过去，月亮升起来，深金色，又硬又利地贴在浓重的、低垂的空气上；影子过去，星星出来，几乎带着听得见的脆响，挣破天的布面……而五分钟外，铺子的灯正亮，马车夫喊着开道，电车铃铛响，孩子们喊着号外。我的四周，是奇妙的寂静。……
1916年初秋，我在一次晚宴上认识了尼古拉·列昂季那维奇·马尔科维奇。谈话间我告诉他，我随第九集团军在加利西亚待过一年，他于是问我有没有见过他妻子的舅舅阿列克谢·彼得罗维奇·谢苗诺夫，那人也在第九集团军。恰好我同阿列克谢·彼得罗维奇熟得很，他的名字一响，把我们两人都连着的人和事便栩栩地涌回来，我竟一时按捺不住突来的激动。马尔科维奇邀我去他家。他说他同妻子住在英国大街的一套公寓里；他妻子的妹妹、还有他妻子的另一位舅舅，阿列克谢·彼得罗维奇的弟弟，也同住。我说我很乐意去。
随后那些日子里，我怎样同这份邀请的吸引力搏斗，深到什么地步，没法形容。

===Original===

I had succeeded during all these months in avoiding any contact with the incidents or characters of the preceding year. I had written no letters and had received none; I had resolutely avoided meeting any members of my old Atriad when they came to the town.
But now I succumbed. Perhaps something of my old vitality and curiosity was already creeping back into my bones, perhaps time was already dimming my memories﻿—at any rate, on an evening early in October I paid my call. Alexei Petrovitch was not present; he was on the Galician front, in Tarnople. I found Markovitch, his wife Vera Michailovna, his sister-in-law Nina Michailovna, his wife’s uncle Ivan Petrovitch and a young man Boris Nicolaievitch Grogoff. Markovitch himself was a thin, loose, untidy man with pale yellow hair thinning on top, a ragged, pale beard, a nose with a tendency to redden at any sudden insult or unkind word and an expression perpetually anxious.
Vera Michailovna on the other hand was a fine young woman, and it must have been the first thought of all who met them as to why she had married him. She gave an impression of great strength; her figure tall and her bosom full, her dark eyes large and clear. She had black hair, a vast quantity of it, piled upon her head. Her face was finely moulded, her lips strong, red, sharply marked. She looked like a woman who had already made up her mind upon all things in life and could face them all. Her expression was often stern and almost insolently scornful, but also she could be tender, and her heart would shine from her eyes. She moved slowly and gracefully, and quite without self-consciousness.
A strange contrast was her sister, Nina Michailovna, a girl still, it seemed, in childhood, pretty, with brown hair, laughing eyes, and a trembling mouth that seemed ever on the edge of laughter. Her body was soft and plump; she had lovely hands, of which she was obviously very proud. Vera dressed sternly, often in black, with a soft white collar, almost like a nurse or nun. Nina was always in gay colours; she wore clothes, as it seemed to me, in very bad taste, colours clashing, strange bows and ribbons and lace that had nothing to do with the dress to which they were attached. She was always eating sweets, laughed a great deal, had a shrill piercing voice, and was never still. Ivan Petrovitch, the uncle, was very different from my Semyonov.

===Chinese===

这几个月里，我成功地避开了上一年的一切人事。信我没写，也没收到；老伙伴们进城来，我一概躲开不见。
可这一回我屈服了。兴许我那点旧日的活力和好奇已经悄悄回到骨头里，兴许时间已经在把我的回忆磨淡——总之，十月初的一个傍晚，我登门造访了。阿列克谢·彼得罗维奇不在；他在加利西亚前线，在塔尔诺波尔。我见到马尔科维奇、他的妻子薇拉·米哈伊洛夫娜、他妻子的妹妹妮娜·米哈伊洛夫娜、他妻子的舅舅伊万·彼得罗维奇，还有一位年轻人，鲍里斯·尼古拉那维奇·格罗戈夫。马尔科维奇本人瘦弱、邋遢，淡黄的头发顶上见稀，一部参差的浅色胡子，一个受一点突然的冒犯或恶话就要红起来的鼻子，一脸常驻的焦急。
薇拉·米哈伊洛夫娜正相反，是位出众的少妇，见了他们俩的人，头一个念头想必都是：她怎么会嫁给他。她给人一种极有力量的印象；身量高，胸脯丰满，深色的眼睛又大又亮。她一头黑发，多得很，堆在头顶。脸型端正，嘴唇厚实、红润、轮廓分明。她看上去像一个对生活里的一切都已拿定主意、而且样样对付得了的女人。她的神情时常严厉，几乎近于放肆的轻蔑，可她也能温柔，那时她的心就从眼睛里照出来。她行动缓慢而优美，全无自我意识。
同她形成古怪对照的是她妹妹妮娜·米哈伊洛夫娜，一个仿佛还停在童年里的姑娘，俊俏，棕色头发，爱笑的眼睛，一张永远像要笑出声的、哆嗦着的嘴。她身子软而丰腴；一双手很好看，她显然也极为之得意。薇拉穿得素严，常穿黑，配软白领，几乎像护士或修女。妮娜永远一身艳色；她的衣裳照我看品位极坏，颜色打架，古怪的蝴蝶结、缎带和花边，同它们所附的裙子毫不相干。她总在吃糖，笑个不停，嗓音又尖又亮，一刻不得安生。舅舅伊万·彼得罗维奇同我那位谢苗诺夫则是另一路。

===Original===

He was short, fat, and dressed with great neatness and taste. He had a short black moustache, a head nearly bald, and a round chubby face with small smiling eyes. He was a *chinovnik*, and held his position in some Government office with great pride and solemnity. It was his chief aim, I found, to be considered cosmopolitan, and when he discovered the feeble quality of my French he insisted in speaking always to me in his strange confused English, a language quite of his own, with sudden startling phrases which he had “snatched” as he expressed it from Shakespeare and the Bible. He was the kindest soul alive, and all he asked was that he should be left alone and that no one should quarrel with him. He confided to me that he hated quarrels, and that it was an eternal sorrow to him that the Russian people should enjoy so greatly that pastime. I discovered that he was terrified of his brother, Alexei, and at that I was not surprised. His weakness was that he was inpenetrably stupid, and it was quite impossible to make him understand anything that was not immediately in line with his own experiences﻿—unusual obtuseness in a Russian. He was vain about his clothes, especially about his shoes, which he had always made in London; he was sentimental and very easily hurt.
Very different again was the young man Boris Nicolaievitch Grogoff. No relation of the family, he seemed to spend most of his time in the Markovitch flat. A handsome young man, strongly built, with a head of untidy curly yellow hair, blue eyes, high cheek bones, long hands with which he was forever gesticulating. Grogoff was an internationalist Socialist and expressed his opinions at the top of his voice whenever he could find an occasion. He would sit for hours staring moodily at the floor, or glaring fiercely upon the company. Then suddenly he would burst out, walking about, flinging up his arms, shouting. I saw at once that Markovitch did not like him and that he despised Markovitch. He did not seem to me a very wise young man, but I liked his energy, his kindness, sudden generosities, and honesty. I could not see his reason for being so much in this company.
During the autumn of 1916 I spent more and more time with the Markovitches. I cannot tell you what was exactly the reason. Vera Michailovna perhaps, although let no one imagine that I fell in love with her or ever thought of doing so.

===Chinese===

他矮胖，衣裳穿得极其整洁考究。黑黑的小胡子，脑袋几乎秃顶，一张圆滚滚的胖脸，笑眯眯的小眼睛。他是公务员，以极大的骄傲和庄严守着他的政府差事。我发现他的头号志愿是让人当他见多识广的世界公民，等他发现我那法语不顶用，他就坚持永远用他那套稀奇古怪、混杂不清的英语同我说话——那完全是他自造的一门语言，时不时蹦出几句他——用他的话说——从莎士比亚和《圣经》里“捞”来的惊人之语。他是天底下最好心的灵魂，全部所求无非别搅扰他、别同他吵。他向我吐露，他恨吵架，俄国人民那么爱这门消遣，是他永恒的悲哀。我发现他怕他哥哥阿列克谢，这我不奇怪。他的短处是愚钝得刀枪不入，凡是不能同他自身经验直接挂钩的事，就休想叫他明白——这在俄国人里是少见的心钝。他讲究衣裳，尤其讲究鞋，他的鞋永远在伦敦定做；他伤感，极易受伤。
再一个截然不同的是年轻人鲍里斯·尼古拉那维奇·格罗戈夫。他不是这家人的亲戚，工夫倒像大半耗在马尔科维奇家。俊朗的青年，身板结实，一头乱蓬蓬的黄卷发，蓝眼睛，高颧骨，一双长手永远在比划。格罗戈夫是国际派社会党人，一逮着机会就放开最大嗓门发表主张。他会几个钟头阴沉沉瞪着地板，或恶狠狠扫视满座。随后忽然爆发，来回走，抡胳膊，喊。我立刻看出马尔科维奇不喜欢他，他也看不起马尔科维奇。我看他不算个多聪明的青年，可我喜欢他的那股劲、他的和气、突如其来的慷慨和诚实。我看不出他凭什么老泡在这一伙里。
1916年整个秋天，我在马尔科维奇家消磨的时光越来越多。我说不出确切的缘故。兴许是薇拉·米哈伊洛夫娜吧——尽管谁也别以为我爱上了她，或起过那样的念头。

===Original===

No, my time for that was over. But I felt from the first that she was a fine, understanding creature, that she sympathised with me without pitying me, that she would be a good and loyal friend, and that I, on my side could give her comprehension and fidelity. They made me feel at home with them; there had been as yet no house in Petrograd whither I could go easily and without ceremony, which I could leave at any moment that I wished. Soon they did not notice whether I were there or no; they continued their ordinary lives and Nina, to whom I was old, plain, and feeble, treated me with a friendly indifference that did not hurt as it might have done in England. Boris Grogoff patronised and laughed at me, but would give me anything in the way of help, property, or opinions, did I need it. I was in fact by Christmas time a member of the family. They nicknamed me “Durdles,” after many jokes about my surname and reminiscences of *Edwin Drood* (my Russian name was Ivan Andreievitch). We had merry times in spite of the troubles and distresses now crowding upon Russia.
And now I come to the first of the links in my story. It was with this family that Henry Bohun was to lodge.

===Chinese===

不，我的那个年代过去了。可我头一回就觉出，她是个出色的、善解人的生灵，她同情我而不可怜我，她会是一位忠实的挚友，而我这一边，也能给她理解和忠诚。他们叫我在他们跟前不拘形迹；那时彼得格勒还没有第二户人家，我能这样随来随去、要走就走。很快他们就不留意我在不在；他们照过他们的日子，妮娜——在她眼里我又老、又丑、又弱——拿一种友好的淡然待我，那淡然若在英格兰是会伤人的，在这儿却不。鲍里斯·格罗戈夫抬举我、笑话我，可我需要时，人力、财物、主意，他什么都肯给。事实上，到圣诞前后，我已是这一家的人了。他们给我起了绰号“杜杜”，先拿我的姓氏打了一堆趣，又想起《埃德温·德鲁德之谜》（我的俄国名字是伊万·安德烈耶维奇）。尽管烦恼和苦难这时正朝俄国蜂拥而来，我们过的是快活日子。
现在，我来到我故事的第一个环扣。亨利·博亨要寄宿的，正是这一家。

### VII / 七
===Original===

Some three years before, when Ivan Petrovitch had gone to live with the Markovitches, it had occurred to them that they had two empty rooms and that these would accommodate one or two paying guests. It seemed to them still more attractive that these guests should be English, and I expect that it was Ivan Petrovitch who emphasised this. The British Consulate was asked to assist them, and after a few inconspicuous clerks and young business men they entertained for a whole six months the Hon. Charles Trafford, one of the junior secretaries at the Embassy. At the end of those six months the Hon. Charles, burdened with debt, and weakened by little sleep and much liquor, was removed to a less exciting atmosphere. With all his faults, he left faithful friends in the Markovitch flat, and he, on his side, gave so enthusiastic an account of Mme. Markovitch’s attempts to restrain and modify his impetuosities that the Embassy recommended her care and guidance to other young secretaries. The war came and Vera Michailovna declared that she could have lodgers no longer, and a terrible blow this was to Ivan Petrovitch. Then suddenly, towards the end of 1916, she changed her mind and announced to the Embassy that she was ready for anyone whom they could send her. Henry Bohun was offered, accepted, and prepared for. Ivan Petrovitch was a happy man once more.
I never discovered that Markovitch was much consulted in these affairs. Vera Michailovna “ran” the flat financially, industrially, and spiritually. Markovitch meanwhile was busy with his inventions. I have, as yet, said nothing about Nicolai Leontievitch’s inventions. I hesitate, indeed, to speak of them, although they are so essential, and indeed important a part of my story. I hesitate simply because I do not wish this narrative to be at all fantastic, but that it should stick quite honestly and obviously to the truth. It is certain moreover that what is naked truth to one man seems the falsest fancy to another, and after all I have, from beginning to end, only my own conscience to satisfy.

===Chinese===

大约三年前，伊万·彼得罗维奇搬来同马尔科维奇家同住时，他们想起家里有两间空屋，可以安置一两个搭伙的房客。更叫他们觉得美的是，房客最好是英国人，我想强调这一点的正是伊万·彼得罗维奇。他们请英国领事馆帮忙，先后来过几个不起眼的文书和年轻生意人之后，他们款待了整整半年查尔斯·特拉福德 Honourable ——使馆的一位三等秘书。那六个月过完， Honourable 的查尔斯债务缠身，又被少睡多酒淘虚了身子，被调去了一个不那么刺激的岗位。毛病归毛病，他在马尔科维奇家却留下了忠实的朋友；他这一边呢，又把马尔科维奇太太怎样约束和修剪他的莽撞形容得那样起劲，于是使馆把她管教年轻人的这份照护，推荐给了别的年轻秘书。战争来了，薇拉·米哈伊洛夫娜宣布不能再收房客，这对伊万·彼得罗维奇是一记闷棍。随后忽然，1916年将尽时，她改了主意，通知使馆，他们送谁来她都接待。亨利·博亨就这样被送来、被收下、被盼着。伊万·彼得罗维奇又做了快活人。
这些事上马尔科维奇被商量过多少，我从没有发现。薇拉·米哈伊洛夫娜在财权、事务和精神上“总管”这套公寓。马尔科维奇呢，忙着他的发明。关于尼古拉·列昂季那维奇的发明，我至今一字未提。说实话我不大愿意讲它们，尽管它们是我故事里那么要紧、那么重要的一环。我不愿意，只因为我不希望这篇叙事有一点奇幻色彩，而希望它老老实实、明明白白地贴着真相。况且，对一个人是赤裸真相的东西，对另一个人往往像最假的胡编，说到底，我从头到尾要对得过去的，只有我自己的良心。

===Original===

The history of the human soul and its relation to divinity, which is, I think, the only history worth any man’s pursuit must push its way, again and again, through this same tangled territory which infests the region lying between truth and fantasy; one passes suddenly into a world that seems pure falsehood, so askew, so obscure, so twisted and coloured is it. One is through, one looks back and it lies behind one as the clearest truth. Such an experience makes one tender to other men’s fancies and less impatient of the vague and half-defined travellers’ tales that other men tell. Childe Roland is not the only traveller who has challenged the Dark Tower.
In the Middle Ages Nicolai Leontievitch Markovitch would have been called, I suppose, a Magician﻿—a very halfhearted and unsatisfactory one he would always have been﻿—and he would have been most certainly burnt at the stake before he had accomplished any magic worthy of the name. His inventions, so far as I saw anything of them, were innocent and simple enough. It was the man himself rather than his inventions that arrested the attention. About the time of Bohun’s arrival upon the scene it was a new kind of ink that he had discovered, and for many weeks the Markovitch flat dripped ink from every pore. He had no laboratory, no scientific materials, nor, I think, any profound knowledge. The room where he worked was a small boxlike place off the living-room, a cheerless enough abode with a little high barred window in it as in a prison-cell, cardboard-boxes piled high with feminine garments, a sewing-machine, old dusty books, and a broken-down perambulator occupying most of the space. I never could understand why the perambulator was there, as the Markovitches had no children. Nicolai Leontievitch sat at a table under the little window, and his favourite position was to sit with the chair perched on one leg and so, rocking in this insecure position, he brooded over his bottles and glasses and trays. This room was so dark even in the middle of the day that he was often compelled to use a lamp. There he hovered, with his ragged beard, his ink-stained fingers and his red-rimmed eyes, making strange noises to himself and envolving from his materials continual little explosions that caused him infinite satisfaction.

===Chinese===

人类灵魂同神性的关系史——我想，这是唯一值得一个人追的历史——必须一次又一次地从这片乱地里杀出路来，这片乱地盘踞在真实与幻想之间的地带；人一脚踏进一个仿佛纯粹虚假的世界，那么斜，那么暗，那么扭曲、染色。走出来回头一望，它却躺在身后，清楚得是再明白不过的真相。这样的经历叫人宽容旁人的奇想，也叫人少一点不耐，听那些含糊其辞、半清不楚的旅人故事。向黑塔挑战过的，不止罗兰公子一个。
在中世纪，尼古拉·列昂季那维奇·马尔科维奇我想会被叫作术士——而且永远是个三心二意、不像样的术士——不等他炼出一桩够格的魔法，就准被绑上火刑柱烧了。他的发明，就我见过的而言，无辜而简单得很。真正留住人注意的，是这个人本身，不是他的发明。博亨登场前后那段日子，他新发现的是一种墨水，于是好几个星期，马尔科维奇家的公寓每个毛孔都在滴墨。他没有实验室，没有科学器材，我想也没有什么深奥的学问。他干活的那间屋是起居室隔出的一间小方匣，够阴凄的，开着一扇装铁栏的小高窗，像牢房里那样，纸板箱堆满女人的衣裳，一架缝纫机，积灰的旧书，还有一辆破童车占去大半地方。我一直不懂那辆童车为什么在那儿，马尔科维奇家又没有孩子。尼古拉·列昂季那维奇坐在小窗底下的桌前，他最爱的姿势是椅子跷在一条腿上，就这么悬悬地摇着，对着他的瓶瓶罐罐和托盘出神。那间屋即使正午也是暗的，他时常不得不点灯。他就在那儿徘徊，乱胡子，墨水染污的手指，红圈套红圈的眼睛，自己对自己发出怪声，从他的材料里不新变出小爆炸，叫他无限受用。

===Original===

He did not mind interruptions, nor did he ever complain of the noise in the other room, terrific though it often was. He would be absorbed, in a trance, lost in another world, and surely amiable and harmless enough. And yet not entirely amiable. His eyes would close to little spots of dull, lifeless colour﻿—the only thing alive about him seemed to be his hands that moved and stirred as though they did not belong to his body at all, but had an independent existence of their own﻿—and his heels protruding from under his chair were like horrid little animals waiting, malevolently, on guard.
His inventions were, of course, never successful, and he contributed, therefore, nothing to the maintenance of his household. Vera Michailovna had means of her own, and there were also the paying guests. But he suffered from no sense of distress at his impecuniosity. I discovered very quickly that Vera Michailovna kept the family purse, and one of the earliest sources of family trouble was, I fancy, his constant demands for money. Before the war he had, I believe, been drunk whenever it was possible. Because drink was difficult to obtain, and in a flood of patriotism roused by the enthusiasm of the early days of the war, he declared himself a teetotaller, and marvellously he kept his vows. This abstinence was now one of his greatest prides, and he liked to tell you about it. Nevertheless he needed money as badly as ever, and he borrowed whenever he could. One of the first things that Vera Michailovna told me was that I was on no account to open my purse to him. I was not always able to keep my promise.
On this particular evening of Bohun’s arrival I came, by invitation, to supper. They had told me about their Englishman, and had asked me indeed to help the first awkward half-hour over the stile. It may seem strange that the British Embassy should have chosen so uncouth a host as Nicolai Leontievitch for their innocent secretaries, but it was only the more enterprising of the young men who preferred to live in a Russian family; most of them inhabited elegant flats of their own, ornamented with coloured stuffs and gaily decorated cups and bright trays from the Jews’ Market, together with English comforts and luxuries dragged all the way from London.

===Chinese===

他不怕人打搅，也从没抱怨过隔壁屋里的吵嚷，尽管那吵嚷时常可怕。他会出神，入定，整个人丢在另一个世界里，够和气、够无害的了。然而又不全然和气。他的眼睛会闭成两点暗淡无生气的色斑——他身上唯一活着的仿佛只剩那双手，动呀搅呀，仿佛根本不属于他的身体，自有它们独立的生命——椅子底下伸出的两只脚后跟，则像两只丑恶的小畜生，恶狠狠地候着，守着。
他的发明自然从来没有成过，因此对家计毫无贡献。薇拉·米哈伊洛夫娜有自己的进项，再有就是房客的房钱。可他对自己不名一文毫不痛心。我很快就发现，钱袋握在薇拉·米哈伊洛夫娜手里，而家里最早的一个苦源，我想，就是他没完没了地要钱。战前，我相信他是逢喝得着必醉。后来酒难弄了，加上开战头几天的热潮激出的一股爱国心，他宣布戒了酒，而且奇迹般守住了誓。这节制如今是他最大的骄傲之一，他爱拿它对你讲。尽管如此，他要钱要得同从前一样急，逮着机会就借。薇拉·米哈伊洛夫娜最早囑咐我的一件事，就是无论如何不能为他开钱袋。我的诺言不是回回守得住。
博亨来的这个特定的晚上，我是应邀来吃晚饭的。他们同我讲过他们那位英国人，还确实请我帮他跨过头半个钟头那道难堪的坎。英国使馆竟给天真的秘书们挑了尼古拉·列昂季那维奇这样一位粗手粗脚的主人，似乎奇怪，可也只有那些更有闯劲的年轻人愿意住进俄国人家里；多数人住自己优雅的公寓，挂着从犹太人市场海来的花布、彩绘杯和亮漆托盘，再加上从伦敦一路拖来的英式舒服和奢侈品。

===Original===

Moreover, Markovitch figured very slightly in the consciousness of his guests, and the rest of the flat was roomy and clean and light. It was, like most of the homes of the Russian Intelligentzia, overburdened with family history. Amazing the things that Russians will gather together and keep, one must suppose, only because they are too lethargic to do away with them. On the walls of the Markovitch dining-room all kinds of pictures were hung﻿—old family photographs yellow and dusty, old calendars, prints of ships at sea, and young men hanging over stiles, and old ladies having tea, photographs of the Kremlin and the Lavra at Kiev, copies of Ivan and his murdered son and Serov’s portrait of Chaliapine as Boris Godounov. Bookcases there were with tattered editions of Pushkin and Lermontov. The middle of the living-room was occupied with an enormous table covered by a dark red cloth, and this table was the centre of the life of the family. A large clock wheezed and groaned against the wall, and various chairs of different shapes and sizes filled up most of the remaining space. Nevertheless, although everything in the room looked old except the white and gleaming stove, Vera Michailovna spread over the place the impress of her strong and active personality. It was not a sluggish room, nor was it untidy as so many Russian rooms are. Around the table everybody sat. It seemed that at all hours of the day and night some kind of meal was in progress there; and it was almost certain that from half-past two in the afternoon until half-past two on the following morning the samovar would be found there, presiding with sleepy dignity over the whole family and caring nothing for anybody. I can smell now that especial smell of tea and radishes and salted fish, and can hear the wheeze of the clock, the hum of the samovar, Nina’s shrill laugh and Boris’s deep voice.﻿ ﻿… I owe that room a great deal. It was there that I was taken out of myself and memories that fared no better for their perpetual resurrection. That room called me back to life.
On this evening there was to be, in honour of young Bohun, an especially fine dinner. A message had come from him that he would appear with his boxes at half-past seven.

===Chinese===

何况，在房客们的意识里，马尔科维奇这个人存在感极低，公寓其余部分却宽敞、干净、亮堂。它同大多数俄国知识分子的家一样，被家族史压得超重。俄国人攒下并留着的那些东西真叫人惊奇——想必只因为他们懒得扔。马尔科维奇家饭厅的墙上挂满了各式画——发黄蒙尘的家族老照片，旧年历，海上帆船和趴在栅栏上的青年、喝茶的老太太的版画，克里姆林宫和基辅洞窟修道院的照片，伊凡雷帝杀子的复制品，还有谢罗夫画的夏里亚宾饰鲍里斯·戈都诺夫的像。书架上是一堆破烂的普希金和莱蒙托夫。起居室正中是一张蒙深红布的大桌子，这张桌子是全家生活的中心。墙上一座大钟喘气叹气，各种形状大小的椅子填满了其余的空当。然而，尽管屋里除了白亮亮的炉子外样样显旧，薇拉·米哈伊洛夫娜却把她的干练泼辣印在了整个地方。这不是一间死气沉沉的屋子，也不像那么多俄国屋子那样凌乱。全家围着桌子坐。仿佛一天到晚什么时候那里都在开饭；而且几乎可以担保，从下午两点半到次日凌晨两点半，茶炊都蹲在那儿，顶着睡意威严地主宰全家，不理会任何人。我如今还闻得到那股茶、小萝卜和咸鱼的特有气味，还听得见钟的喘喘、茶炊的嗡嗡、妮娜的尖笑和鲍里斯的低嗓。……我欠那间屋子很多。正是在那里，我被从自己心里、从那些越追忆越无益的追忆里拽了出来。那间屋子把我唤回了生活。
这天晚上，为着小博亨，要开一顿格外丰盛的晚饭。他捎来话说，七点半带着行李箱到。

===Original===

When I arrived Vera was busy in the kitchen, and Nina adding in her bedroom extra ribbons and laces to her costume; Boris Nicolaievitch was not present; Nicolai Leontievitch was working in his den.
I went through to him. He did not look up as I came in. The room was darker than usual; the green shade over the lamp was tilted wickedly as though it were cocking its eye at Markovitch’s vain hopes, and there was the man himself, one cheek a ghastly green, his hair on end and his chair precariously balanced.
I heard him say as though he repeated an incantation﻿—“*Nu Vot﻿ ﻿… Nu Vot﻿ ﻿… Nu Vot.*”
“*Zdras te*, Nicolai Leontievitch,” I said. Then I did not disturb him but sat down on a rickety chair and waited. Ink dripped from his table on to the floor. One bottle lay on its side, the ink oozing out, other bottles stood, some filled, some half-filled, some empty.
“Ah, ha!” he cried, and there was a little explosion; a cork spurted out and struck the ceiling; there was smoke and the crackling of glass. He turned round and faced me, a smudge of ink on one of his cheeks, and that customary nervous unhappy smile on his lips.
“Well, how goes it?” I asked.
“Well enough.” He touched his cheek then sucked his fingers. “I must wash. We have a guest tonight. And the news, what’s the latest?”
He always asked me this question, having apparently the firm conviction that an Englishman must know more about the war than a man of any other nationality. But he didn’t pause for an answer﻿—“News﻿—but of course there is none. What can you expect from this Russia of ours?﻿—and the rest﻿—it’s all too far away for any of us to know anything about it﻿—only Germany’s close at hand. Yes. Remember that. You forget it sometimes in England. She’s very near indeed.﻿ ﻿… We’ve got a guest coming﻿—from the English Embassy. His name’s Boon and a funny name too. You don’t know him, do you?”
No, I didn’t know him. I laughed. Why should he think that I always knew everybody, I who kept to myself so?
“The English always stick together. That’s more than can be said for us Russians. We’re a rotten lot. Well, I must go and wash.”
Then, whether by a sudden chance of light and shade, or if you like to have it, by a sudden revelation on the part of a beneficent Providence, he really did look malevolent, standing in the middle of the dirty little room, malevolent and pathetic too, like a cross, sick bird.

===Chinese===

我到的时候，薇拉在厨房里忙，妮娜在自己屋里往行头上一层层加缎带和花边；鲍里斯·尼古拉那维奇不在；尼古拉·列昂季那维奇在他的小窝里干活。
我穿过去看他。我进去他没抬头。屋里比平常更暗，灯上的绿罩子歪得促狭，仿佛正睨着马尔科维奇的那些徒劳的希望，而那个人本人就在那儿，一边脸颊一片惨绿，头发根根直竖，椅子悬在半条腿上。
我听见他像念咒似的反复念——“这个……这个……这个。”
“您好，尼古拉·列昂季那维奇，”我说。我没搅他，在一把摇晃的破椅子上坐下等着。墨水顺着他的桌子往地板上滴。一只瓶子倒了，墨水渗出来，其余的瓶子立着，有的满，有的半满，有的空。
“啊，哈！”他喊，接着一声小小的爆炸；一只软木塞喷射出去，打在天花板上；一阵烟，玻璃的噼啪。他转过身来对着我，一边腮帮上一道墨渍，嘴上挂着惯常的、紧张的、不幸的微笑。
“呃，怎么样？”我问。
“还凑合。”他摸了摸脸，又嘆嘆手指。“我得洗洗。今晚咱们有客。还有新闻，最新的？”
他老拿这个问题问我，显然坚信英国人打仗的事必定比任何别国的人知道得多。可他不等回答——“新闻——当然没有新闻。咱们这个俄国还能指望什么？——其余的呢——离得太远，谁也闹不清——只有德国近在眼前。是。记住这个。你们在英国有时候忘了。它真的非常近。……咱们有客要来——英国使馆的。他姓布恩，也是个滑稽姓。您不认识他吧？”
不，我不认识。我笑了。他凭什么以为我谁都认得，我这样不与人来往的人？
“英国人总是抱团。这一点可比咱们俄国人强。咱们是一伙烂货。好，我去洗了。”
随后，不知是光影的一下偶然，还是——随你怎么说——仁慈上苍的一下点拨，他站在那间肮脏小屋的当间，那样子真显得恶，恶而且可怜，像一只害了病的、落了难的鸟。

===Original===

“Vera’s got a good dinner ready. That’s one thing, Ivan Andreievitch,” he said; “and vodka﻿—a little bottle. We got it from a friend. But I don’t drink now, you know.”
He went off and I, going into the other room, found Vera Michailovna giving last touches to the table. I sat and watched with pleasure her calm assured movements. She really was splendid, I thought, with the fine carriage of her head, her large mild eyes, her firm strong hands.
“All ready for the guest, Vera Michailovna?” I asked.
“Yes,” she answered, smiling at me, “I hope so. He won’t be very particular, will he, because we aren’t princes?”
“I can’t answer for him,” I replied, smiling back at her. “But he can’t be more particular than the Hon. Charles﻿—and he was a great success.”
The Hon. Charles was a standing legend in the family, and we always laughed when we mentioned him.
“I don’t know”﻿—she stopped her work at the table and stood, her hand up to her brow as though she would shade her eyes from the light﻿—“I wish he wasn’t coming﻿—the new Englishman, I mean. Better perhaps as we were﻿—Nicholas﻿—” she stopped short. “Oh, I don’t know! They’re difficult times, Ivan Andreievitch.”
The door opened and old Uncle Ivan came in. He was dressed very smartly with a clean white shirt and a black bow tie and black patent leather shoes, and his round face shone as the sun.
“Ah, Mr. Durward,” he said, trotting forward. “Good health to you! What excellent weather we’re sharing.”
“So we are, M. Semyonov,” I answered him. “Although it did rain most of yesterday you know. But weather of the soul perhaps you mean? In that case I’m very glad to hear that you are well.”
“Ah﻿—of the soul?” He always spoke his words very carefully, clipping and completing them, and then standing back to look at them as though they were china ornaments arranged on a shining table. “No﻿—my soul today is not of the first rank, I’m afraid.”
It was obvious that he was in a state of the very greatest excitement; he could not keep still, but walked up and down beside the long table, fingering the knives and forks.
Then Nina burst in upon us in one of her frantic rages. Her tempers were famous both for their ferocity and the swiftness of their passing. In the course of them she was like some impassioned bird of brilliant plumages, tossing her feathers, fluttering behind the bars of her cage at some impertinent, teasing passerby.

===Chinese===

“薇拉备好了一顿好饭。这是一件，伊万·安德烈耶维奇，”他说，“还有伏特加——一小瓶。从一个朋友那儿弄的。可我如今不喝了，您知道。”
他走了，我进了另一间屋，见薇拉·米哈伊洛夫娜正给桌子收尾。我坐着，快意地看她那从容笃定的动作。她真好，我想，头颈那么端庄，眼睛那么大而温厚，双手那么结实有力。
“都为客人备好了，薇拉·米哈伊洛夫娜？”我问。
“好了，”她含笑答我，“但愿好了。他不会很挑剔吧，我们又不是王公？”
“这个我可担保不了，”我笑着回她，“可他再挑剔也挑剔不过 Honourable 的查尔斯——而他是大获全胜的。”
 Honourable 的查尔斯是这个家里一部活的传奇，提起来我们总要笑。
“我不知道”——她停下桌上的活计，站着，一只手抬到眉边，像要挡住灯光——“我倒盼着他别来——我是说那位新英国人。兴许还像从前那样好——就尼古拉斯——”她猛地收住。“哦，我不知道！这日子难，伊万·安德烈耶维奇。”
门开了，伊万老舅舅进来。他穿得很精神，干净的白衬衫，黑领结，黑漆皮鞋，圆脸亮得像太阳。
“啊，杜沃德先生，”他小步快走过来，“恭祝康健！咱们共享的天气多好。”
“可不是嘛，谢苗诺夫先生，”我答他，“虽然您知道，昨天大半都在下雨。您指的多半是灵魂的天气？那我就很高兴听到您安好。”
“啊——灵魂的？”他说话永远字斟句酌，把每个词裁得圆圆的、完完整整，随后退后一步端详它们，仿佛它们是摆亮桌上的一件件瓷器。“不——我的灵魂今天恐怕不算上等。”
看得出他正处在最大的亢奋里；他安坐不下来，沿着长桌走来走去，手指拨弄着刀叉。
随后妮娜带着她那狂怒中的一阵冲了进来。她的脾气出名，一是凶，二是来得快去得快。发作起来，她像一只羽毛华丽的热带鸟，抖着毛，隔着笼栏朝哪个冒失撩逗的路人扑腾。

===Original===

She stood there now in the doorway, gesticulating with her hands.
“*Nu, Tznaiesh schto?* Michael Alexandrovitch has put me off﻿—says he is busy all night at the office. He busy all night! Don’t I know the business he’s after? And it’s the third time﻿—I won’t see him again﻿—no, I won’t. He﻿—”
“Good evening, Nina Michailovna,” I said, smiling. She turned to me.
“Durdles﻿—Mr. Durdles﻿—only listen. It was all arranged for tonight﻿—the *Parisian*, and then we were to come straight back﻿—”
“But your guest﻿—” I began.
However the torrent continued. The door opened and Boris Grogoff came in. Instantly she turned upon him.
“There’s your fine friend!” she cried; “Michael Alexandrovitch isn’t coming. Put me off at the last moment, and it’s the third time. And I might have gone to Musikalnaya Drama. I was asked by﻿—”
“Well, why not?” Grogoff interrupted calmly. “If he had something better to do﻿—”
Then she turned upon him, screaming, and in a moment they were at it, tooth and nail, heaping up old scores, producing fact after fact to prove, the one to the other, false friendship, lying manners, deceitful promises, perjured records. Vera tried to interrupt, Markovitch said something, I began a remonstrance﻿—in a moment we were all at it, and the room was a whirl of noise. In the tempest it was only I who heard the door open. I turned and saw Henry Bohun standing there.
I smile now when I think of that moment of his arrival, go fitting to the characters of the place, so appropriate a symbol of what was to come. Bohun was beautifully dressed, spotlessly neat, in a bowler hat a little to one side, a light-blue silk scarf, a dark-blue overcoat. His face wore an expression of dignified self-appreciation. It was as though he stood there breathing blessings on the house that he had sanctified by his arrival. He looked, too, with it all, such a boy that my heart was touched. And there was something good and honest about his eyes.
He may have spoken, but certainly no one heard him in the confusion.
I just caught Nina’s shrill voice: “Listen all of you! There you are! You hear what he says! That I told him it was to be Tuesday when, everybody knows﻿—Verotchka! Ah﻿—Verotchka! He says﻿—” Then she paused; I caught her amazed glance at the door, her gasp, a scream of stifled laughter, and behold she was gone!
Then they all saw.

===Chinese===

此刻她站在门口，双手直比划。
“喏，你知道吗？米哈伊尔·亚历山德罗维奇把我甩了——说他在公事房要忙一整夜。他忙一整夜！他忙的什么勾当我不知道？这已是第三回——我再也不见他了——不，不见了。他——”
“晚上好，妮娜·米哈伊洛夫娜，”我含笑说。她转向我。
“杜杜——杜杜先生——您听。本来今晚都说好了——去巴黎人剧院，完了直接回来——”
“可你们的客人——”我开口。
洪流却继续奔涌。门开了，鲍里斯·格罗戈夫进来。她立刻调转炮口。
“瞧你那位好朋友！”她喊，“米哈伊尔·亚历山德罗维奇不来了。最后一刻甩了我，这是第三回。我还本可以去音乐话剧剧院的。人家请我的是——”
“嗐，那又怎样？”格罗戈夫不动声色地打断，“他要是有更要紧的事——”
她扑上去冲他尖叫，转眼两人就撕咬到一处，牙齿指甲齐上，翻老账，一件事实接一件事实地抛，向对方证明：假朋友、谎话连篇、骗人的许诺、发过誓的记录。薇拉想劝，马尔科维奇说了句什么，我也开口相劝——转眼我们全体都卷了进去，满屋一片声浪。风暴里只有我听见门开。我回头，看见亨利·博亨站在那儿。
此刻想起他到达的那一幕我还要笑：它同这一家人的脾性扣得那么准，同往后的事又是一个多么合适的象征。博亨衣冠楚楚，一尘不染，圆顶帽微微歪戴，浅蓝丝围巾，深蓝大衣。脸上是一副庄重的自我欣赏。那样子仿佛他立在那里，正为他一亲芳泽——他一到便使之圣洁——的这座宅邸祝祷。可这一副气派底下，他又那么像个孩子，叫我心里一动。他眼睛里还有一点又好又诚实的东西。
他也许开了口，可那一片乱里谁也没听见。
我只捕到妮娜尖利的嗓音：“你们大家听着！瞧啊！听见他说什么了！说我告诉他定的是星期二，可谁都知道——薇拉奇卡！啊——薇拉奇卡！他说——”随后她顿住；我捕到她朝门口那惊愕的一瞥、她的一声抽气、一阵憋住的笑——瞧，人没了！
随后大家都看见了。

===Original===

There was instant silence, a terrible pause, and then Bohun’s polite gentle voice: “Is this where Mr. Markovitch lives? I beg your pardon﻿—”
Great awkwardness followed. It is quite an illusion to suppose that Russians are easy, affable hosts. I know of no people in the world who are so unable to put you at your ease if there is something unfortunate in the air. They have few easy social graces, and they are inclined to abandon at once a situation if it is made difficult for them. If it needs an effort to make a guest happy they leave him alone and trust to a providence in whose powers, however, they entirely disbelieve. Bohun was led to his room, his bags being carried by old Sacha, the Markovitch’s servant, and the *dvornik*.
His bags, I remember, were very splendid, and I saw the eyes of Uncle Ivan grow large as he watched their progress. Then with a sigh he drew a chair up to the table and began eating *zakuska*, putting salt-fish and radishes and sausage on to his plate and eating them with a fork.
“*Dyadya*, Ivan!” Vera said reproachfully. “Not yet﻿—we haven’t begun. Ivan Andreievitch, what do you think? Will he want hot water?”
She hurried after him.
The evening thus unfortunately begun was not happily continued. There was a blight upon us all. I did my best, but I was in considerable pain and very tired. Moreover, I was not favourably impressed with my first sight of young Bohun. He seemed to me foolish and conceited. Uncle Ivan was afraid of him. He made only one attack.
“It was a very fruitful journey that you had, sir, I hope?”
“I beg your pardon,” said Bohun.
“A very fruitful journey﻿—nothing burdensome nor extravagant?”
“Oh, all right, thanks,” Bohun answered, trying unsuccessfully to show that he was not surprised at my friend’s choice of words. But Uncle Ivan saw that he had not been successful and his lip trembled. Markovitch was silent and Boris Nicolaievitch sulked. Only once towards the end of the meal Bohun interested me.
“I wonder,” he asked me, “whether you know a fellow called Lawrence? He travelled from England with me. A man who’s played a lot of football.”
“Not Jerry Lawrence, the international!” I said. “Surely he can’t have come out here?” Of course it was the same. I was interested and strangely pleased. The thought of Lawrence’s square back and cheery smile was extremely agreeable just then.
“Oh! I’m very glad,” I answered.

===Chinese===

霎时死静，一阵可怕的停顿，随后是博亨客气文雅的嗓音：“请问马尔科维奇先生住在这儿吗？失礼了——”
接着是一场大尴尬。以为俄国人都是随和可亲的主人，纯属错觉。依我说，世上再没有哪个民族，在气氛不对时这样不会叫你自在。他们的社交里缺现成的圆转，局面一给他们弄难了，他们就干脆撂下。要费力气才能叫客人快活，他们就由他去，交给天意——而天意的本事，他们又全然不信。博亨被领去他的房间，行李由马尔科维奇家的老萨莎和看门人抬着。
我记得他那几口箱子非常气派，我看见伊万舅舅望着它们抬过去，眼睛越睁越大。随后他叹口气，把椅子拖到桌边，吃起冷盘来，把咸鱼、小萝卜、香肠拨到盘里，用叉子吃着。
“伊万舅舅！”薇拉嗔道，“还没开始呢。伊万·安德烈耶维奇，您说呢？他要热水吗？”
她急急跟了过去。
这样倒霉开了头的晚上，续得也不顺。我们人人头上像罩着什么。我尽了力，可我疼得厉害，又乏。再说，小博亨给我的头一眼印象并不好。我觉得他又蠢又自负。伊万舅舅怕他。只进攻了一次。
“先生这趟旅途想必十分丰硕吧？”
“您说什么？”博亨问。
“十分丰硕的旅途——不累赘也不奢华？”
“哦，还好，谢谢，”博亨答，竭力想显出对我这位朋友用词毫不惊讶，却没成功。伊万舅舅看出自己没成功，嘴唇抖了。马尔科维奇沉默，鲍里斯·尼古拉那维奇生闷气。只是快到饭末，博亨才叫我发生了一点兴趣。
“不知道，”他问我，“您认不认识一个叫劳伦斯的家伙？他同我一路从英国来。一个踢过好多足球的人。”
“总不会是杰里·劳伦斯，打国际赛的！”我说，“他可不该到这儿来？”当然正是他。我发生兴趣，还莫名地高兴。想着劳伦斯那方方的背和快活的笑，此刻极其受用。
“哦！我很高兴，”我答。

===Original===

“I must get him to come and see me. I knew him pretty well at one time. Where’s he to be found?”
Bohun, with an air of rather gentle surprise, as though he could not help thinking it strange that anyone should take an interest in Lawrence’s movements, told me where he was lodging.
“And I hope you also will find your way to me sometime,” I added. “It’s an out-of-place grimy spot, I’m afraid. You might bring Lawrence round one evening.”
Soon after that, feeling that I could do no more towards retrieving an evening definitely lost, I departed. At the last I caught Markovitch’s eye. He seemed to be watching for something. A new invention perhaps. He was certainly an unhappy man.

===Chinese===

“我一定叫他来看我。我一度同他相当熟。上哪儿找他？”
博亨带着一种温和的惊讶——仿佛有人竟关心劳伦斯的行踪，他觉得不能不奇怪——把他的住址告诉了我。
“也盼您哪天找到我那儿去，”我补一句，“那地方又偏又脏，恐怕。哪天晚上带劳伦斯一起来。”
不久，眼看这注定了的晚上再无可挽回，我便告辞了。临走我撞见马尔科维奇的眼睛。他仿佛在守着什么。兴许又是一项新发明。他确实是个不幸福的人。

### VIII / VIII
===Original===

I was to meet Jerry Lawrence sooner than I had expected. And it was in this way.
Two days after the evening that I have just described I was driven to go and see Vera Michailovna. I was driven, partly by my curiosity, partly by my depression, and partly by my loneliness. This same loneliness was, I believe, at this time beginning to affect us all. I should be considered perhaps to be speaking with exaggeration if I were to borrow the title of one of Mrs. Oliphant’s old-fashioned and charming novels and to speak of Petrograd as already “A Beleaguered City”﻿—beleaguered, moreover, in very much the same sense as that other old city was. From the very beginning of the war Petrograd was isolated﻿—isolated not by the facts of the war, its geographical position or any of the obvious causes, but simply by the contempt and hatred with which it was regarded. From very old days it was spoken of as a German town. “If you want to know Russia don’t go to Petrograd.” “Simply a cosmopolitan town like any other.” “A smaller Berlin”﻿—and so on, and so on. This sense of outside contempt influenced its own attitude to the world. It was always at war with Moscow. It showed you when you first arrived its Nevski, its ordered squares, its official buildings, as though it would say: “I suppose you will take the same view as the rest. If you don’t wish to look any deeper here you are. I’m not going to help you.”
As the war developed it lost whatever gaiety and humour it had. After the fall of Warsaw the attitude of the Russian people in general became fatalistic. Much nonsense was talked in the foreign press about “Russia coming back again and again.” “Russia, the harder she was pressed the harder she resisted,” and the ghost of Napoleon retreating from Moscow was presented to every home in Europe; but the plain truth was that, after Warsaw, the temper of the people changed. Things were going wrong once more as they had always gone wrong in Russian history, and as they always would go wrong. Then followed bewilderment. What to do? Whose fault was it all? Shall we blame our blood or our rulers? Our rulers, certainly, as we always, with justice, have blamed them﻿—our blood, too, perhaps. From the fall of Warsaw, in spite of momentary flashes of splendour and courage, the Russians were a blindfolded, naked people, fighting a nation fully armed.

===Chinese===

我比预想的更早见到了杰里·劳伦斯（Jerry Lawrence）。事情是这样的。
我刚描述过的那个晚上之后两天，我忍不住要去见薇拉·米哈伊洛夫娜。驱使我去的一部分是好奇，一部分是沮丧，还有一部分是孤独。我相信，正是这种孤独感，在当时开始攫住我们每一个人。倘若我借奥利芬特夫人某部旧派而迷人的小说的书名，说彼得格勒已经是一座“被围之城”——而且被围的意味，同那座古城几乎如出一辙——人们大概要说我夸大其词了。从战争一开始，彼得格勒就是孤立的——它的孤立并非由于战事本身、地理位置或任何显而易见的原因，而仅仅由于外人对它的蔑视与憎恶。很早以前，它就被说成是一座德国城市。“想了解俄国，就别去彼得格勒。”“不过是个同别处无二的世界性都会。”“一座小一号的柏林”——如此等等，不一而足。这种遭人轻蔑的感觉，也影响了它自己对世界的态度。它永远在同莫斯科交战。你初来乍到，它把涅夫斯基大街、齐整的广场、气派的官署大厦指给你看，仿佛在说：“我想你也会随大流这么看。你若不肯往深处看，那就请便。我是不会帮你的。”
随着战争推移，它把仅有的那点欢乐和幽默也丢掉了。华沙陷落之后，俄国百姓的心态普遍变得听天由命。外国报刊上充斥着“俄国一次又一次卷土重来”之类的胡话，“俄国受压越重，反抗越强”，拿破仑兵败莫斯科的幽灵被送进了欧洲千家万户；可是明摆着的事实是：华沙之后，人心变了。事情又在出岔子，正如俄国历史上事情总在出岔子，往后也永远会出岔子。接着来的是惶惑。怎么办？这一切是谁的过错？该怪我们的血统，还是怪我们的统治者？当然是怪统治者，我们一向这样怪，而且怪得有理——或许血统也要怪上一份。自华沙陷落以后，纵然偶有壮烈与英勇的闪光，俄国人始终是一个蒙着眼、赤着身的民族，在同全副武装的敌国作战。

===Original===

Now, Europe was vast continents away, and only Germany, that old Germany whose soul was hateful, whose practical spirit was terribly admirable, was close at hand. The Russian people turned hither and thither, first to its Czar, then to its generals, then to its democratic spirit, then to its idealism﻿—and there was no hope anywhere. They appealed for Liberty. In the autumn of 1916 a great prayer from the whole country went up that the bandage might be taken from its eyes, and soon, lest when the light did at last come the eyes should be so unused to it that they should see nothing. Nicholas had his opportunity﻿—the greatest opportunity perhaps ever offered to man. He refused it. From that moment the easiest way was closed, and only a most perilous rocky path remained.
With every week of that winter of 1916, Petrograd stepped deeper and deeper into the darkness. Its strangeness grew and grew upon me as the days filed through. I wondered whether my illness and the troubles of the preceding year made me see everything at an impossible angle﻿—or it was perhaps my isolated lodging, my crumbling rooms, with the grey expanse of sea and sky in front of them that was responsible. Whatever it was, Petrograd soon came to be to me a place with a most terrible secret life of its own.
There is an old poem of Pushkin’s that Alexandre Benois has most marvellously illustrated, which has for its theme the rising of the river Neva in November 1824. On that occasion the splendid animal devoured the town, and in Pushkin’s poem you feel the devastating power of the beast, and in Benois’ pictures you can see it licking its lips as it swallowed down pillars and bridges and streets and squares with poor little fragments of humanity clutching and crying and fruitlessly appealing.
This poem only emphasised for me the suspicion that I had originally had, that the great river and the marshy swamp around it despised contemptuously the buildings that man had raised beside and upon it, and that even the buildings in their turn despised the human beings who thronged them. It could only be some sense of this kind that could make one so repeatedly conscious that one’s feet were treading ancient ground.

===Chinese===

如今，欧洲远隔重重大陆，近在眼前的只有德国——那个旧日的德国，灵魂可惜可恨，务实精神却又令人叹服得可怕。俄国人民东张西望，先求沙皇，再求将军们，又求自己的民主精神，再求自己的理想主义——处处皆无指望。他们祈求自由。1916年秋，全国上下发出一声宏大的祷告：愿蒙眼的布带早日取下，而且要快，免得光明终于降临时，那双眼睛久不惯光，反而什么也看不见了。尼古拉有过他的机会——那也许是上天给过一个人的最大的机会。他拒绝了。从那一刻起，最省力的路关闭了，只剩一条凶险万状的乱石小径。
随着1916年那个冬天一周一周过去，彼得格勒一步步陷进更深的黑暗。日子一天天流走，它的怪异在我心头越积越沉。我不知道，是我前一年的病和种种变故叫我戴上了歪斜的镜片看一切——还是该怪我那离群索居的寓所、那几间破败的屋子，和屋前灰蒙蒙的一片海天。不管是什么缘故，彼得格勒很快就在我心里成了一个自有其可怕隐秘生命的地方。
普希金有一首旧诗，写的是1824年11月涅瓦河的暴涨，亚历山大·贝努瓦为它作过精妙绝伦的插图。那一次，这头华美的巨兽吞掉了全城。在普希金的诗里，你能感到这野兽摧枯拉朽的伟力；在贝努瓦的画里，你能看见它舔着嘴唇，把廊柱、桥梁、街道、广场，连同那些攀附着、哭喊着、徒然哀求着的可怜的人类碎屑，一股脑儿吞下肚去。
这首诗只是坐实了我心底原先的疑心：那条大河和它四周泥泞的沼泽，正冷眼睥睨着人在它身旁、在它身上建起的屋宇；而那些屋宇转过来又睥睨着其中熙攘的人群。惟其有这种感觉，人才会一次又一次地意识到，脚下踩的是太古的土地。

===Original===

The town, raised all of a piece by Peter the Great, could claim no ancient history at all; but through every stick and stone that had been laid there stirred the spirit and soul of the ground, so that out of one of the sluggish canals one might expect at any moment to see the horrid and scaly head of some palaeolithic monster with dead and greedy eyes slowly push its way up that it might gaze at the little black hurrying atoms as they crossed and recrossed the grey bridge. There are many places in Petrograd where life is utterly dead; where some building, half-completed, has fallen into red and green decay; where the water lies still under iridescent scum and thick clotted reeds seem to stand at bay, concealing in their depths some terrible monster.
At such a spot I have often fancied that the eyes of countless inhabitants of that earlier world are watching me, and that not far away the waters of Neva are gathering, gathering, gathering their mighty momentum for some instant, when, with a great heave and swell, they will toss the whole fabric of brick and mortar from their shoulders, flood the streets and squares, and then sink tranquilly back into great sheets of unruffled waters marked only with reeds and the sharp cry of some travelling bird.
All this may be fantastic enough, I only know that it was sufficiently real to me during that winter of 1916 to be ever at the back of my mind; and I believe that some sense of that kind had in all sober reality something to do with that strange weight of uneasy anticipation that we all of us, yes, the most unimaginative amongst us, felt at this time.
Upon this afternoon when I went to pay my call on Vera Michailovna, the real snow began to fall. We had had the false preliminary attempt a fortnight before; now in the quiet persistent determination, the solid soft resilience beneath one’s feet, and the patient aquiescence of roofs and bridges and cobbles one knew that the real winter had come. Already, although it was only four o’clock in the afternoon, there was darkness, with the strange almost metallic glow as of the light from an inverted looking-glass that snow makes upon the air.

===Chinese===

这座城是彼得大帝一锤定音、平地建起的，本说不上什么古老的历史；可是铺在那里的每一根木、每一块石之间，都悸动着这片土地的精魄，于是你会觉得，说不定哪一刻，哪条死水运河里就会慢慢拱出一颗洪荒怪兽的脑袋，鳞甲森然，一双死沉沉的贪婪眼睛，好教它瞧一瞧那些小黑点般匆匆的人影，如何在灰桥上来来去去。彼得格勒有许多地方，生命死得透透的：某座半途而废的建筑，烂得红一片绿一片；水面在虹彩的浮沫下纹丝不动，浓稠纠结的芦苇像在负隅而立，深处藏着什么可怕的怪物。在这样的地方，我常常恍然觉得，那个太古世界无数生灵的眼睛正望着我；不远处的涅瓦河水，正在聚积，聚积，再聚积它磅礴的势头，只等某个瞬息，猛然一掀一涨，把整座砖石营构从肩头抖落，灌满街道和广场，然后静静退回成一大幅波澜不惊的水面，水上只余芦苇，和某只过路水鸟的一声尖唳。
这一切也许够荒唐的了。我只知道，在1916年那个冬天，这一切对我真实得很，无时无刻不伏在我心底；而且我相信，某种这样的预感，的的确确同我们人人——是的，连我们当中最无想象力的人——此时都感到的那阵莫名而不安的重压，有些干系。
就在我登门拜访薇拉·米哈伊洛夫娜的这个下午，真正的雪落了下来。半个月前有过一场虚晃一招的预演；而此刻，从那安静而不屈不挠的劲儿里，从脚下厚实松软的弹性里，从屋顶、桥梁、石板路那默默的顺从里，你知道，真正的冬天来了。虽然才下午四点，天已经黑了，空气里浮着雪带来的那种奇异的、近乎金属的光泽，像一面倒扣的镜子映出的光。

===Original===

I had not far to go, but the long stretch of the Ekateringofsky Canal was black and gloomy and desolate, repeating here and there the pale yellow reflection of some lamp, but for the most part dim and dead, with the hulks of barges lying like sleeping monsters on its surface. As I turned into Anglisky Prospect I found stretched like a black dado, far down the street, against the wall, a queue of waiting women. They would be there until the early morning, many of them, and it was possible that then the bread would not be sufficient. And this not from any real lack, but simply from the mistakes of a bungling, peculating Government. No wonder that one’s heart was heavy.
I found Vera Michailovna to my relief alone. When Sacha brought me into the room she was doing what I think I had never seen her do before, sitting unoccupied, her eyes staring in front of her, her hands folded on her lap.
“I don’t believe that I’ve ever caught you idle before, Vera Michailovna,” I said.
“Oh, I’m glad you’ve come!” She caught my hand with an eagerness very different from her usual calm, quiet greeting. “Sit down. It’s an extraordinary thing. At that very moment I was wishing for you.”
“What is it I can do for you?” I asked. “You know that I would do anything for you.”
“Yes, I know that you would. But﻿—well. You can’t help me because I don’t know what’s the matter with me.”
“That’s very unlike you,” I said.
“Yes, I know it is﻿—and perhaps that’s why I am frightened. It’s so vague; and you know I long ago determined that if I couldn’t define a trouble and have it there in front of me, so that I could strangle it﻿—why I wouldn’t bother about it. But those things are so easy to say.”
She got up and began to walk up and down the room. That again was utterly unlike her, and altogether I seemed to be seeing, this afternoon, some quite new Vera Michailovna, someone more intimate, more personal, more appealing. I realised suddenly that she had never before, at any period of our friendship, asked for my help﻿—not even for my sympathy. She was so strong and reliant and independent, cared so little for the opinion of others, and shut down so closely upon herself her private life, that I could not have imagined her asking help from anyone. And of the two of us, she was the man, the strong determined soul, the brave and self-reliant character.

===Chinese===

路并不远，可叶卡捷琳戈夫斯基运河那一长段又黑又暗又荒凉，河面这儿那儿映出几点昏黄的灯影，其余大半昏沉死寂，驳船的笨躯像睡熟的怪兽一样浮在水上。拐上英国大街时，我看见沿墙根朝街的深处远远排开一条黑色的横带——一队等候的女人。她们中许多人要一直站到后半夜，而到那时面包还可能不够分。这并非因为真的缺粮，只因为一个笨拙无能、贪贿成风的政府把事情办砸了。难怪人心里沉甸甸的。
让我松一口气的是，薇拉·米哈伊洛夫娜独自在家。萨莎领我进屋时，她正做着一件事，我想我从没见她做过：无所事事地坐着，两眼直勾勾望着前方，双手交叠在膝上。
“我看我从没撞见过你闲着，薇拉·米哈伊洛夫娜。”我说。
“啊，你来了我真高兴！”她一把抓住我的手，那份急切，同她平日安静从容的招呼判若两人。“坐吧。真是奇怪。就在方才那一刻，我正盼着你来呢。”
“我能为你做点什么呢？”我问。“你知道，为你我什么都肯做。”
“是的，我知道你肯。可是——唉。你帮不上我，因为我自己也不知道我这是怎么了。”
“这可太不像你了。”我说。
“是的，我知道不像——也许正因如此我才害怕。这事这么含糊；你知道，我早就拿定了主意，凡是一个说不清、道不明、摆不到眼前、没法掐死的烦恼，我就不为它费神。可这种话，说起来终究容易。”
她站起身，在屋里来回踱步。这也不像她。总而言之，这天下午我仿佛见到了一个全然陌生的薇拉·米哈伊洛夫娜，一个更亲近、更有血肉、更打动人的人。我猛然醒悟：在我们相交的这些年月里，她从没求过我帮忙——连一句同情也没讨过。她是那样坚强、自恃、独立，那样不在乎旁人的看法，把自己的私生活捂得那样严实，我根本无法想象她会向任何人开口求助。我们两个之间，她才是男人，才是那个坚强果决的灵魂，那个勇敢自立的角色。

===Original===

It seemed to me ludicrous that she should ask for my help. Nevertheless I was greatly touched.
“I would do anything for you,” I said.
She turned to me, a splendid figure, her head, with its crown of black hair, lifted, her hands on her hips, her eyes gravely regarding me.
“There are three things,” she said, “perhaps all of them nothing.﻿ ﻿… And yet all of them disturbing. First my husband. He’s beginning to drink again.”
“Drink?” I said; “where can he get it from?”
“I don’t know. I must discover. But it isn’t the actual drinking. Everyone in our country drinks if he can. Only what has made my husband break his resolve? He was so proud of it. You know how proud he was. And he lies about it. He says he is not drinking. He never used to lie about anything. That was not one of his faults.”
“Perhaps his inventions,” I suggested.
“Pouf! His inventions! You know better than that, Ivan Andreievitch. No, no. It is something.﻿ ﻿… He’s not himself. Well, then, secondly, there’s Nina. The other night did you notice anything?”
“Only that she lost her temper. But she’s always doing that.”
“No, it’s more than that. She’s unhappy, and I don’t like the life she’s leading. Always out at cinematographs and theatres and restaurants, and with a lot of boys who mean no harm, I know﻿—but they’re idiotic, they’re no good.﻿ ﻿… Now, when the war’s like this and the suffering.﻿ ﻿… To be always at the cinematograph! But I’ve lost my authority over her, Ivan Andreievitch. She doesn’t care any longer what I say to her. Once, and not so long ago, I meant so much to her. She’s changed, she’s harder, more careless, more selfish. You know, Ivan Andreievitch, that Nina’s simply everything to me. I don’t talk about myself, do I? but at least I can say that since﻿—oh, many, many years, she’s been the whole world and more than the whole world to me. Our mother and father were killed in a railway accident coming up from Odessa when Nina was very small, and since then Nina’s been mine﻿—all mine!”
She said that word with sudden passion, flinging it at me with a fierce gesture of her hands. “Do you know what it is to want that something should belong to you, belong entirely to you, and to no one else? I’ve been too proud to say, but I’ve wanted that terribly all my life. I haven’t had children, although I prayed for them, and perhaps now it is as well. But Nina!

===Chinese===

她竟然向我求助，我觉得近乎可笑。可我还是深深感动了。
“为你我什么都肯做。”我说。
她朝我转过身来，仪态堂堂，戴着一头黑发的头颅高高扬起，双手叉腰，目光沉静地打量着我。
“有三件事，”她说，“也许全都不算什么。……可又全都叫人心神不宁。第一件，是我丈夫。他又开始喝酒了。”
“喝酒？”我说；“他能从哪儿弄到酒？”
“我不知道。我得查出来。可毛病不在喝酒本身。在我们国家，谁弄得到酒谁就喝。可是什么叫他破了戒？他从前多么以此为豪。你知道他多么看重这个。他还撒谎。他说他没喝。他从前是从不撒谎的。撒谎可不在他的毛病之列。”
“也许是为他那些发明。”我试着说。
“呸！他的发明！这你还不明白吗，伊万·安德烈耶维奇。不，不对。是别的什么。……他不对劲了。那么，第二件，是妮娜。前几天晚上，你察觉到什么了吗？”
“只察觉她发了脾气。可她老是这样。”
“不，不止这个。她不快活，我不喜欢她眼下过的日子。老是往外跑，看电影、上戏园子、下馆子，同一帮小伙子混——我知道他们没有坏心——可他们蠢，没出息。……如今战争打成这样，生灵涂炭。……还整天泡在电影院里！可我对她已经没有管束的力量了，伊万·安德烈耶维奇。我对她说什么，她再也不放在心上。就在不久之前，我在她心里还那么有分量。她变了，变硬了，更漫不经心，更自私了。你知道的，伊万·安德烈耶维奇，妮娜就是我的全部。我从不谈自己，对不对？可至少我可以说，从——哦，许多许多年前起，她就是我的整个世界，比整个世界还要多。我们的母亲和父亲从敖德萨北上的路上死于火车失事，那时妮娜还很小，从那以后，妮娜就是我一个人的——全是我一个人的！”
她说出“一个人”这几个字时忽然满腔激愤，双手猛地一挥，像把它掷到我脸上。“你可知道，盼望一样东西属于你，完完全全属于你，不属于任何别人，是什么滋味？我一直骄傲得不肯说，可我一辈子都在苦苦盼望这个。我没有生过孩子，虽然我祈求过，如今看来或许倒也是好事。可是妮娜！”

===Original===

She’s known she was mine, and, until now, she’s loved to know it. But now she’s escaping from me, and she knows that too, and is ashamed. I think I could bear anything but that sense that she herself has that she’s being wrong﻿—I hate her to be ashamed.”
“Perhaps,” I suggested, “it’s time that she went out into the world now and worked. There are a thousand things that a woman can do.”
“No﻿—not Nina. I’ve spoilt her, perhaps; I don’t know. I always liked to feel that she needed my help. I didn’t want to make her too self-reliant. That was wrong of me, and I shall be punished for it.”
“Speak to her,” I said. “She loves you so much that one word from you to her will be enough.”
“No,” Vera Michailovna said slowly. “It won’t be enough now. A year ago, yes. But now she’s escaping as fast as she can.”
“Perhaps she’s in love with someone,” I suggested.
“No. I should have seen at once if it had been that. I would rather it were that. I think she would come back to me then. No, I suppose that this had to happen. I was foolish to think that it would not. But it leaves one alone﻿—it﻿—”
She pulled herself up at that, regarding me with sudden shyness, as though she would forbid me to hint that she had shown the slightest emotion, or made in any way an appeal for pity.
I was silent, then I said:
“And the third thing, Vera Michailovna?”
“Uncle Alexei is coming back.” That startled me. I felt my heart give one frantic leap.
“Alexei Petrovitch!” I cried. “When? How soon?”
“I don’t know. I’ve had a letter.” She felt in her dress, found the letter and read it through. “Soon, perhaps. He’s leaving the Front for good. He’s disgusted with it all, he says. He’s going to take up his Petrograd practice again.”
“Will he live with you?”
“No. God forbid!”
She felt then, perhaps, that her cry had revealed more than she intended, because she smiled and, trying to speak lightly, said:
“No. We’re old enemies, my uncle and I. We don’t get on. He thinks me sentimental, I think him﻿—but never mind what I think him. He has a bad effect on my husband.”
“A bad effect?” I repeated.
“Yes. He irritates him. He laughs at his inventions, you know.”
I nodded my head. Yes, with my earlier experience of him I could understand that he would do that.
“He’s a cynical, embittered man,” I said. “He believes in nothing and in nobody.

===Chinese===

“她知道自己是我的人，而且直到如今，她一直乐于知道这一点。可现在她在从我身边逃走，她自己心里也明白，还为此羞愧。我想我什么都受得了，唯独受不了她心里那种做错了事的自我感觉——我恨她羞愧。”
“也许，”我提议道，“如今正是她走进社会去工作的时候。女人能做的事有千千万。”
“不——妮娜不行。也许是我惯坏了她；我不知道。我一向喜欢觉得她需要我的帮助。我不想让她太自立足。这是我的错处，我会为此受罚的。”
“跟她谈谈，”我说。“她那样爱你，你一句话就够了。”
“不，”薇拉·米哈伊洛夫娜慢慢地说。“现在一句话不够了。一年前也许够。可现在她正在拼命从我身边逃走。”
“也许她爱上什么人了。”我试着说。
“不是。若是那样，我一眼就能看出来。我倒宁愿是那样。我想，若真是那样，她还会回到我身边来。不，我看这是注定要来的。我从前以为它不会来，真是傻。可这叫人孤单——它——”
说到这里她猛然收住，带着突然的羞怯望着我，仿佛不许我暗示她流露过一丝感情，或是发过半点乞怜之声。
我沉默了，然后说：“那么第三件事呢，薇拉·米哈伊洛夫娜？”
“阿列克谢舅舅要回来了。”
这话吓了我一跳。我感到心口猛地一颤。
“阿列克谢·彼得罗维奇！”我叫道。“什么时候？多快？”
“不知道。我收到一封信。”她在衣袋里摸索，找出信来从头到尾念了一遍。“也许快了。他这次要永远离开前线。他说他对这一切都厌透了。他打算重操彼得格勒的旧业。”
“他会住到你们家来吗？”
“不。千万别！”
也许这时她觉得自己的叫喊泄露了过多的心事，于是她笑了笑，故意装出轻描淡写的口气说：
“不。我们是老对头了，我这位舅舅和我。我们合不来。他嫌我多愁善感，我嫌他——算了，别管我嫌他什么。他对我丈夫影响不好。”
“影响不好？”我重复道。
“是的。他招他烦。你不知道吗，他笑话他的那些发明。”
我点了点头。是啊，凭我早先同他打过的交道，我完全能想见他会干出这种事来。
“他是个玩世不恭、满腔怨毒的人，”我说。“他什么都不信，谁也不信。”

===Original===

And yet he has his fine side﻿—”
“No, he has no fine side,” she interrupted me fiercely. “None. He is a bad man. I’ve known him all my life, and I’m not to be deceived.”
Then in a softer, quieter tone she continued:
“But tell me, Ivan Andreievitch. I’ve wanted before to ask you. You were with him on the Front last year. We have heard that he had a great love affair there, and that the Sister whom he loved was killed. Is that true?”
“Yes,” I said, “that is true.”
“Was he very much in love with her?”
“I believe terribly.”
“And it hurt him deeply when she was killed?”
“Desperately deeply.”
“But what kind of woman was she? What type? It’s so strange to me. Uncle Alexei﻿ ﻿… with his love affairs!”
I looked up, smiling. “She was your very opposite, Vera Michailovna, in everything. Like a child﻿—with no knowledge, no experience, no self-reliance﻿—nothing. She was wonderful in her ignorance and bravery. We all thought her wonderful.”
“And she loved *him*?”
“Yes﻿—she loved him.”
“How strange! Perhaps there is some good in him somewhere. But to us at any rate he always brings trouble. This affair may have changed him. They say he is very different. Worse perhaps﻿—”
She broke out then into a cry:
“I want to get away, Ivan Andreievitch! To get away, to escape, to leave Russia and everything in it behind me! To escape!”
It was just then that Sacha knocked on the door. She came in to say that there was an Englishman in the hall inquiring for the other Englishman who had come yesterday, that he wanted to know when he would be back.
“Perhaps I can help,” I said. I went out into the hall and there I found Jerry Lawrence.
He stood there in the dusk of the little hall looking as resolute and unconcerned as an Englishman, in a strange and uncertain world, is expected to look. Not that he ever considered the attitudes fitting to adopt on certain occasions. He would tell you, if you inquired, that “he couldn’t stand those fellows who looked into every glass they passed.” His brow wore now a simple and innocent frown like that of a healthy baby presented for the first time with a strange and alarming rattle. It was only later that I was to arrive at some faint conception of Lawrence’s marvellous acceptance of anything that might happen to turn up. Vice, cruelty, unsuspected beauty, terror, remorse, hatred, and ignorance﻿—he accepted them all once they were there in front of him.

===Chinese===

“不过他也有他高贵的一面——”
“不，他没有什么高贵的一面，”她激烈地打断我。“没有。他是个坏人。我认识他一辈子了，骗不了我。”
随后她换上一种柔和些、安静些的声调，接着说：
“可是告诉我，伊万·安德烈耶维奇。这话我早就想问你了。去年你在前线同他在一起。我们听说他在那边有过一场轰轰烈烈的恋爱，他爱的那位女护士后来死了。是真的吗？”
“是的，”我说，“是真的。”
“他很爱她吗？”
“我想是爱极了。”
“她死的时候，他伤得很深吧？”
“深得没法说。”
“可她是个怎样的女人？哪一类人？我真想不透。阿列克谢舅舅……居然会谈恋爱！”
我抬起头，笑了。“她同你恰恰相反，薇拉·米哈伊洛夫娜，样样相反。像个孩子——没有见识，没有阅历，一点也不自立——什么都没有。她那份天真与勇敢真是了不起。我们个个都觉得她了不起。”
“她居然爱他？”
“是——她爱他。”
“多奇怪！也许他心里到底还藏着几分好的。可不管怎么说，他带给我们的总是麻烦。这件事也许改变了他。人们说他大不一样了。也许更糟了——”
说到这里，她忽然脱口喊道：
“我想走，伊万·安德烈耶维奇！想走，想逃，想把俄罗斯和俄罗斯的一切都丢在身后！想逃！”
正在这时，萨莎敲了敲门。她进来说，大厅里有位英国人，来找昨天来的那位英国人，想知道他什么时候回来。
“也许我能帮上忙。”我说。我出去走进大厅，就在那儿见到了杰里·劳伦斯。
他站在小门厅的暮色里，那副坚决而满不在乎的神情，正是一个英国人身处陌生莫测的世界时应有的神情。倒不是说他惯于斟酌什么场合该端什么架子。你若问起，他会告诉你，他“看不惯那种路过一面镜子就要照一照的家伙”。此刻他眉头上挂着一道简单天真的皱痕，像一个健康的婴儿头一回见到一只古怪吓人的拨浪鼓。要到后来，我才对劳伦斯那种凡事来了便照单全收的惊人本领有了些微的领会。邪恶、残忍、料想不到的美、恐怖、悔恨、仇恨、愚昧——只要真摆到了他眼前，他一概接受。

===Original===

He sometimes, as I shall on a later occasion, show, allowed himself a free expression of his views in the company of those whom he could trust, but they were never the views of a suspicious or a disappointed man. It was not that he had great faith in human nature. He had, I think, very little. Nor was he without curiosity﻿—far from it. But once a thing was really there he wasted no time over exclamations as to the horror or beauty or abomination of its actual presence. There was as he once explained to me, “precious little time to waste.” Those who thought him a dull, silent fellow﻿—and they were many﻿—made of course an almost ludicrous mistake, but most people in life are, I take it, too deeply occupied with their own personal history to do more than estimate at its surface value the appearance of others﻿ ﻿… but after all such a dispensation makes, in all probability for the general happiness.﻿ ﻿…
On this present occasion Jerry Lawrence stood there exactly as I had seen him stand many times on the football field waiting for the referee’s whistle, his thick short body held together, his mouth shut and his eyes on guard. He did not at first recognise me.
“You’ve forgotten me,” I said.
“I beg your pardon,” he answered in his husky good-natured voice, like the rumble of an amiable bulldog.
“My name is Durward,” I said, holding out my hand. “And years ago we had a mutual friend in Olva Dune.”
That pleased him. He gripped my hand very heartily and smiled a big ugly smile. “Why, yes,” he said. “Of course. How are you? Feeling fit? Damned long ago all that, isn’t it? Hope you’re really fit?”
“Oh, I’m all right,” I answered. “I was never a Hercules, you know. I heard that you were here from Bohun. I was going to write to you. But it’s excellent that we should meet like this.”
“I was after young Bohun,” he explained. “But it’s pleasant to find there’s another fellow in the town one knows. I’ve been a bit at sea these two days. To tell you the truth I never wanted to come.” I heard a rumble in his throat that sounded like “silly blighters.”
“Come in,” I said. “You must meet Madame Markovitch with whom Bohun is staying﻿—and then wait a bit. He won’t be long, I expect.”
The idea of this seemed to fill Jerry with alarm. He turned back toward the door. “Oh! I don’t think﻿ ﻿… she won’t want﻿ ﻿… better another time﻿ ﻿…” his mouth was filled with indistinct rumblings.

===Chinese===

在信得过的人中间，他有时也放言无忌地大发议论——这一点容我以后再细讲——可那从来不是一个多疑者或失意者的议论。这倒不是说他对人性的信心有多深。我想，他的信心很浅。他也并非没有好奇心——远非如此。可是一旦事情真的到了眼前，他就绝不再浪费工夫，去惊叹它实际呈现的可怖、美丽或可憎。正如他有一次对我解释的：“工夫金贵得很，浪费不起。”那些把他当作乏味寡言之辈的人——这种人很多——自然是大错特错，错得近乎可笑。不过我想，世上大多数人，都太埋头于自己那本个人的历史，对旁人的相貌至多照着表面估个价而已……但说到底，这样的安排，多半倒成全了众人的安乐。……
这时，杰里·劳伦斯站在那儿，那姿态同我从前在足球场上多次见过的一模一样——等着裁判开球的哨声：敦实矮壮的身子收得紧紧的，闭着嘴，眼睛放哨。他起初没有认出我。
“你把我忘了。”我说。
“对不住，”他用他那沙哑而和善的嗓音答道，像一条好脾气的斗牛犬的低吼。
“我姓德沃德，”我伸出手去说。“多年前咱们有过一位共同的朋友，奥尔瓦·杜恩。”
这话叫他高兴了。他热热乎乎地攥住我的手，咧开一个大大的、不算好看的笑。“哎呀，是啊，”他说。“当然记得。你好吗？身子骨硬朗吗？那都是老早以前的事了，是不是？希望你真的硬朗？”
“哦，我还好，”我答道。“你知道，我从来当不成赫拉克勒斯。我是从博亨那儿听说你来了。我正要给你写信呢。不过咱们这样碰上了，再好没有。”
“我是来找小博亨的，”他解释道。“不过在这城里又碰见一个熟人，也挺美。这两天我有点儿两眼一抹黑。跟你说实话，我压根儿就不想来。”我听见他喉咙里咕哝了一团含混的声音，像是“一群蠢货”。
“进来吧，”我说。“你得见见博亨借住的那家的马尔科维奇夫人——然后等一等。我想他去不了多久。”
这个主意似乎把杰里吓了一跳。他转身又朝门口退。“哦！我看……她不会愿意……还是改天吧……”他嘴里含混地咕哝个不停。

===Original===

“Nonsense.” I caught his arm. “She is delightful. You must make yourself at home here. They’ll be only too glad.”
“Does she speak English?” he asked.
“No,” I answered. “But that’s all right.”
He backed again towards the door.
“My Russian’s so slow,” he said. “Never been here since I was a kid. I’d rather not, really﻿—”
However, I dragged him in and introduced him. I had quite a fatherly desire, as I watched him, that “he should make good.” But I’m afraid that that first interview was not a great success. Vera Michailovna was strange that afternoon, excited and disturbed as I had never known her, and I could see that it was only with the greatest difficulty that she could bring herself to think about Jerry at all.
And Jerry himself was so unresponsive that I could have beaten him. Why, you’re duller than you used to be, I thought to myself, and wondered how I could have suspected, in those days, subtle depths and mysterious comprehensions. Vera Michailovna asked him questions about France and London but, quite obviously, did not listen to his answers.
After ten minutes he pulled himself up slowly from his chair:
“Well, I must be going,” he said. “Tell young Bohun I shall be waiting for him tonight﻿—7:30﻿—Astoria﻿—” He turned to Vera Michailovna to say goodbye, and then, suddenly, as she rose and their eyes met, they seemed to strike some unexpected chord of sympathy. It took both of them, I think, by surprise; for quite a moment they stared at one another.
“Please come whenever you want to see your friend,” she said, “we shall be delighted.”
“Thank you,” he answered simply, and went.
When he had gone she said to me:
“I like that man. One could trust him.”
“Yes, one could,” I answered her.

===Chinese===

“胡说。”我一把抓住他的胳膊。“她这人再可爱不过。你只管把这儿当自己家。他们会非常高兴的。”
“她说英语吗？”他问。
“不说，”我答。“不过没关系。”
他又朝门口退。
“我的俄语太迟钝，”他说。“打小时候来过一回，就再没来过。我真的宁可不——”
但我还是把他拽了进去，引见了一番。我望着他，心里真怀着几分父辈的期望，盼他“能拿出个样子来”。可惜我恐怕，这头一面的成绩不算漂亮。那天下午薇拉·米哈伊洛夫娜神情反常，激动不安得我从未见过，我看得出，她要费天大的劲儿，才能勉强把心思挪到杰里身上来。
而杰里自己又那样叫人搭不上话，简直让我想揍他。哎呀，你比从前更乏味了，我暗自想道，一面纳闷，当年我怎么会疑心他有什么幽深的城府、什么神秘的洞见。薇拉·米哈伊洛夫娜问了他些法国和伦敦的情形，可是明摆着，并不听他回答。
十分钟后，他慢腾腾地从椅子里撑起身来。
“呃，我该走了，”他说。“告诉小博亨，我今晚等他——7:30——阿斯托利亚——”他转向薇拉·米哈伊洛夫娜道别。就在她站起身来、两人目光相接的一刹那，他们之间仿佛拨响了一根意想不到的同情之弦。我想，他们两个都吃了一惊；两人竟怔怔地对望了好一会儿。
“你想见你朋友的时候，请随时来，”她说，“我们都非常高兴。”
“谢谢你，”他简单地答了一句，走了。
他走之后，她对我说：
“我喜欢这个人。可以信得过他。”
“是的，可以信得过。”我回答她。

### X / 十
===Original===

We were approaching Christmas. The weather of these weeks was wonderfully beautiful, sharply cold, the sky pale bird’s-egg blue, the ice and the snow glittering, shining with a thousand colours. There began now a strange relationship between Markovitch and myself.
There was something ineffectual and pessimistic about me that made Russians often feel in me a kindred soul. At the Front, Russians had confided in me again and again, but that was not astonishing, because they confided in everyone. Nevertheless, they felt that I was less English than the rest, and rather blamed me in their minds, I think, for being so. I don’t know what it was that suddenly decided Markovitch to “make me part of his life.” I certainly did not on my side make any advances.
One evening he came to see me and stayed for hours. Then he came two or three times within the following fortnight. He gave me the effect of not caring in the least whether I were there or no, whether I replied or remained silent, whether I asked questions or simply pursued my own work. And I, on my side, had soon in my consciousness his odd, irascible, nervous, pleading, shy and boastful figure painted permanently, so that his actual physical presence seemed to be unimportant. There he was, as he liked to stand up against the white stove in my draughty room, his rather dirty nervous hands waving in front of me, his thin hair on end, his ragged beard giving his eyes an added expression of anxiety. His body was a poor affair, his legs thin and uncertain, an incipient stomach causing his waistcoat suddenly to fall inwards somewhere halfway up his chest, his feet in ill-shapen boots, and his neck absurdly small inside his high stiff collar. His stiff collar jutting sharply into his weak chin was perhaps his most striking feature. Most Russians of his careless habits wore soft collars or students’ shirts that fastened tight about the neck, but this high white collar was with Markovitch a sign and a symbol, the banner of his early ambitions; it was the first and last of him. He changed it every day, it was always high and sharp, gleaming and clean, and it must have hurt him very much. He wore with it a shabby black tie that ran as far up the collar as it could go, and there was a sense of pathos and struggle about this tie as though it were a wild animal trying to escape over an imprisoning wall.

===Chinese===

我们快到圣诞节了。这几个星期的天气美极了，冷得凛冽，天空是淡得像鸟蛋壳的蓝色，冰雪闪闪发光，映出千种颜色。就在这时，马尔科维奇和我之间开始了一段奇怪的关系。
我身上有一种无所作为的、悲观的东西，常让俄国人觉得我是他们的同类。在前线，俄国人一再向我倾吐心事，那倒不奇怪，因为他们对谁都倾吐。不过，他们总觉得我比起其他英国人少了些英国气，我想，心里还暗暗为此怪我。不知道是什么使马尔科维奇突然决定“把我纳入他的生活”。我这边肯定没有主动示过好。
一天晚上他来看我，一坐几个钟头。随后半个月里又来了两三次。他给人的感觉是，我在不在、我搭不搭腔、我是发问还是只顾做自己的事，他全然无所谓。而我这边呢，他那个古怪的、暴躁的、神经质的、带点恳求、又羞怯又爱吹嘘的身影，很快就在我心里定格成了一幅永久的画像，以致他本人到不到场反倒显得不重要了。他就在那儿，爱站在我那间四面透风的屋里的白炉子跟前，那双不大干净的神经质的手在我眼前挥动，稀疏的头发根根直立，乱蓬蓬的胡子给他的眼睛平添几分焦虑。他的身子是个可怜的构造：两条腿细而站不稳，初起的肚子让他的背心在胸口半腰处忽然向里塌陷，脚上一双变了形的靴子，脖子在高高的硬领里显得小得可笑。那副硬领尖尖地顶着他软弱的下巴，兴许是他最显眼的特征。他那副懒散习气的俄国人多半穿软领，或者穿领口紧扣的学生衫，可马尔科维奇的这副高白领是一种记号、一个象征，是他早年抱负的旗帜；这是他的第一样东西，也是最后一样。他每天换领子，领子总是又高又尖，雪亮干净，想必硌得他很疼。领子底下配一条寒酸的黑色领带，能拉多高就拉多高，这领带透着一种挣扎的悲怆，仿佛一只野兽想翻越囚禁它的高墙。

===Original===

He would stand clutching my stove as though it assured his safety in a dangerous country; then suddenly he would break away from it and start careering up and down my room, stopping for an instant to gaze through my window at the sea and the ships, then off again, swinging his arms, his anxious eyes searching everywhere for confirmation of the ambitions that still enflamed him.
For the root and soul of him was that he was greatly ambitious. He had been born, I learnt, in some small town in the Moscow province, and his father had been a schoolmaster in the place﻿—a kind of Perodonov, I should imagine, from the things that Markovitch told me about him. The father, at any rate, was a mean, malicious, and grossly sensual creature, and he finally lost his post through his improper behaviour towards some of his own small pupils. The family then came to evil days, and at a very early age young Markovitch was sent to Petrograd to earn what he could with his wits. He managed to secure the post of a secretary to an old fellow who was engaged in writing the life of his grandfather﻿—a difficult book, as the grandfather had been a voluminous letter-writer, and this correspondence had to be collected and tabulated. For months, and even years, young Markovitch laboriously endeavoured to arrange these old yellow letters, dull, pathetic, incoherent. His patron grew slowly imbecile, but through the fogs that increasingly besieged him saw only this one thing clearly, that the letters must be arranged. He kept Markovitch relentlessly at his table, allowing him no pleasures, feeding him miserably and watching him personally undress every evening lest he should have secreted certain letters somewhere on his body. There was something almost sadist apparently in the old gentleman’s observation of Markovitch’s labours.
It was during these years that Markovitch’s ambitions took flame. He was always as he told me having “amazing ideas.” I asked him﻿—What kind of ideas? “Ideas by which the world would be transformed.﻿ ﻿… Those letters were all old, you know, and dusty, and yellow, and eaten, some of them, by rats, and they’d lie on the floor and I’d try to arrange them in little piles according to their dates.﻿ ﻿… There’d be rows of little packets all across the floor﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

他常抓住我的炉子站着，仿佛在这危险的国度里炉子能保他平安；随后又忽然撒开手，在我屋里横冲直撞地踱来踱去，停下一瞬，隔着窗望一望海和船，接着又走起来，抡着胳膊，那双焦虑的眼睛四处搜寻，想为他胸中仍在燃烧的抱负找到印证。
因为他的根和灵魂，便是野心勃勃。我得知他生在莫斯科省的某座小城，父亲是当地的学堂教师——从马尔科维奇讲给我的那些事看，我想是个佩罗东诺夫式的人物。不管怎样，那位父亲是个卑鄙、恶毒、极其好色之徒，最后因为对自己年幼的学生行为不检点而丢了差事。家道从此败落，小小年纪的马尔科维奇便被送到彼得格勒，靠脑子挣一口饭吃。
他设法谋到一个秘书的位子，伺候一位正撰写自己祖父传记的老先生——那是本难弄的书，因为祖父生前写信写得极多，这些通信都得收集起来、编目整理。一连数月甚至数年，年轻的马尔科维奇辛辛苦苦地整理那些发黄的旧信，枯燥、可怜、颠三倒四。他的东家慢慢变得痴呆，可在他日益浓重的迷雾里，只有一件事看得真切：信必须整理。他毫不留情地把马尔科维奇摁在桌前，不给他一点消遣，给他吃得很坏，还每晚亲自监视他脱衣裳，生怕他把某些信藏在自己身上什么地方。这位老先生观察马尔科维奇干活的架势，几乎有种虐待狂的味道。
正是在那几年，马尔科维奇的野心燃起来了。他告诉我，他总有“惊人的想法”。我问他——什么样的想法？“能把世界翻个新的想法。……那些信都是旧的，你知道，落满灰、发了黄，有几封还被老鼠啃了，摊得一地都是，我按日期把它们一摞一摞码好。……满屋子一排排的小纸包……

===Original===

, and then somehow, when one’s back was turned, they’d move, all of their own wicked purpose﻿—and one would have to begin all over again, bending with one’s back aching, and seeing always the stupid handwriting.﻿ ﻿… I hated it, Ivan Andreievitch, of course I hated it, but I had to do it for the money. And I lived in his house, too, and as he got madder it wasn’t pleasant. He wanted me to sleep with him because he saw things in the middle of the night, and he’d catch hold of me and scream and twist his fat legs round me﻿ ﻿… no, it wasn’t agreeable. *On ne sympatichne saff-szem.* He wasn’t a nice man at all. But while I was sorting the letters these ideas would come to me and I would be on fire.﻿ ﻿… It seemed to me that I was to save the world, and that it would not be difficult if only one might be resolute enough. That was the trouble﻿—to be resolute. One might say to oneself, ‘On Friday October 13th I will do so-and-so, and then on Saturday November 3rd I will do so-and-so, and then on December 24th it will be finished.’ But then on October 13th one is, maybe, in quite another mood﻿—one is even ill possibly﻿—and so nothing is done and the whole plan is ruined.
