“Not at all!” I answered. “I’ve got a telephone. I’m very happy where I am.”
“It is a queer place,” he said. “Isn’t it awfully unhealthy?”
“Quite the reverse﻿—with the sea in front of it! About the healthiest spot in Petrograd!”
“But I should get the blues here. So lonely and quiet.

===Chinese===

我忽然好起来了。热度退尽，只是还躺在床上，虚弱，而且饿得很。我快活，来彼得格勒以后从没这样快活过。康复期的奢侈滋味渗进了骨头里。我什么也不用做，只消躺着，让人喂，头不疼就读一点书。对面墙上挂着一幅亚历山大·贝努瓦的水彩，高加索的一夜：一道浓黑的山的弧线，一片沉静的深蓝天穹，一条灰色细路伸向无穷的远方。一幅叫人惬意的画，意味无穷又不把话说尽——还亲近，仿佛那路是我自己的路，山是我自己的山。去年在“艺术世界”（Mir Eskoustva）展览上我不惜血本买下了它，如今倒为自己的挥霍得意了。
玛尔法待我极好，喂我吃的，跟我怄气好叫我打起精神，对我的来访者调度得极有分寸。一拨一拨的人，英国人俄国人，都来看我——我不知道自己竟有这么多朋友。我对全人类都和气起来，对世事也抱着希望了。回望病前那段日子，恍如一场噩梦。
人人都说我傻，住在我这破地方，又冷又荒又孤单。可等他们再来，又拿不准了；他们望着窗外阳光下如波浪与光影般明亮的冰面，心想也许自己也该试试。当然，我心里有数，他们不会的。……
身体一有劲，我对病倒时正在展开的那部历史，就烧起一股强烈的热望。听说薇拉·米哈伊洛夫娜和尼娜来过好几趟。马尔科维奇来过，亨利·博亨和劳伦斯也来过。
后来，一个晴朗的下午，亨利·博亨进来了，我竟为自己的高兴吃了一惊。他见到我的变化吓了一跳，又年轻，藏不住。
“哦，你气色可真糟！”他在我床边坐下，头一句就是这个。“我说，你住这儿舒服吗？不想搬到方便点的地方去——有电话、有电梯什么的？”
“一点也不想！”我回答，“我有电话。住这儿快活得很。”
“这地方真怪，”他说，“不是很不卫生吗？”
“恰恰相反——门前就是海！全彼得格勒顶养人的地方！”
“换了我可要发愁。这么孤零零、静悄悄的。

===Original===

Petrograd is a strange town! Most people don’t dream there’s a queer place like this.”
“That’s why I like it,” I said. “I expect there are lots of queer places in Petrograd if you only knew.”
He wandered about the room, looking at my few pictures and my books and my writing-table. At last he sat down again by my bed.
“Now tell me all the news,” I said.
“News?” he asked. He looked uncomfortable, and I saw at once that he had come to confide something in me. “What sort of news? Political?”
“Anything.”
“Well, politics are about the same. They say there’s going to be an awful row in February when the Duma meets﻿—but then other people say there won’t be a row at all until the war is over.”
“What else do they say?”
“They say Protopopoff is up to all sorts of tricks. That he says prayers with the Empress and they summon Rasputin’s ghost.﻿ ﻿… That’s all rot of course. But he does just what the Empress tells him, and they’re going to enslave the whole country and hand it over to Germany.”
“What will they do that for?” I asked.
“Why, then, the Czarevitch will have it﻿—under Germany. They say that none of the munitions are going to the Front, and Protopopoff’s keeping them all to blow up the people here with.”
“What else?” I asked sarcastically.
“No, but really, there’s something in it, I expect.” Henry looked serious and important. “Then on the other hand, Clutton-Davies says the Czar’s absolutely all right, dead keen on the war and hates Germany﻿ ﻿… *I* don’t know﻿—but Clutton-Davies sees him nearly every day.”
“Anything else?” I asked.
“Oh, food’s worse than ever! Going up every day, and the bread queues are longer and longer. The Germans have spies in the queues, women who go up and down telling people it’s all England’s fault.”
“And people are just the same?”
“Just the same; Donons’ and the Bear are crowded every day. You can’t get a table. So are the cinematographs and the theatres. I went to the Ballet last night.”
“What was it?”
“*La Fille Mal Gardée*﻿—Karsavina dancing divinely. Everyone was there.”
This closed the strain of public information. I led him further.
“Well, Bohun, what about our friends the Markovitches?” I asked. “How are you getting on there?”
He blushed and looked at his boots.
“All right,” he said. “They’re very decent.”
Then he burst out with: “I say, Durward, what do you think of this uncle that’s turned up, the doctor chap?”

===Chinese===

彼得格勒是座奇怪的城！多数人做梦也想不到有这么个怪地方。”
“我就喜欢它这点，”我说，“我看彼得格勒有的是怪地方，只看你知不知道。”
他在屋里踱来踱去，看看我那几幅画、我的书和我的写字台。末了又在我床边坐下。
“好了，把新闻都讲给我听。”我说。
“新闻？”他反问，神情不自在，我一眼看出他是有心事要对我讲。“哪类新闻？政治的？”
“随便什么。”
“嗯，政治还是老样子。都说二月杜马开会要大闹一场——可又有人说，仗打完之前根本闹不起来。”
“还说什么？”
“说普罗托波波夫什么鬼把戏都玩。说他跟皇后一起祷告，两人一起召拉斯普京的亡魂。……当然是胡扯。可他确实皇后说什么他干什么，他们要把全国变成奴隶，交给德国。”
“他们图什么？”我问。
“嘿，那样皇储就能坐天下——在德国底下。都说军火根本没运到前线，普罗托波波夫全扣下了，留着炸这儿的老百姓。”
“还有呢？”我讷谑地问。
“不，说真的，我看这里头有名堂。”亨利一副郑重其事的样子。“可另一方面，克拉顿-戴维斯说沙皇完全没问题，打仗死心塌地，恨德国入骨……我就不知道了——不过克拉顿-戴维斯差不多天天见他。”
“还有别的吗？”
“哦，吃的比任何时候都紧！天天涨价，面包队越排越长。德国人往队伍里派了奸细，都是女人，来来回回地跟人说这全是英国的错。”
“人呢？还是老样子？”
“老样子；多农餐厅和熊餐厅天天客满，桌子都抢不着。电影院剧院也一样。我昨晚看芭蕾去了。”
“什么戏？”
“《关不住的女儿》——卡萨维娜跳得跟仙女似的。谁都在场。”
公共消息这一路就到此为止。我引他往深处走。
“那么，博亨，咱们的朋友马尔科维奇一家怎么样？”我问，“你在那儿处得如何？”
他红了脸，看自己的靴子。
“挺好，”他说，“人都很不赖。”
随后他脱口而出：“我说，德沃德，你对那位冒出来的舅舅怎么看，就是当医生的那位？”

===Original===

“Nothing particular. Why?”
“You were with him at the Front, weren’t you?”
“I was.”
“Was he a good doctor?”
“Excellent.”
“He had a love affair at the Front, hadn’t he?”
“Yes.”
“And she was killed?”
“Yes.”
“Poor devil.﻿ ﻿…” Then he added: “Did he mind very much?”
“Very much.”
“Funny thing, you wouldn’t think he would.”
“Why not,” I asked.
“Oh, he looks a hard sort of fellow﻿—as though he’d stand anything. I wouldn’t like to have a row with him.”
“Has he been to the Markovitches much lately?”
“Yes﻿—almost every evening.”
“What does he do there?”
“Oh, just sits and talks. Markovitch can’t bear him. You can see that easily enough. He teases him.”
“How do you mean?” I asked.
“Oh, he laughs at him all the time, at his inventions and that kind of thing. Markovitch gets awfully wild. He is bit of an ass, isn’t he?”
“Do you like Semyonov?” I asked.
“I do rather,” said Henry. “He’s very decent to me. I had a walk with him one afternoon. He said you were awfully brave at the Front.”
“Thank him for nothing,” I said.
“And he said you didn’t like him﻿—don’t you?”
“Ah, that’s too old a story,” I answered. “We know what we feel about one another.”
“Well, Lawrence simply hates him,” continued Bohun. “He says he’s the most thundering cad, and as bad as you make them. I don’t see how he can tell.”
This interested me extremely. “When did he tell you this?” I asked.
“Yesterday. I asked him what he had to judge by and he said instinct. I said he’d no right to go only by that.”
“Has Lawrence been much to the Markovitches?”
“Yes﻿—once or twice. He just sits there and never opens his mouth.”
“Very wise of him if he hasn’t got anything to say.”
“No, but really﻿—do you think so? It doesn’t make him popular.”
“Why, who doesn’t like him?”
“Nobody,” answered Henry ungrammatically. “None of the English anyway. They can’t stand him at the Embassy or the Mission. They say he’s fearfully stuck-up and thinks about nothing but himself.﻿ ﻿… I don’t agree, of course﻿—all the same, he might make himself more agreeable to people.”
“What nonsense!” I answered hotly. “Lawrence is one of the best fellows that ever breathed. The Markovitches don’t dislike him, do they?”
“No, he’s quite different with them. Vera Michailovna likes him I know.”
It was the first time that he had mentioned her name to me. He turned towards me now, his face crimson. “I say﻿—that’s really what I came to talk about, Durward.

“没什么特别的看法。怎么问？”
“你打仗时跟他一起在前线，是吧？”
“是。”
“他是个好医生吗？”
“顶好。”
“他在前线有过一段情事，对吧？”
“对。”
“后来她死了？”
“死了。”
“可怜的家伙。……”他顿了顿又问：“他当时很伤心吗？”
“很伤心。”
“怪了，看不出来他会。”
“为什么看不出来？”我问。
“哦，他那副样子是个硬心肠的人——仿佛什么都扛得住。我可不想跟他闹翻。”
“他近来常去马尔科维奇家吗？”
“常去——差不多每天晚上。”
“去了干什么？”
“哦，就是坐着聊天。马尔科维奇受不了他。这谁都看得出来。他逗他。”
“怎么个逗法？”我问。
“哦，一个劲儿笑话他，笑他的发明什么的。马尔科维奇气得要疯。他也是有点蠢，对吧？”
“你喜欢谢苗诺夫吗？”我问。
“还算喜欢，”亨利说，“他待我很好。有天下午我跟他散过一回步。他说你在前线特别勇敢。”
“替我谢谢他。”
“他还说你不喜欢他——你不喜欢吗？”
“啊，那是段太旧的公案了，”我回答，“我们对彼此的心思，彼此心里有数。”
“反正劳伦斯顶讨厌他，”博亨接着说，“说他是个天字第一号的无赖，坏得没法再坏。我不懂他凭什么断定。”
这话叫我极感兴趣。“他什么时候跟你说的？”我问。
“昨天。我问他拿什么作判断，他说是直觉。我说光凭直觉没权利下断语。”
“劳伦斯常去马尔科维奇家吗？”
“去——去过一两回。他就那么坐着，一句话不开口。”
“他要是无话可说，不开口再聪明不过。”
“不，说真的——你也这么想？这可不讨人喜欢。”
“怎么，谁不喜欢他？”
“谁都不喜欢，”亨利这话说得不成语法。“反正英国人没一个喜欢。使馆里、使团里都容不下他。说他傲得吓人，心里除了自己没别的。……我当然不同意——不过话说回来，他也未免太不随和。”
“胡说八道！”我动气了，“劳伦斯是天底下数得着的好人。马尔科维奇一家不讨厌他吧？”
“不，他在他们跟前完全是另一个人。薇拉·米哈伊洛夫娜就喜欢他，我知道。”
这是他头一回在我面前提她的名字。他朝我转过身来，脸涨得通红。“我说——我今天来，其实就是要讲这个，德沃德。

===Original===

I care for her madly!﻿ ﻿… I’d die for her. I would really. I love her, Durward. I see now I’ve never loved anybody before.”
“Well, what will you do about it?”
“Do about it?﻿ ﻿… Why nothing, of course. It’s all perfectly hopeless. In the first place, there’s Markovitch.”
“Yes. There’s Markovitch,” I agreed.
“She doesn’t care for him﻿—does she? You know that﻿—” He waited, eagerly staring into my face.
I had a temptation to laugh. He was so very young, so very helpless, and yet﻿—that sense of his youth had pathos in it too, and I suddenly liked young Bohun﻿—for the first time.
“Look here, Bohun,” I said, trying to speak with a proper solemnity. “Don’t be a young ass. You know that it’s hopeless, any feeling of that kind. She *does* care for her husband. She could never care for you in that way, and you’d only make trouble for them all if you went on with it.﻿ ﻿… On the other hand, she needs a friend badly. You can do that for her. Be her pal. See that things are all right in the house. Make a friend of Markovitch himself. Look after *him*!”
“Look after Markovitch!” Bohun exclaimed.
“Yes﻿ ﻿… I don’t want to be melodramatic, but there’s trouble coming there; and if you’re the friend of them all, you can help﻿—more than you know. Only none of the other business﻿—”
Bohun flushed. “She doesn’t know﻿—she never will. I only want to be a friend of hers, as you put it. Anything else is hopeless, of course. I’m not the kind of fellow she’d ever look at, even if Markovitch wasn’t there. But if I can do anything﻿ ﻿… I’d be awfully glad. What kind of trouble do you mean?” he asked.
“Probably nothing,” I said; “only she wants a friend. And Markovitch wants one too.”
There was a pause﻿—then Bohun said, “I say, Durward﻿—what an awful ass I was.”
“What about?” I asked.
“About my poetry﻿—and all that. Thinking it so important.”
“Yes,” I said, “you were.”
“I’ve written some poetry to her and I tore it up,” he ended.
“That’s a good thing,” said I.
“I’m glad I told you,” he said. He got up to go. “I say, Durward﻿—”
“Well,” I asked.
“You’re an awfully funny chap. Not a bit what you look﻿—”
“That’s all right,” I said; “I know what you mean.”
“Well, good night,” he said, and went.

===Chinese===

我爱她爱得要命！……我肯为她死。真的肯。我爱她，德沃德。我如今才明白，我从前谁也没爱过。”
“那么你打算怎么办？”
“怎么办？……自然什么都不办。完全没有指望。头一条，有马尔科维奇在。”
“是。有马尔科维奇在。”我随声附和。
“她不喜欢他——对吧？这你知道——”他等着我，眼睛急切地盯着我的脸。
我有点想笑。他那么年轻，那么无措，然而——他这份年轻里又有一种动人的东西，我忽然喜欢起年轻的博亨来——头一回喜欢。
“听我说，博亨，”我尽量拿出应有的郑重，“别犯傻。你知道这种感情没有指望。她喜欢她的丈夫。她那种感情永远不会给你，你再缠下去，只会给他们全家添乱。……不过话说回来，她极需要一位朋友。这个你做得到。做她的伙伴。留心家里诸事都顺。跟马尔科维奇本人也交个朋友。多照应照应他！”
“照应马尔科维奇！”博亨叫起来。
“是……我不想危言耸听，可那家里要有麻烦了；你要是他们大家的朋友，就帮得上忙——比你以为的还大。只是不许有别的念头——”
博亨脸红了。“她不知道——永远不会知道。我只想做她的朋友，照你说的。别的当然没指望。就算没有马尔科维奇，我也不是她肯看一眼的那号人。可要是我能出点力……我乐意极了。你说的是哪种麻烦？”他问。
“多半什么也不是，”我说，“只不过她要个朋友。马尔科维奇也要。”
停了片刻——博亨说：“我说，德沃德——我先前多蠢啊。”
“哪方面？”我问。
“我的诗——还有那一套。把它们当成天大的事。”
“是，”我说，“是蠢。”
“我给她写过几首诗，后来撕了。”他末了说。
“斯得好。”我说。
“亏得跟你说出来了。”他说。他起身要走。“我说，德沃德——”
“嗯？”我问。
“你这人真有意思。跟你那副长相一点都不像——”
“行了，”我说，“我懂你的意思。”
“那，晚安。”他说完就走了。

### XVI / 十六
===Original===

I thought that night, as I lay cosily in my dusky room, of those old stories by Wilkie Collins that had once upon a time so deeply engrossed my interest﻿—stories in which, because someone has disappeared on a snowy night, or painted his face blue, or locked up a room and lost the key, or broken down in his carriage on a windy night at the crossroads, dozens of people are involved, diaries are written, confessions are made, and all the characters move along different roads towards the same lighted, comfortable Inn. That is the kind of story that intrigues me, whether it be written about outside mysteries by Wilkie Collins or inside mysteries by the great creator of *The Golden Bowl* or mysteries of both kinds, such as Henry Galleon has given us. I remember a friend of mine, James Maradick, once saying to me, “It’s no use trying to keep out of things. As soon as they want to put you in﻿—you’re in. The moment you’re born, you’re done for.”
It’s just that spectacle of some poor innocent being suddenly caught into some affair, against his will, without his knowledge, but to the most serious alteration of his character and fortunes, that one watches with a delight almost malicious﻿—whether it be *The Woman in White*, *The Wings of the Dove*, or *The Roads* that offer it us. Well, I had now to face the fact that something of this kind had happened to myself.
I was drawn in﻿—and I was glad. I luxuriated in my gladness, lying there in my room under the wavering, uncertain light of two candles, hearing the church bells clanging and echoing mysteriously beyond the wall. I lay there with a consciousness of being on the very verge of some adventure, with the assurance, too, that I was to be of use once more, to play my part, to fling aside, thank God, that old cloak of apathetic disappointment, of selfish betrayal, of cynical disbelief. Semyonov had brought the old life back to me and I had shrunk from the impact of it; but he had brought back to me, too, the presences of my absent friends who, during these weary months, had been lost to me. It seemed to me that, in the flickering twilight, John and Marie were bringing forward to me Vera and Nina and Jerry and asking me to look after them.﻿ ﻿… I would do my best.
And while I was thinking of these things Vera Michailovna came in.

===Chinese===

那天夜里，我舒舒服服躺在我幽暗的房间里，想起了威尔基·柯林斯那些老故事——它们当年曾那样紧紧抓住我——故事里，只因有人在雪夜里失踪了，或把脸涂成蓝色，或锁上一间屋子丢了钥匙，或在起风的夜里于十字路口翻了车，几十个人便被卷了进来，日记一篇篇写出来，忏悔一场场做出来，所有人物沿着各不相同的路，走向同一家灯火通明、温暖舒适的客栈。正是这种故事叫我着迷，不管是威尔基·柯林斯写的外部之谜，《金碗》那位伟大作家写的内心之谜，还是亨利·加利恩给我们的那种内外兼备之谜。我记得我的朋友詹姆斯·马拉迪克有一回对我说：“想置身事外是没用的。人家一要你进去——你就在里头了。你一落地，就完了。”
正是这样一种光景——某个无辜的可怜人，违背己意、浑然不觉地被突然拽进一桩事里，性格和命运却被彻底改写——让人带着近乎恶意的兴致看下去，不管它来自《白衣女人》、《鸽翼》，还是《路》。好了，如今我不得不面对这个事实：这样的事落到我自己头上了。
我被卷进去了——而且我高兴。我躺在这房间里，在两支蜡烛摇曳不定的光下，奢侈地品味着这份高兴，听着墙外教堂钟声铮铮，神秘地回荡。我躺在那儿，意识到自己正站在一场奇遇的边上，还带着一种确信：我又要有用了，要扮演我的角色了，要甩掉——感谢上帝——那件旧斗篷了：冷漠的失意，自私的背叛，犬償的不信。谢苗诺夫把旧日的生活带回到我面前，我曾畏缩过；可他也带回了我不在场的朋友们——那几个疲惫的月份里，我失去了他们。在闪烁的幽光里，我仿佛看见约翰和玛丽把薇拉、尼娜和杰里领到我面前，托付我照看他们。……我必尽力。
我正想着这些，薇拉·米哈伊洛夫娜进来了。

===Original===

She was suddenly in the room, standing there, her furs up to her throat, her body in shadow, but her large, grave eyes shining through the candlelight, her mouth smiling.
“Is it all right?” she said, coming forward. “I’m not in the way? You’re not sleeping?”
I told her that I was delighted to see her.
“I’ve been almost every day, but Marfa told me you were not well enough. She *does* guard you﻿—like a dragon. But tonight Nina and I are going to Rozanov’s, to a party, and she said she’d meet me here.﻿ ﻿… Shan’t I worry you?”
“Worry me! You’re the most restful friend I have﻿—” I felt so glad to see her that I was surprised at my own happiness. She sat down near to me, very quietly, moving, as she always did, softly and surely.
I could see that she was distressed because I looked ill, but she asked me no tiresome questions, said nothing about my madness in living as I did (always so irritating, as though I were a stupid child), praised the room, admired the Benois picture, and then talked in her soft, kindly voice.
“We’ve missed you so much, Nina and I,” she said. “I told Nina that if she came tonight she wasn’t to make a noise and disturb you.”
“She can make as much noise as she likes,” I said. “I like the right kind of noise.”
We talked a little about politics and England and anything that came into our minds. We both felt, I know, a delightful, easy intimacy and friendliness and trust. I had never with any other woman felt such a sense of friendship, something almost masculine in its comradeship and honesty. And tonight this bond between us strengthened wonderfully. I blessed my luck. I saw that there were dark lines under her eyes and that she was pale.
“You’re tired,” I said.
“Yes, I am,” she acknowledged. “And I don’t know why. At least, I do know. I’m going to use you selfishly, Durdles. I’m going to tell you all my troubles and ask your help in every possible way. I’m going to let you off nothing.”
I took her hand.
“I’m proud,” I said, “now and always.”
“Do you know that I’ve never asked anyone’s help before? I was rather conceited that I could get on always without it. When I was very small I wouldn’t take a word of advice from anyone, and mother and father, when I was tiny, used to consult me about everything. Then they were killed and I *had* to go on alone.﻿ ﻿… And after that, when I married Nicholas, it was I again who decided everything.

===Chinese===

她一下子就在屋里了，站在那儿，皮衣一直裹到喉咙，身子隐在暗处，只有那双大而端凝的眼睛透过烛光发亮，嘴角含笑。
“方便吗？”她走上前来说，“不打扰你？没在睡吧？”
我告诉她，见到她我很高兴。
“我几乎天天来，可玛尔法总说你身体还不够好。她可真护着你——像条龙。今晚我和尼娜要去罗扎诺夫家参加聚会，她说在这儿跟我碰头。……不会吵着你吧？”
“吵！你是我朋友里最叫人安静的一个——”我见着她那么高兴，连自己都吃惊。她在近旁坐下，非常安静，一举一动像往常一样轻柔而稳当。
我看得出，我的病容令她难过，可她一句烦人的话也不问，也不说我这么住着是发疯（这话总是气人，仿佛我是个傻孩子），反倒夸这屋子，赞那幅贝努瓦的画，然后才用她那柔和敦厚的声音说话。
“我和尼娜可想你了，”她说，“我跟尼娜说，今晚她来不许吵你。”
“她爱怎么吵就怎么吵，”我说，“我喜欢对路子的吵闹。”
我们稍稍谈了谈政治、英国，想到什么谈什么。我知道，我们两个都感到一种愉快、松弛的亲密、友好和信任。同任何别的女人在一起，我都不曾有过这样的友谊感，一种近乎男性之间的同志情谊和坦荡。今晚我们之间的这条纽带更是奇妙地加固了。我为自己的运气祝福。我看见她眼下有黑影，脸色苍白。
“你累了。”我说。
“是，累了，”她承认，“又不知道为什么。不，我知道。我要自私地用你了，迪尔多斯。我要把我所有的苦恼都讲给你，事事都要你帮忙。一点也不放过你。”
我握住她的手。
“我引以为荣，”我说，“现在，永远。”
“你可知道，我从没求过人帮忙？从前颇有点自负，觉得一个人总过得下去。我很小的时候，谁的一句劝都不听，爸妈那时大事小事都来问我。后来他们死了，我只得一个人走下去。……再后来嫁了尼古拉，还是我样样拿主意。”

===Original===

And my mistakes taught me nothing. I didn’t want them to teach me.”
She spoke that last word fiercely, and through the note that came into her voice I saw suddenly the potentialities that were in her, the other creature that she might be if she were ever awakened.
She talked then for a long time. She didn’t move at all; her head rested on her hand and her eyes watched me. As I listened I thought of my other friend Marie, who now was dead, and how restless she was when she spoke, moving about the room, stopping to demand my approval, protesting against my criticism, laughing, crying out.﻿ ﻿… Vera was so still, so wise, too, in comparison with Marie, braver too﻿—and yet the same heart, the same charity, the same nobility.
But she was my friend, and Marie I had loved.﻿ ﻿… The difference in that! And how much easier now to help than it had been then, simply because one’s own soul *was* one’s own and one stood by oneself!
How happy a thing freedom is﻿—and how lonely!
She told me many things that I need not repeat here, but, as she talked, I saw how, far more deeply than I had imagined, Nina had been the heart of the whole of her life. She had watched over her, protected her, advised her, warned her, and loved her, passionately, jealously, almost madly all the time.
“When I married Nicholas,” she said, “I thought of Nina more than anyone else. That was wrong.﻿ ﻿… I ought to have thought most of Nicholas; but I knew that I could give her a home, that she could have everything she wanted. And still she would be with me. Nicholas was only too ready for that. I thought I would care for her until someone came who was worthy of her, and who would look after her far better than I ever could.
“But the only person who had come was Boris Grogoff. He loved Nina from the first moment, in his own careless, conceited, opinionated way.”
“Why did you let him come so often to the house if you didn’t approve of him?” I asked.
“How could I prevent it?” she asked me. “We Russians are not like the English. In England I know you just shut the door and say, ‘Not at home.’
“Here if anyone wanted to come he comes. Very often we hate him for coming, but still there it is. It is too much trouble to turn him out, besides it wouldn’t be kind﻿—and anyway they wouldn’t go. You can be as rude as you like here and nobody cares. For a long while Nina paid no attention to Boris.

===Chinese===

“我的错也没教会我什么。我也不想叫它们教我。”
最后一个词她说得激烈，从她声音里的这个调子，我忽然看见了她身上的种种可能，看见她一旦被唤醒会成为怎样的另一种造物。
随后她讲了很久。她一动不动；头枕在手上，眼睛望着我。我听着，想起我另一位朋友玛丽——她如今死了——想起她说话时多么不安生，在屋里走来走去，停下来要我称许，抗议我的批评，笑，喊。……相形之下薇拉多么安静，也多么明智，也更有勇气——然而同一颗心，同样的仁慈，同样的高贵。
可她是我的朋友，而玛丽，是我爱过的人。……这其间的分别！如今帮忙又比当年容易了多少，只因为一个人的灵魂如今是自己的，一个人独立站着！
自由是件多么好的东西——又是多么孤单！
她讲了许多事，这里不必重复，可她讲着讲着，我看出来，尼娜远比我想象的更深地是她整个生命的中心。她守护她，保护她，劝她，警告她，爱她——热烈地、带着妒意地、几乎疯狂地，一直如此。
“嫁给尼古拉的时候，”她说，“我想得最多的是尼娜。这是错的。……我本该最想尼古拉；可我知道我能给尼娜一个家，她要什么有什么，而且她还能同我在一起。尼古拉对此求之不得。我想，我要照看她，直到有配得上她的人来，那个人会照应得远比我好。
“可来了的唯一一个人，是鲍里斯·格罗戈夫。他从第一眼就爱尼娜，用他自己那种漫不经心、自负武断的方式爱着。”
“你既然看不上他，怎么还让他那样常来家里？”我问。
“我拦得住吗？”她反问我，“我们俄国人不像你们英国人。在英国，我知道，你们只消关上门，说一句‘不在家’。
“在这儿，谁想来他就来。我们常常恨他来，可他就是来了。赶他出去太费事，何况也不厚道——反正他们也不走。在这儿你爱多粗鲁就多粗鲁，没人在乎。有很长一段时间，尼娜根本不理鲍里斯。

===Original===

She doesn’t like him. She will never like him, I’m sure. But now, these last weeks, I’ve begun to be afraid. In some way, he has power over her﻿—not much power, but a little﻿—and she is so young, so ignorant﻿—she knows nothing.
“Until lately she always told me everything. Now she tells me nothing. She’s strange with me; angry for nothing. Then sorry and sweet again﻿—then suddenly angry.﻿ ﻿… She’s excited and wild, going out all the time, but unhappy too.﻿ ﻿… I *know* she’s unhappy. I can feel it as though it were myself.”
“You’re imagining things,” I said. “Now when the war’s reached this period we’re all nervous and overstrung. The atmosphere of this town is enough to make anyone fancy that they see anything. Nina’s all right.”
“I’m losing her! I’m losing her!” Vera cried, suddenly stretching out her hand as though in a gesture of appeal. “She must stay with me. I don’t know what’s happening to her. Ah, and I’m so lonely without her!”
There was silence between us for a little, and then she went on.
“Durdles, I did wrong to marry Nicholas﻿—wrong to Nina, wrong to Nicholas, wrong to myself, I thought it was right. I didn’t love Nicholas﻿—I never loved him and I never pretended to. He knew that I did not. But I thought then that I was above love, that knowledge was what mattered. Ideas﻿—saving the world﻿—and he had *such* ideas! Wonderful! There was, I thought, nothing that he would not be able to do if only he were helped enough. He wanted help in every way. He was such a child, so unhappy, so lonely, I thought that I could give him everything that he needed. Don’t fancy that I thought that I sacrificed myself. I felt that I was the luckiest girl in all the world﻿—and still, now when I see that he is not strong enough for his ideas I care for him as I did then, and I would never let any trouble touch him if I could help it. But if﻿—if﻿—”
She paused, turned away from me, looking towards the window.
“If, after all, I was wrong. If, after all, I was meant to love. If love were to come now﻿ ﻿… real love﻿ ﻿… now.﻿ ﻿…”
She broke off, suddenly stood up, and very low, almost whispering, said:
“I have fancied lately that it might come. And then, what should I do? Oh, what should I do? With Nicholas and Nina and all the trouble there is now in the world﻿—and Russia﻿—I’m afraid of myself﻿—and ashamed.﻿ ﻿…”
I could not speak. I was utterly astonished.

===Chinese===

“她不喜欢他。她永远不会喜欢他，我确信。可如今，最近这几个星期，我开始怕了。不知怎么，他对她有了权力——不大的权力，可有一点——而她又那么年轻，那么无知——她什么都不懂。
“到不久前，她什么事都告诉我。如今她什么都不说了。她对我生分；无缘无故地生气。过后又后悔，又甜蜜——忽然又恼。……她兴奋，撒野，一天到晚往外跑，可又不快活。……我知道她不快活。我感觉得到，就像是我自己不快活。”
“你多虑了，”我说，“仗打到这个节骨眼上，人人都神经紧张。这座城的空气足以叫任何人疑神见鬼。尼娜没事。”
“我要失去她了！我要失去她了！”薇拉喊起来，忽然伸出手，像求告的姿势。“她必须留在我身边。我不知道她出了什么事。啊，没有她我多么孤单！”
我们之间静了一会儿，她又接着说下去。
“迪尔多斯，我嫁给尼古拉是错的——对不起尼娜，对不起尼古拉，也对不起我自己。我当时以为是对的。我不爱尼古拉——从没爱过，也从不假装爱。他知道我不爱。可我那时以为自己超乎爱情之上，以为要紧的是见识。理想——拯救世界——而他有那样的理想！了不起的理想！我当时想，只要扶他扶得够，没有他做不成的事。他样样要人扶。他又那么孩子气，那么不快活，那么孤单，我想我能给他需要的一切。别以为我觉得自己牺牲了什么。我那时觉得自己是天下最幸运的姑娘——如今也是，尽管我看出他撑不起他的那些理想，我还是像当年那样在乎他，只要我做得到，绝不让一点烦恼碰他。可要是——要是——”
她停住，从我身边转开脸，望着窗子那边。
“要是，到头来我错了呢。要是，到头来我天生是为了爱的呢。要是爱如今来了……真正的爱……如今。……”
她薯地收住，忽然站起来，声音低得几乎耳语：
“我近来常常觉得它也许会来。那时候，我怎么办？哦，我怎么办？有尼古拉，有尼娜，有如今世上的这一切乱子——还有俄国——我怕我自己——也羞愧。……”
我说不出话。我惊呆了。

===Original===

Could it be Bohun of whom she was speaking? No, I saw at once that the idea was ludicrous. But if not﻿—.
I took her hand.
“Vera,” I said. “Believe me. I’m much older than you, and I know. Love’s always selfish, always cruel to others, always means trouble, sorrow, and disappointment. But it’s worth it, even when it brings complete disaster. Life isn’t life without it.”
I felt her hand tremble in mine.
“I don’t know,” she said, “I know nothing of it, except my love for Nina. It isn’t that now there’s anybody. Don’t think that. There is no one﻿—no one. Only my self-confidence is gone. I can’t see clearly any more. My duty is to Nina and Nicholas. And if they are happy nothing else matters﻿—nothing. And I’m afraid that I’m going to do them harm.”
She paused as though she were listening. “There’s no one there, is there?” she asked me﻿—“there by the door?”
“No﻿—no one.”
“There are so many noises in this house. Don’t they disturb you?”
“I don’t think of them now. I’m used to them﻿—and in fact I like them.”
She went on: “It’s Uncle Alexei of course. He comes to see us nearly every day. He’s very pleasant, more pleasant than he has ever been before, but he has a dreadful effect on Nicholas﻿—”
“I know the effect he can have,” I said.
“I know that Nicholas has been feeling for a long time that his inventions are no use. He will never own it to me or to anyone﻿—but I can tell. I know it so well. The war came and his new feeling about Russia carried him along. He put everything into that. Now that has failed him, and he despises himself for having expected it to do otherwise. He’s raging about, trying to find something that he can believe in, and Uncle Alexei knows that and plays on that.﻿ ﻿… He teases him; he drives him wild and then makes him happy again. He can do anything with him he pleases. He always could. But now he has some plan. I used to think that he simply laughed at people because it amused him to see how weak they can be. But now there’s more than that. He’s been hurt himself at last, and that has hurt his pride, and he wants to hurt back.﻿ ﻿… It’s all in the dark. The war’s in the dark﻿ ﻿… everything.﻿ ﻿…” Then she smiled and put her hand on my arm. “That’s why I’ve come to you, because I trust you and believe you and know you say what you mean.”
Once before Marie had said those same words to me. It was as though I heard her voice again.

===Chinese===

她说的是博亨吗？不，我立刻看出这念头荒唐。可若不是——。
我握住她的手。
“薇拉，”我说，“相信我。我比你年长得多，我懂。爱情永远是自私的，永远伤及旁人，永远意味着麻烦、愁苦和失望。可它值得，哪怕它带来彻底的灾难。没有它，人生不成其为人生。”
我觉出她的手在我手里发抖。
“我不知道，”她说，“我对此一无所知，除了我对尼娜的爱。并不是如今有了什么人。别那么想。没有人——没有人。只是我的自信没了。我再也看不清了。我的责任是对尼娜和尼古拉。只要他们快活，别的都不算什么——什么都不算。可我怕我会害了他们。”
她顿住，仿佛在听。“那儿没有人吧？”她问我——“门边上？”
“没有——没人。”
“这屋子里响动真多。不吵你吗？”
“如今不觉得了。习惯了——说实在的，还挺喜欢。”
她接着说：“当然是阿列克谢舅舅。他差不多天天来看我们。他很和气，比从前任何时候都和气，可他对尼古拉的影响可怕极了——”
“我知道他能有什么影响。”我说。
“我知道，尼古拉长久以来就觉得他的发明没用。他对我、对谁都不会承认——可我看得出来。我看得太清楚了。战争来了，他对俄国的那份新感情裹挟着他往前走。他把一切都投进去了。如今那也破灭了，他又瞧不起自己，怪自己当初不该指望别的。他到处暴怒地转，想找个能信的东西，阿列克谢舅舅知道这一点，就拨着这一点。……他逗他；把他逼得发疯，再把他哄高兴。他要他怎样他就能怎样。一向如此。可如今他有了什么盘算。我从前以为他捉弄人，不过是看着人能软弱到什么地步觉得好玩。如今不止了。他自己终于受了伤，伤了自尊，他要还回去。……全在黑暗里。战争在黑暗里……一切。……”随后她笑了笑，手搭上我的胳膊。“所以我来找你，因为我信你，信得过你，知道你心里怎么想嘴上就怎么说。”
从前玛丽也对我说过一模一样的话。那仿佛是她的声音又在我耳边响起。

===Original===

“I won’t fail you,” I said.
There was a knock on the door, it was flung open as though by the wind, and Nina was with us. Her face was rosy with the cold, her eyes laughed under her little round fur cap. She came running across the room, pulled herself up with a little cry beside the bed, and then flung herself upon me, throwing her arms around my neck and kissing me.
“My dear Nina!” cried Vera.
She looked up, laughing.
“Why not? Poor Durdles. Are you better? *Biédnie*﻿ ﻿… give me your hands. But﻿—how cold they are! And there are draughts everywhere. I’ve brought you some chocolates﻿—and a book.”
“My dear!﻿ ﻿…” Vera cried again. “He won’t like *that*,” pointing to a work of fiction by a modern Russian literary lady whose heart and brain are of the succulent variety.
“Why not? She’s very good. It’s lovely! All about impossible people! Durdles, *dear*! I’ll give up the party. We won’t go. We’ll sit here and entertain you. I’ll send Boris away. We’ll tell him we don’t want him.”
“Boris!” cried Vera.
“Yes,” Nina laughed a little uneasily, I thought. “I know you said he wasn’t to come. He’ll quarrel with Rozanov of course. But he said he would. And so how was one to prevent him? You’re always so tiresome, Vera.﻿ ﻿… I’m not a baby now, nor is Boris. If he wants to come he shall come.”
Vera stood away from us both. I could see that she was very angry. I had never seen her angry before.
“You know that it’s impossible, Nina,” she said. “You know that Rozanov hates him. And besides﻿—there are other reasons. You know them perfectly well, Nina.”
Nina stood there pouting, tears were in her eyes.
“You’re unfair,” she said. “You don’t let me do anything. You give me no freedom, I don’t care for Boris, but if he wants to go he shall go. I’m grown up now. You have your Lawrence. Let me have my Boris.”
“My Lawrence?” asked Vera.
“Yes. You know that you’re always wanting him to come﻿—always looking for him. I like him, too. I like him very much. But you never let me talk to him. You never﻿—”
“Quiet, Nina.” Vera’s voice was trembling. Her face was sterner than I’d ever seen it. “You’re making me angry.”
“I don’t care how angry I make you. It’s true. You’re impossible now. Why shouldn’t I have my friends? I’ve nobody now. You never let me have anybody. And I like Mr. Lawrence﻿—”
She began to sob, looking the most desolate figure.
Vera turned.

===Chinese===

“我不会辜负你。”我说。
门上响起敲击声，门仿佛被风一下子掀开，尼娜到了。她的脸冻得红扑扑，圆圆的小皮帽底下眼睛里全是笑。她一阵风跑过屋子，在床边喊了一声收住脚，随即扑到我身上，搂住我的脖子亲我。
“我亲爱的尼娜！”薇拉喊。
她抬起头，笑着。
“怎么不行？可怜的迪尔多斯。你好些了吗？Biédnie……把手给我。呀——手多凉！这屋里到处是穿堂风。我给你带了巧克力——还有一本书。”
“亲爱的！……”薇拉又喊了一声，“他不会喜欢那个的。”她指的是当代俄国某位文学女士的一部小说，那位女士的心和脑都是多汁品种的。
“怎么不喜欢？她写得好。美极了！全是些不可能的人！迪尔多斯，亲爱的！聚会我不去了。咱们不去。咱们就坐这儿陪你玩儿。我把鲍里斯打发走。告诉他说我们不要他。”
“鲍里斯！”薇拉喊。
“是。”尼娜笑起来，我觉得笑得有点不自然。“我知道你说过不许他来。他准要跟罗扎诺夫吵架。可他非要来。叫我怎么拦？你总是这么烦人，薇拉。……我如今不是小娃娃了，鲍里斯也不是。他想来就来。”
薇拉从我们两人身边退开。我看得出她非常生气。我从没见她生过气。
“你知道那不可能，尼娜，”她说，“你知道罗扎诺夫恨他。再说——还有别的原因。你心里完全清楚，尼娜。”
尼娜站在那儿看1着嘴，眼里含着泪。
“你不公平，”她说，“什么都不让我做。一点自由不给我。我又不在乎鲍里斯，可他想去就去。我长大了。你有你的劳伦斯。也让我有我的鲍里斯。”
“我的劳伦斯？”薇拉问。
“是。你明知道你老想他来——老张望他。我也喜欢他。我非常喜欢他。可你从不让我跟他说话。你从不——”
“住口，尼娜。”薇拉的声音在抖。她的脸比我见过的任何时候都严厉。“你要惹我生气了。”
“惹就惹。是真的。你现在真没法处。我凭什么不能有我的朋友？我如今谁都没有。你从不让我有任何人。我喜欢劳伦斯先生——”
她哭起来，一副天下最凄惶的样子。
薇拉转过身。

===Original===

“You don’t know what you’ve said, Nina, nor how you’ve hurt.﻿ ﻿… You can go to your party as you please﻿—”
And before I could stop her she was gone.
Nina turned to me a breathless, tearful face. She waited; we heard the door below closed.
“Oh, Durdles, what have I done?”
“Go after her! Stop her!” I said.
Nina vanished and I was alone. My room was intensely quiet.

===Chinese===

“你不知道自己说了什么，尼娜，也不知道伤了人。……你爱去聚会就去吧——”
我还没来得及拦她，她人已经走了。
尼娜转向我，一张挂满泪水、喘息的脸。她等着；我们听见楼下门关上了。
“哦，迪尔多斯，我干了什么呀？”
“追去！拦住她！”我说。
尼娜一溜烟没了，只剩我一个人。我的房间静极了。

### XVII / XVII
===Original===

They didn’t come to see me again together. Vera came twice, kind and good as always, but with no more confidences; and Nina once with flowers and fruit and a wild chattering tongue about the cinemas and Smyrnov, who was delighting the world at the Narodny Dom, and the wonderful performance of Lermontov’s *Masquerade* that was shortly to take place at the Alexander Theatre.
“Are you and Vera friends again?” I asked her.
“Oh yes! Why not?” And she went on, snapping a chocolate almond between her teeth﻿—“The one at the Piccadilly is the best. It’s an Italian one, and there’s a giant in it who throws people all over the place, out of windows and everywhere. Ah! how lovely!﻿ ﻿… I wish I could go every night.”
“You ought to be helping with the war,” I said severely.
“Oh, I hate the war!” she answered. “We’re all terribly tired of it. Tanya’s given up going to the English hospital now, and is just meaning to be as gay as she can be; and Zinaida Fyodorovna had just come back from her Otriad on the Galician front, and she says it’s shocking there now﻿—no food or dancing or anything. Why doesn’t everyone make peace?”
“Do you want the Germans to rule Russia?” I asked.
“Why not?” she said, laughing. “We can’t do it ourselves. We don’t care who does it. The English can do it if they like, only they’re too lazy to bother. The Germans aren’t lazy, and if they were here we’d have lots of theatres and cinematographs.”
“Don’t you love your country?” I asked.
“This isn’t our country,” she answered. “It just belongs to the Empress and Protopopoff.”
“Supposing it became your country and the Emperor went?”
“Oh, then it would belong to a million different people, and in the end no one would have anything. Can’t you see how they’d fight?”﻿ ﻿… She burst out laughing: “Boris and Nicholas and Uncle Alexei and all the others!”
Then she was suddenly serious.
“I know, Durdles, you consider that I’m so young and frivolous that I don’t think of anything serious. But I can see things like anyone else. Can’t you see that we’re all so disappointed with ourselves that nothing matters? We thought the war was going to be so fine﻿—but now it’s just like the Japanese one, all robbery and lies﻿—and we can’t do anything to stop it.”
“Perhaps some day someone will,” I said.
“Oh yes!” she answered scornfully, “men like Boris.”

===Chinese===

她们没有再一起来看我。薇拉来过两回，还像往常一样善良亲切，却不再吐露心事；妮娜来过一回，带着花和水果，一张嘴叽叽喳喳说个没完，说电影，说正在人民宫倾倒众人的斯米尔诺夫，说不久要在亚历山大剧院上演的莱蒙托夫名剧《假面舞会》，那该多精彩。
“你同薇拉和好了？”我问她。
“和好啦！干吗不和好？”她说着，牙齿呀嘣咬开一颗巧克力杏仁——“皮卡迪利那家最好。是意大利片，里头有个巨人，把人扔得满天飞，从窗户里往外扔，哪儿都扔。啊！真好看！……恨不得天天晚上去。”
“你该去为战争出把力。”我严肃地说。
“嗐，我恨透了这场战争！”她答道。“我们全都烦得要死。唐妮现在不去英国医院了，一心只想怎么快活怎么来；济娜伊达·费奥多罗夫娜刚从加利西亚前线她的支队回来，说那边如今糟透了——没吃的，没舞跳，什么都没有。大家干吗不媾和？”
“你想让德国人来统治俄国？”我问。
“干吗不？”她笑着说。“我们自己反正义不了。谁爱义谁义，我们无所谓。英国人想义也行，只是他们懒得费那个神。德国人不懒，他们要是来了，我们就有好多戏院、好多电影院。”
“你不爱你的国家？”我问。
“这又不是我们的国家，”她答。“这是皇后和普罗托波波夫的。”
“假如它变成你的国家，皇帝走了呢？”
“嗐，那它就归一百万人分，到头来谁也落不着什么。你没看见他们会怎么打吗？”……她放声大笑：“鲍里斯、尼古拉斯、阿列克谢舅舅，还有所有的那些人！”
随后她忽然正色起来。
“我知道，杜杜，你们觉得我又年轻又轻浮，什么都不往心里去。可我跟别人一样看得见。你难道看不见吗？我们对自己都失望透了，什么都没什么要紧了。我们原以为这场战争会多么了不起——可如今它就跟日本人那场一样，全是抢和谎——我们又拦不住它。”
“也许有一天有人拦得住。”我说。
“哦，是啊！”她轻蔑地答，“鲍里斯那号人呗。”

===Original===

After that she refused to be grave for a moment, danced about the room, singing, and finally vanished, a whirlwind of blue silk.
---
A week later I was out in the world again. That curious sense of excitement that had first come to me during the early days of my illness burnt now more fiercely than ever. I cannot say what it was exactly that I thought was going to happen. I have often looked back, as many other people must have done, to those days in February and wondered whether I foresaw anything of what was to come, and what were the things that might have seemed to me significant if I had noticed them. And here I am deliberately speaking of both public and private affairs. I cannot quite frankly dissever the two. At the Front, a year and a half before, I had discovered how intermingled the souls of individuals and the souls of countries were, and how permanent private history seemed to me and how transient public events; but whether that was true or no before, it was now most certain that it was the story of certain individuals that I was to record﻿—the history that was being made behind them could at its best be only a background.
I seemed to step into a city ablaze with a sinister glory. If that appears melodramatic I can only say that the dazzling winter weather of those weeks was melodramatic. Never before had I seen the huge buildings tower so high, never before felt the shadows so vast, the squares and streets so limitless in their capacity for swallowing light and colour. The sky was a bitter changeless blue; the buildings black; the snow and ice, glittering with purple and gold, swept by vast swinging shadows as though huge doors opened and shut in heaven, or monstrous birds hovered, their wings spread, motionless in the limitless space.
And all this had, as ever, nothing to do with human life. The little courtyards with their woodstacks and their coloured houses, carts and the cobbled squares and the little stumpy trees that bordered the canals and the little wooden huts beside the bridges with their candles and fruit﻿—these were human and friendly and good, but they had their precarious condition like the rest of us.
On the first afternoon of my new liberty I found myself in the Nevski Prospect, bewildered by the crowds and the talk and trams and motors and carts that passed in unending sequence up and down the long street.

===Chinese===

此后她一分钟也不肯正经了，唱着歌在屋里团团转，最后化作一阵蓝绸子的旋风，一卷而去。
---
一星期后，我又重新走到了外面的世界。那股古怪的兴奋劲——起初生病头几天就有了的——如今烧得比什么时候都旺。我说不清自己究竟以为要发生什么。我常常回想——想必许多人也这样——回想二月的那些日子，琢潣我当时是否预感到了后来的事，又有哪些征兆，倘若我留意，本会觉得非同小可。在这里，我是故意把公事和私事放在一起说的。老实说，我没法把两者剖开。一年半以前在前线，我就发现个人的灵魂同国家的灵魂交织得多么深，也发现私人的历史在我看来多么恒久，而公共的事件多么仓促；但不管从前是否如此，如今最确定不过的是：我要记下的，是几个人的故事——在她们背后展开的历史，充其量不过是一幅背景。
我仿佛一步跨进了一座燃着不祥光焰的城。这话若嫌夸张，我只能说，那几个星期耀眼的冬日天气，本来就是夸张的。我从未见过那些巨厦高高耸立到这般地步，从未感到影子如此浩大，广场和街道吞起光和色彩来，那样无边无际。天空是一种冷冽不变的蓝；楼宇是黑的；冰雪闪着紫与金，被大团大团摆荡的影子扫过，仿佛天上有巨大的门开了又合，又仿佛有怪鸟展着翅，一动不动地悬在无边的太空里。
而这一切，一如既往，同人的生活毫不相干。那些小院子，柴垛，彩色的房子，大车，铺鹅卵石的广场，运河边矮墩墩的小树，桥头小木屋里的蜡烛和水果——这些才是人的、亲切的、善良的，可它们也同我们大家一样，命悬一线。
重获自由头一天下午，我发觉自己站在涅夫斯基大街上，被人流、人语、电车、汽车和大车搅得眼花缭乱，它们一浪接一浪，在长长的街道上来来去去，没有个完。

===Original===

Standing at the corner of the Sadovia and the Nevski one was carried straight to the point of the golden spire that guarded the farther end of the great street. All was gold, the surface of the road was like a golden stream, the canal was gold, the thin spire caught into its piercing line all the colour of the swiftly fading afternoon; the wheels of the carriages gleamed, the flower-baskets of the women glittered like shining foam, the snow flung its crystal colour into the air like thin fire dim before the sun. The street seemed to have gathered on to its pavements the citizens of every country under the sun. Tartars, Mongols, Little Russians, Chinamen, Japanese, French officers, British officers, peasants and fashionable women, schoolboys, officials, actors and artists and business men and priests and sailors and beggars and hawkers and, guarding them all, friendly, urbane, filled with a pleasant self-importance that seemed at that hour the simplest and easiest of attitudes, the police. *Rum﻿—rum﻿—rum﻿—whirr﻿—whirr﻿—whirr﻿—whirr*﻿—like the regular beat of a shuttle the hum rose and fell, as the sun faded into rosy mist and white vapours stole above the still canals.
I turned to go home and felt someone touch my elbow.
I swung round and there, his broad face ruddy with the cold, was Jerry Lawrence.
I was delighted to see him and told him so.
“Well, I’m damned glad,” he said gruffly. “I thought you might have a grudge against me.”
“A grudge?” I said. “Why?”
“Haven’t been to see you. Heard you were ill, but didn’t think you’d want me hanging round.”
“Why this modesty?” I asked.
“No﻿—well﻿—you know what I mean.” He shuffled his feet. “No good in a sickroom.”
“Mine wasn’t exactly a sickroom,” I said. “But I heard that you did come.”
“Yes. I came twice,” he answered, looking at me shyly. “Your old woman wouldn’t let me see you.”
“Never mind that,” I said; “let’s have an evening together soon.”
“Yes﻿—as soon as you like.” He looked up and down the street. “There are some things I’d like to ask your advice about.”
“Certainly,” I said.
“What do you say to coming and dining at my place? Ever met Wilderling?”
“Wilderling?” I could not remember for the moment the name.
“Yes﻿—the old josser I live with. Fine old man﻿—got a point of view of his own!”
“Delighted,” I said.
“Tomorrow. Eight o’clock. Don’t dress.”
He was just going off when he turned again.

===Chinese===

站在萨多瓦街和涅夫斯基大街的街角，一眼便被引到那金色的尖塔顶上——它守着这条大街的尽头。满眼是金：路面像一条金河，运河是金的，细细的尖塔用它刺破天际的一线，收尽了午后迅速消隐的全部颜色；马车的轮辐闪闪，女人臂上的花篮像发亮的泡沫，冰雪把它的晶莹泼向空中，像太阳前面黯淡的薄火。这条街仿佛把天底下所有国家的公民都收拢到了它的人行道上。鞑靼人、蒙古人、小俄罗斯人、中国人、日本人、法国军官、英国军官、庄稼汉、摩登女子、中小学生、官僚、演员、画家、生意人、神甫、水兵、乞丐、小贩，还有照看这一切的警察们——和和气气，彬彬有礼，满心一种愉快的自命不凡，在那个时辰，这似乎是最简单最省事的态度。嗡——嗡——嗡——呼——呼——呼——嗡嗡的人声像梭子一样规律地起落，太阳淡进玫瑰色的雾里，白茫茫的水汽悄悄爬上静止的运河。
我转身要回家，觉得有人碰了碰我的胳膊肘。
我一回身，是杰里·劳伦斯，宽宽的脸膛冻得通红。
我见到他很高兴，也这么对他说了。
“嗬，我他妈的真高兴。”他粗声粗气地说。“还当你记我的仇呢。”
“记仇？”我说。“为什么？”
“没去看你呀。听说你病了，可想着你未必愿意我在旁边转悠。”
“怎么忽然谦逊起来了？”我问。
“没——呃——你懂的。”他两脚蹭来蹭去。“病房里有我用不着的地方。”
“我那屋子倒也算不得病房，”我说。“不过我听说你来过。”
“来过。来了两回。”他答，羞怯地望着我。“你家老太婆不让我见你。”
“那不碍事，”我说。“哪天咱们找个晚上聚聚。”
“好——随你什么时候。”他朝街两头望了望。“有些事我想讨讨你的主意。”
“没问题。”我说。
“上我那儿吃顿晚饭怎么样？见过维尔德林没有？”
“维尔德林？”我一时想不起这个名字。
“对——跟我同住的那个老家伙。好老头——自有他一套看法！”
“荣幸之至。”我说。
“明晚。八点。不用穿礼服。”
他刚要走，又回过头来。

===Original===

“Awfully glad you’re better,” he said. He cleared his throat, looked at me in a very friendly way, then smiled.
“*Awfully* glad you’re better,” he repeated, then went off, rolling his broad figure into the evening mist.
I turned towards home.

===Chinese===

“你好了，真他妈高兴。”他说。他清了清嗓子，非常友好地看了我一眼，然后笑了。
“真高兴你好了。”他又说了一遍，随后迈开步子，那宽阔的身躯滚进暮霭里去了。
我转身朝家走去。

### XVIII / 十八
===Original===

I arrived at the Baron’s punctually at eight o’clock. His flat was in a small side street off the English Quay. I paused for a moment, before turning into its dark recesses, to gather in the vast expanse of the frozen river and the long white quay. It was as though I had found my way behind a towering wall that now closed me in with a smile of contemptuous derision. There was no sound in the shining air and the only figure was a guard who moved monotonously up and down outside the Winter Palace.
I rang the bell and the “Schwitzer,” bowing very ceremoniously, told me the flat was on the second floor. I went up a broad stone staircase and found a heavy oak door with brass nails confronting me. When this slowly swung open I discovered a very old man with white hair bowing before me. He was a splendid figure in a uniform of dark blue, his tall thin figure straight and slim, his white moustaches so neat and fierce that they seemed to keep guard over the rest of his face as though they warned him that they would stand no nonsense. There was an air of hushed splendour behind him, and I could hear the heavy, solemn ticking of a clock keeping guard over all the austere sanctities of the place. When I had taken off my *shuba* and goloshes I was ushered into a magnificent room with a high gold clock on the mantelpiece, gilt chairs, heavy dark carpets and large portraits frowning from the grey walls. The whole room was bitterly silent, save for the tick of the clock. There was no fire in the fireplace, but a large gleaming white stove flung out a close scented heat from the further corner of the room. There were two long glass bookcases, some little tables with gilt legs, and a fine Japanese screen of dull gold. The only other piece of furniture was a huge grand piano near the window.
I sat down and was instantly caught into the solemn silence. There was something threatening in the hush of it all. “We do what we’re told,” the clock seemed to say, “and so must you.” I thought of the ice and snow beyond the windows, and, in spite of myself, shivered.
Then the door opened and the Baron came in. He stood for a moment by the door, staring in front of him as though he could not penetrate the heavy and dusky air, and seen thus, under the height and space of the room, he seemed so small as to be almost ridiculous. But he was not ridiculous for long.

===Chinese===

八点整，我准时到了男爵府上。他的公寓在离英国堤岸不远的一条小侧街上。拐进那黑黢黢的巷口之前，我停了片刻，把结冻的大河和长长的白色堤岸尽收眼底。那感觉仿佛我摸到了一道高墙背后，如今这墙带着轻蔑的冷笑把我关了进来。清亮的空气里没有一点声音，唯一的一个人影，是冬宫外一个卫兵，单调地来回走动。
我按了铃，那位“门房”（Schwitzer）礼数周全地躬身告诉我，公寓在二楼。我登上宽阔的石阶，面前是一扇钉着铜钉的厚橡木门。门缓缓荡开，一位白发老人向我躬身。他身穿深蓝制服，仪表堂堂，瘦高的身形挺直颅长，两撮白胡子齐整而威风，仿佛守卫着他脸上的其余部分，警告人休得放肆。他身后是一片静穆的富丽，一座时钟沉重而庄严的滴答声守卫着这地方的一切森严的圣物。我脱下皮大衣（shuba）和套鞋，被引进一间气派的屋子：壁炉架上立着高高的金色时钟，镀金椅子，厚重深色的地毯，灰色墙上大幅肖像板着脸。满屋死寂，只有钟的滴答。壁炉里没有火，一台雪亮的大白炉从屋子深处的角落里散出一股紧裹着的、带香气的热。还有两只长玻璃书柜，几张细腿镀金的小桌，一架精美的暗金色日本屏风。屋里最后一件家具，是窗边一台大三角钢琴。
我坐下，立刻被这片庄严的寂静收了进去。这寂静里有一种威吓。“我们奉命行事，”那钟仿佛在说，“你也得如此。”我想起窗外封冻的冰雪，不由自主打了个寒噪。
随后门开了，男爵进来。他在门边站了片刻，直望着前方，仿佛穿透不了这浓重昏暗的空气；在这屋子的高阔之下这么看去，他小得近乎可笑。但他没有可笑多久。

===Original===

As he approached one was struck at once by the immaculate efficiency that followed him like a protecting shadow. In himself he was a scrupulously neat old man with weary and dissipated eyes, but behind the weariness, the neatness, and dissipation was a spirit of indomitable determination and resolution. He wore a little white Imperial and a long white moustache. His hair was brushed back and his forehead shone like marble. He wore a black suit, white spats, and long, pointed, black patent-leather shoes. He had the smallest feet I have ever seen on any man.
He greeted me with great courtesy. His voice was soft, and he spoke perfect English, save for a very slight accent that was rather charming; this gave his words a certain naivete. He rubbed his hands and smiled in a gentle but determined way, as though he meant no harm by it, but had decided that it was a necessary thing to do. I forget of what we talked, but I know that I surrendered myself at once to an atmosphere that had been strange to me for so long that I had almost forgotten its character﻿—an atmosphere of discipline, order, comfort, and above all, of security. My mind flew to the Markovitches, and I smiled to myself at the thought of the contrast.
Then, strangely, when I had once thought of the Markovitch flat the picture haunted me for the rest of the evening. I could see the Baron’s gilt chairs and gold clock, his little Imperial and shining shoes only through the cloudy disorder of the Markovitch tables and chairs. There was poor Markovitch in his dark little room perched on his chair with his boots, with his hands, with his hair﻿ ﻿… and there was poor Uncle and there poor Vera.﻿ ﻿… Why was I pitying them? I gloried in them. That is Russia﻿ ﻿… This is.﻿ ﻿…
“Allow me to introduce you to my wife,” the Baron said, bending forward, the very points of his toes expressing amiability.
The Baroness was a large solid lady with a fine white bosom and strong white arms. Her face was homely and kind; I saw at once that she adored her husband; her placid smile carried beneath its placidity a tremulous anxiety that he should be pleased, and her mild eyes swam in the light of his encouragement. I was sure, however, that the calm and discipline that I felt in the things around me came as much from her domesticity as from his discipline.

===Chinese===

他一走近，人就立刻感到一种无懈可击的干练，像护身的影子一样跟着他。他本身是个一丝不苟的整洁老人，一双疲惫而放荡过的眼睛；可在这疲惫、整洁和放荡背后，是一股不屈不挠的决心和果敢。他蓄着一小撮白色唇须，两撇长长的白髭。头发向后梳，前额亮得像大理石。他穿一身黑西服，白鞋罩，尖头的黑漆皮长鞋。我从未在任何男人脚上见过那么小的脚。
他极其客气地招呼我。他嗓音柔和，说一口地道的英语，只带一点点口音，倒还悦耳；这口音给他的话添了几分天真。他搓着手，温和而坚决地微笑，仿佛并无恶意，只是断定这件事非做不可。我们谈了什么我忘了，只知道我立刻向一种气氛投降了——那气氛对我生疏了太久，我几乎忘了它的品性——一种纪律、秩序、舒适，尤其是安全的气氛。我的心飞到马尔科维奇一家，想到那反差，自己暗笑了。
可怪的是，一旦想起马尔科维奇家的公寓，那画面便纠缠了我一晚上。男爵的镀金椅子和金钟，他的小唇须和锃亮的皮鞋，我只能透过马尔科维奇家桌椅的云雾般的凌乱去看。可怜的马尔科维奇，蹲在他黑乎乎的小屋里他那把椅子上，摆弄他的靴子，摆弄他的手，挠他的头发……可怜的舅舅，可怜的薇拉。……我为什么可怜他们？我以他们为荣。那才是俄国……这才是。……
“请允许我向您介绍我的妻子。”男爵欠身说，连他的鞋尖都透着和气。
男爵夫人是位宽大结实的夫人，胸口白净漂亮，胳膊白而有力。她面相家常而和善；我一眼看出她敬爱丈夫；她安详的微笑底下藏着一份颤巍巍的焦虑，唯恐他不痛快，她温和的眼睛泡在他嘉许的光里。不过我确信，我在身周感到的这份宁静和纪律，一半来自她的治家，一半才来自他的治军。

===Original===

She was a fortunate woman in that she had attained the ambition of her life﻿—to govern the household of a man whom she could both love and fear.
Lawrence came in, and we went through high folding doors into the dining-room. This room had dark-blue wallpaper, electric lights heavily shaded, and soft heavy carpets. The table itself was flooded with light﻿—the rest of the room was dusk. I wondered as I looked about me why the Wilderlings had taken Lawrence as a paying guest. Before my visit I had imagined that they were poor, as so many of the better-class Russians were, but here were no signs of poverty. I decided that.
Our dinner was good, and the wine was excellent. We talked, of course, politics, and the Baron was admirably frank.
“I won’t disguise from you, M. Durward,” he said, “that some of us watch your English effort at winning the heart of this country with sympathy, but also, if I am not offending you, with some humour. I’m not speaking only of your propaganda efforts. You’ve got, I know, one or two literary gentlemen here﻿—a novelist, I think, and a professor and a journalist. Well, soon you’ll find them inefficient, and decide that you must have some commercial gentlemen, and then, disappointed with them, you’ll decide for the military﻿ ﻿… and still the great heart of Russia will remain untouched.”
“Yes,” I said, “because your class are determined that the peasant shall remain uneducated, and until he is educated he will be unable to approach any of us.”
“Quite so,” said the Baron smiling at me very cheerfully. “I perceive, M. Durward, that you are a democrat. So are we all, these days.﻿ ﻿… You look surprised, but I assure you that the good of the people in the interests of the people is the only thing for which any of us care. Only some of us know Russia pretty well, and we know that the Russian peasant is not ready for liberty, and if you were to give him liberty tonight you would plunge his country into the most desperate torture of anarchy and carnage known in history. A little more soup?﻿—we are offering you only a slight dinner.”
“Yes, but, Baron,” I said, “would you tell me when it is intended that the Russian peasant shall begin his upward course towards light and learning? If that day is to be forever postponed?”
“It will not be forever postponed,” said the Baron gently.

===Chinese===

她是位有福的女人：她达到了她毕生的抱负——执掌一个她既爱又怕的男人的家。
劳伦斯到了，我们穿过高大的折门进了饭厅。这间屋子糊着深蓝墙纸，电灯罩着厚厚的灯罩，铺着柔软厚重的地毯。唯有餐桌浸在光里——屋子其余处是昏暗的。我环顾四周，心里纳闷维尔德林夫妇为什么收劳伦斯做包膳食宿的房客。来之前我想他们穷——上等俄国人多半如此——可这里毫无贫穷的迹象。
晚饭很好，酒极好。我们谈的自然是政治，男爵坦率得令人佩服。
“我不向您隐瞒，德沃德先生，”他说，“你们英国人努力赢取这个国家的人心，我们当中有人是怀着同情看的，不过——恕我冒犯——也带着几分好笑。我说的不只是你们的宣传。我知道，你们这儿有一两位文人——一位小说家，我想，还有一位教授、一位记者。很快你们就会发现他们不顶事，断定还得来几位商界先生；对他们失望之后，你们又会转向军人……可俄国这颗伟大的心，依旧岿然不动。”
“是啊，”我说，“因为你们那个阶层打定了主意，农民必须继续无知；一天不受教育，他就一天亲近不了我们任何人。”
“正是如此。”男爵喜滋滋地朝我笑，“我看得出，德沃德先生，您是位民主派。如今我们谁不是呢。……您面露惊讶，可我向您保证，为了人民利益的福祉，是我们人人唯一关心的东西。只不过我们中有些人相当了解俄国，我们知道俄国农民还没准备好自由；今晚你若把自由给他，你就会把他的国家推进史上最绝望的无政府和屠杀的酷刑。再来一点汤？——我们只略备了便饭。”
“可是，男爵，”我说，“您能否告诉我，俄国农民哪一天才开始他向光明和学问的上升之路？如果那一天要永远推迟？”
“不会永远推迟。”男爵温言说。

===Original===

“Let us finish the war, and education shall be given slowly, under wise direction, to every man, woman, and child in the country. Our Czar is the most liberal ruler in Europe﻿—and he knows what is good for his children.”
“And Protopopoff and Stürmer?” I asked.
“Protopopoff is a zealous, loyal liberal, but he has been made to see during these last months that Russia is not at this moment ready for freedom. Stürmer﻿—well, M. Stürmer is gone.”
“So you, yourself, Baron,” I asked, “would oppose at this moment all reform?”
“With every drop of blood in my body,” he answered, and his hand flat against the tablecloth quivered. “At this crisis admit one change and your dyke is burst, your land flooded. Every Russian is asked at this moment to believe in simple things﻿—his religion, his Czar, his country. Grant your reforms, and in a week every babbler in the country will be off his head, talking, screaming, fighting. The Germans will occupy Russia at their own good time, you will be beaten on the West and civilisation will be set back two hundred years. The only hope for Russia is unity, and for unity you must have discipline, and for discipline, in Russia at any rate, you must have an autocracy.”
As he spoke the furniture, the grey walls, the heavy carpets, seemed to whisper an echo of his words: “Unity﻿ ﻿… Discipline﻿ ﻿… Discipline﻿ ﻿… Autocracy﻿ ﻿… Autocracy﻿ ﻿… Autocracy.﻿ ﻿…”
“Then tell me, Baron,” I said, “if it isn’t an impertinent question, do you feel so secure in your position that you have no fears at all? Does such a crisis, as for instance Milyukoff’s protest last November, mean nothing? You know the discontent.﻿ ﻿… Is there no fear.﻿ ﻿… ?”
“Fear!” He interrupted me, his voice swift and soft and triumphant. “M. Durward, are you so ignorant of Russia that you consider the outpourings of a few idealistic Intelligentzia, professors and teachers and poets, as important? What about the people, M. Durward? You ask any peasant in the Moscow Government, or little Russia, or the Ukraine whether he will remain loyal to his Little Father or no! Ask﻿—and the question you suggested to me will be answered.”
“Then, you feel both secure and justified?” I said.
“We feel both secure and justified,” he answered me, smiling.
After that our conversation was personal and social. Lawrence was very quiet.

===Chinese===

“打完这场仗，教育将在贤明的指导之下，缓缓地给予这个国家的每一个男人、女人和孩子。我们的沙皇是全欧洲最开明的君主——他知道什么对他的孩子们好。”
“那普罗托波波夫和施图尔默呢？”我问。
“普罗托波波夫是位热忱、忠诚的自由派，只是这几个月他被人点醒：俄国此刻还没准备好自由。施图尔默嘛——这个，施图尔默先生已经去职了。”
“那么您本人，男爵，”我问，“此刻也要反对一切改革了？”
“用我身体里的每一滴血反对。”他答道，平贴在桌布上的手微微发抖。“这当口放进一个变革，你的堤就溃了，你的土地就淹了。此刻要求每个俄国人相信的是最简单的几样东西——他的宗教，他的沙皇，他的祖国。你一给改革，不出一个星期，全国每一个碎嘴子都要发昏，讲话的、尖叫的、打架的。德国人会挑个方便日子占领俄国，你们在西线吃败仗，文明倒退两百年。俄国唯一的希望是统一，要统一就得有纪律，而要有纪律——在俄国无论如何——就得有专制。”
他说话时，家具、灰墙、厚地毯仿佛都在低声应和他的话：“统一……纪律……纪律……专制……专制……专制。……”
“那么告诉我，男爵，”我说，“若不是个冒失的问题——您对您的地位这般有把握，竟没有一丝畏惧？诸如米留可夫去年十一月的抗议那样的危机，就毫无意义？您知道民怨。……就一点不怕……？”
“怕！”他打断我，声音急促、轻柔而得意，“德沃德先生，您对俄国就这么无知，竟把几个理想主义知识界（Intelligentzia）的教授、教员、诗人的高谈阔论当回事？人民呢，德沃德先生？您去问问莫斯科省、小俄罗斯或乌克兰的任何一个农民，他还忠不忠于他的小父亲！去问吧——您向我提的问题自会有答案。”
“那么，你们既安心，又理直气壮？”我说。
“我们既安心，又理直气壮。”他微笑着回答我。
此后我们的谈话就是私人闲话了。劳伦斯很安静。

===Original===

I observed that the Baroness had a motherly affection for him, that she saw that he had everything that he wanted, and that she gave him every now and then little friendly confidential smiles. As the meal proceeded, as I drank the most excellent wine and the warm austerity of my surroundings gathered ever more closely around me, I wondered whether after all my apprehensions and forebodings of the last weeks had not been the merest sick man’s cowardice. Surely if any kingdom in the world was secure, it was this official Russia. I could see it stretching through the space and silence of that vast land, its servants in every village, its paths and roads all leading back to the central citadel, its whispered orders flying through the air from district to district, its judgements, its rewards, its sins, its virtues, resting upon a basis of superstition and ignorance and apathy, the three sure friends of autocracy through history!
And on the other side﻿—who? The Rat, Boris Grogoff, Markovitch. Yes, the Baron had reason for his confidence.﻿ ﻿… I thought for a moment of that figure that I had seen on Christmas Eve by the river﻿—the strong grave bearded peasant whose gaze had seemed to go so far beyond the bounds of my own vision. But no! Russia’s mystical peasant﻿—that was an old tale. Once, on the Front, when I had seen him facing the enemy with bare hands, I had, myself, believed it. Now I thought once more of the Rat﻿—*that* was the type whom I must now confront.
I had a most agreeable evening. I do not know how long it had been since I had tasted luxury and comfort and the true fruits of civilisation. The Baron was a most admirable teller of stories, with a capital sense of humour. After dinner the Baroness left us for half an hour, and the Baron became very pleasantly Rabelaisian, speaking of his experiences in Paris and London, Vienna and Berlin so easily and with so ready a wit that the evening flew. The Baroness returned and, seeing that it was after eleven, I made my farewells. Lawrence said that he would walk with me down the quay before turning into bed. My host and hostess pressed me to come as often as possible. The Baron’s last words to me were:
“Have no fears, M. Durward. There is much talk in this country, but we are a lazy people.”
The “we” rang strangely in my ears.

===Chinese===

我注意到男爵夫人对他有一份慈母般的感情，事事看他什么都不缺，还不时朝他投去友好的、知心的微笑。饭一程一程下去，我喝着极好的酒，身周那温暖的森严越裹越紧，我不禁想：我这几个星期的忧虑和预感，会不会不过是一个病夫的怯懦？倘若世上真有哪个王国固若金汤，那准是这个官家俄国。我看见它铺展在那片广袤土地的空旷与寂静之上，每个村子都有它的仆役，条条路径都通回中央的城堡，口授的低令从一区飞到一区，它的判决、它的赏赐、它的罪、它的德，都安放在迷信、无知和麻木这三块基石上——历史上专制制度的三位铁杆朋友！
而在另一边——是谁？老鼠，鲍里斯·格罗戈夫，马尔科维奇。是的，男爵的自信有他的道理。……我想起圣诞夜在河边见过的那个身影——那个健壮、端凝、蓄须的农民，他的目光仿佛远远越过我目力的边界。可是不！俄国神秘的农民——那是老掉牙的故事了。从前在前线，我亲眼见他赤手空拳面对敌人，我自己也信过。如今我又想起老鼠——那才是我如今必须面对的类型。
我过了一个极痛快的晚上。不知有多少时候没尝过奢侈、舒适和文明的真正果实了。男爵讲故事的本事一流，幽默感绝佳。饭后男爵夫人退了半个钟头，男爵变得很惬意地拉伯雷起来，谈起他在巴黎、伦敦、维也纳、柏林的经历，那样从容，那样妙语如珠，晚上飞也似的过去了。男爵夫人回来，我看已过十一点，起身告辞。劳伦斯说睡前陪我沿堤岸走走。男女主人再三请我常来。男爵送我的最后一句话是：
“不必担心，德沃德先生。这个国家空话很多，可我们是个懒惰的民族。”
那声“我们”，在我耳朵里响得古怪。

===Original===

“He’s of course no more a Russian than you or I,” I said to Lawrence, as we started down the quay.
“Oh yes, he is!” Lawrence said. “Quite genuine﻿—not a drop of German blood in spite of the name. But he’s a Prussian at heart﻿—a Prussian of the Prussians. By that I don’t mean in the least that he wants Germany to win the war. He doesn’t﻿—his interests are all here, and you mayn’t believe me, but I assure you he’s a Patriot. He loves Russia, and he wants what’s best for her﻿—and believes that to be Autocracy.”
After that Lawrence shut up. He would not say another word. We walked for a long time in silence. The evening was most beautiful. A golden moon flung the snow into dazzling relief against the deep black of the palaces. Across the Neva the line of towers and minarets and chimneys ran like a huge fissure in the golden, light from sky to sky.
“You said there was something you wanted to ask my advice about?”
I broke the silence.
He looked at me with his long slow considering stare. He mumbled something; then, with a sudden gesture, he gripped my arm, and his heavy body quivering with the urgency of his words he said:
“It’s Vera Markovitch.﻿ ﻿… I’d give my body and soul and spirit for her happiness and safety.﻿ ﻿… God forgive me, I’d give my country and my honour.﻿ ﻿… I ache and long for her, so that I’m afraid for my sanity. I’ve never loved a woman, nor lusted for one, nor touched one in my whole life, Durward﻿—and now﻿ ﻿… and now﻿ ﻿… I’ve gone right in. I’ve spoken no word to anyone; but I couldn’t stand my own silence.﻿ ﻿… Durward, you’ve got to help me!”
I walked on, seeing the golden light and the curving arc of snow and the little figures moving like dolls from light to shadow. Lawrence! I had never thought of him as an urgent lover; even now, although I could still feel his hand quivering on my arm, I could have laughed at the ludicrous incongruity of romance, and that stolid thickset figure. And at the same time I was afraid. Lawrence in love was no boy on the threshold of life like Bohun﻿ ﻿… here was no trivial passion. I realised even in that first astonished moment the trouble that might be in store for all of us.
“Look here, Lawrence!” I said at last. “The first thing that you may as well realise is that it is hopeless. Vera Michailovna has confided in me a good deal lately, and she is devoted to her husband, thinks of nothing else.

===Chinese===

“他当然和你我一样，算不得俄国人。”我们沿堤岸走去时，我对劳伦斯说。
“哦不，他是！”劳伦斯说，“地道得很——别看那姓氏，一滴德意志血也没有。可他骨子里是个普鲁士人——普鲁士人里的普鲁士人。这绝不是说他盼德国打赢。他不盼——他的利益全在这儿，你也许不信，可我向你保证，他是个爱国者。他爱俄国，想要俄国得到最好的——而他认为那最好的就是专制。”
说完劳伦斯就闭了口，再不肯多说一个字。我们默默地走了很久。夜色美极了。金色的月亮把雪照得炫目，衬着宫殿深黑的墙身。涅瓦河对岸，塔楼、尖顶和烟囱连成一线，像天光之间一道巨大的裂隙。
“你说有件事要请教我的意见？”
我打破了沉默。
他用他那悠长缓慢、掂量人的目光望着我。他咕哝了句什么；随后，一个突兀的手势，他一把攥住我的胳膊，他那沉重的身子因话语的急切而发抖：
“是薇拉·马尔科维奇。……为了她的幸福和平安，我肯交出我的身、我的魂、我的灵。……上帝饶恕我，我肯交出我的祖国和我的名誉。……我渴望她，想她想得发疼，疼得我怕自己失掉理智。我这一辈子没爱过一个女人，没贪过一个女人，没碰过一个，德沃德——可如今……如今……我陷到底了。我对谁也没吐过一个字；可我受不住自己的沉默了。……德沃德，你得帮我！”
我继续往前走，望着金色的光、雪的弧线，小小的人影像木偶一样从光里移进影里。劳伦斯！我从来没把他想成一个火烧火燎的情人；即使此刻，虽然还能觉出他的手在我臂上发抖，我仍可以笑出声来——浪漫，配上那敦实粗壮的身坯，多么不协调的滑稽。可同时我也怕。恋爱中的劳伦斯，不是博亨那种站在人生门槛上的男孩子……这可不是一段无足轻重的情热。就在那吃惊的头一刻，我已明白这会给我们所有人带来怎样的麻烦。
“听我说，劳伦斯！”我终于开口，“头一件，你不妨先明白：没有指望。薇拉·米哈伊洛夫娜近来对我推心置腹，她一心在丈夫身上，别的什么都不想。

===Original===

She’s simple, naive, with all her sense and wisdom.﻿ ﻿…”
“Hopeless!” he interrupted, and he gave a kind of grim chuckle of derision. “My dear Durward, what do you suppose I’m after?﻿ ﻿… rape and adultery and Markovitch after us with a pistol? I tell you﻿—” and here he spoke fiercely, as though he were challenging the whole icebound world around us﻿—“that I want nothing but her happiness, her safety, her comfort! Do you suppose that I’m such an ass as not to recognise the kind of thing that my loving her would lead to? I tell you I’m after nothing for myself, and that not because I’m a fine unselfish character, but simply because the thing’s too big to let anything into it but herself. She shall never know that I care twopence about her, but she’s got to be happy and she’s got to be safe.﻿ ﻿… Just now, she’s neither of those things, and that’s why I’ve spoken to you.﻿ ﻿… She’s unhappy and she’s afraid, and that’s got to change. I wouldn’t have spoken of this to you if I thought you’d be so shortsighted.﻿ ﻿…”
“All right! All right!” I said testily. “You may be a kind of Galahad, Lawrence, outside all natural law. I don’t know, but you’ll forgive me if I go for a moment on my own experience﻿—and that experience is, that you can start on as highbrow an elevation as you like, but love doesn’t stand still, and the body’s the body, and tomorrow isn’t yesterday﻿—not by no means. Moreover, Markovitch is a Russian and a peculiar one at that. Finally, remember that I want Vera Michailovna to be happy quite as much as you do!”
He was suddenly grave and almost boyish in his next words.
“I know that﻿—you’re a decent chap, Durward﻿—I know it’s hard to believe me, but I just ask you to wait and test me. No one knows of this﻿—that I’d swear﻿—and no one shall; but what’s the matter with her, Durward, what’s she afraid of? That’s why I spoke to you. You know her, and I’ll throttle you here where we stand if you don’t tell me just what the trouble is. I don’t care for confidences or anything of the sort. You must break them all and tell me﻿—”
His hand was on my arm again, his big ugly face, now grim and obstinate, close against mine.
“I’ll tell you,” I said slowly, “all I know, which is almost nothing. The trouble is Semyonov, the doctor. Why or how I can’t say, although I’ve seen enough of him in the past to know the trouble he *can* be.

===Chinese===

她又单纯，又天真，尽管又明白事理又有智慧。……”
“没有指望！”他打断我，发出一种狞笑似的嘲弄，“我亲爱的德沃德，你以为我图什么？……强奸、通奸，然后马尔科维奇提着手枪追我们？我告诉你——”说到这里他凶狠起来，仿佛向四周整个冰封的世界挑战，“我什么也不要，只要她幸福、平安、舒心！你以为我蠢到认不出我爱她会引出什么事？我告诉你，我自己一无所图，不是因为我品格多么高尚无私，只是因为这件事太大了，大得除了她本人，什么都容不下。她永远不会知道我在乎她一个铜板，可她必须幸福，必须平安。……眼下她两样都不是，所以我才来找你。……她不幸福，她在怕，这必须改变。我要是知道你目光这么短浅，就不对你提了。……”
“行了！行了！”我不耐烦地说，“你或许是一位超乎自然法则之外的加拉哈德，劳伦斯。我不知道；不过原谅我，容我凭自己的经验说一句——我的经验是：你起点爱摆多高都可以，可爱情不会站着不动，肉体就是肉体，明天不是昨天——绝不是。再说，马尔科维奇是个俄国人，还是个特别的俄国人。最后，记住：薇拉·米哈伊洛夫娜的幸福，我要得不比你少！”
他忽然郑重起来，下面的话几乎像个孩子。
“这我知道——你是个好人，德沃德——我知道叫人信服难，我只请你等着，考验我。这件事没有人知道——这我可以起誓——也不许任何人知道；可她到底怎么了，德沃德，她在怕什么？我才是为这个找你。你了解她，你不把麻烦一五一十告诉我，我就地掐死你。我不管什么信托不信托。你必须把一切捅破，告诉我——”
他的手又落在我胳膊上，他那张又大又丑的脸凑近我，又狠又倔。
“我告诉你，”我慢慢地说，“我所知道的一切，其实少得很。麻烦在谢苗诺夫，那个医生。为什么、怎么一回事，我说不上，不过他从前的事我见得够多，知道他能掀起怎样的麻烦。”

===Original===

She’s afraid of him, and Markovitch is afraid of him. He likes playing on people’s nerves. He’s a bitter, disappointed man, who loved desperately once, as only real sensualists can﻿ ﻿… and now he’s in love with a ghost. That’s why real life maddens him.”
“Semyonov!” Lawrence whispered the name.
We had come to the end of the quay. My dear church with its round grey wall stood glistening in the moonlight, the shadows from the snow rippling up its sides, as though it lay under water. We stood and looked across the river.
“I’ve always hated that fellow,” Lawrence said. “I’ve only seen him about twice, but I believe I hated him before I saw him.﻿ ﻿… All right, Durward, that’s what I wanted to know. Thank you. Good night.”
And before I could speak he had gripped my hand, had turned back, and was walking swiftly away, across the golden-lighted quay.

===Chinese===

她怕他，马尔科维奇也怕他。他爱拨弄人的神经。他是个怨愤失意的人，从前不顾一切地爱过一回——只有真正的肉欲之徒才那样爱……如今他爱的是一个亡魂。所以现实生活叫他发疯。”
“谢苗诺夫！”劳伦斯低声念这名字。
我们走到了堤岸尽头。我那可爱的教堂，圆圆的灰墙在月光里莹莹发亮，雪的影子一层层漾上墙身，仿佛它卧在水底。我们站住，望着河对岸。
“我一向恨那家伙，”劳伦斯说，“统共只见过他两回，可我相信未见他之前就恨了。……好吧，德沃德，我要知道的就是这个。谢谢。晚安。”
我还没来得及开口，他已经攥住我的手，转身大步走了，穿过那洒满金光的堤岸。

### XX / XX
===Original===

His greeting was most amiable. He was wearing a rather short fur coat that only reached to a little below his knees, and the fur of the coat was of a deep rich brown, so that his pale square yellow beard contrasted with this so abruptly as to seem false. His body was as ever thick and self-confident, and the round fur cap that he wore was cocked ever so slightly to one side. I did not want to see him, but I was caught. I fancied that he knew very well that I wanted to escape, and that now, for sheer perversity, he would see that I did not. Indeed, he caught my arm and drew me out of the Market. We passed into the dusky streets.
“Now, Ivan Andreievitch,” he said, “this is very pleasant﻿ ﻿… very.﻿ ﻿… You elude me, you know, which is unkind with two so old acquaintances. Of course I know that you dislike me, and I don’t suppose that I have the highest opinion of *you*, but, nevertheless, we should be interested in one another. Our common experience.﻿ ﻿…” He broke off with a little shiver, and pulled his fur coat closer around him.
I knew that all that I wanted was to break away. We had passed quickly on leaving the Market into some of the meanest streets of Petrograd. This was the Petrograd of Dostoevsky, the Petrograd of *Poor Folk* and *Crime and Punishment* and *The Despised and Rejected*.﻿ ﻿… Monstrous groups of flats towered above us, and in the gathering dusk the figures that slipped in and out of the doors were furtive shadows and ghosts. No one seemed to speak; you could see no faces under the spare pale-flamed lamps, only hear whispers and smell rotten stinks and feel the snow, foul and soiled under one’s feet.﻿ ﻿…
“Look here, Semyonov,” I said, slipping from the control of his hand, “it’s just as you say. We don’t like one another, and we know one another well enough to say so. Neither you nor I wish to revive the past, and there’s nothing in the present that we have in common.”
“Nothing!” He laughed. “What about my delightful nieces and their home circle? You were always one to shrink from the truth, Ivan Andreievitch. You fancy that you can sink into the bosom of a charming family and escape the disadvantages.﻿ ﻿… Not at all. There are always disadvantages in a Russian family. *I* am the disadvantage in this one.” He laughed again, and insisted on taking my arm once more.

===Chinese===

他的招呼极其和蔼。他穿着一件相当短的皮大衣，只到膝下一点，皮子是深浓的棕色，他那淡黄色的方胡子衬在上面，突兀得像假的。他的身子一如既往地敦实而自负，头上那顶圆皮帽微微歪向一边。我并不想见到他，却被逮了个正着。我恍惚觉得，他很清楚我想脱身，如今纯粹为了呕气，偏不让我走。果然，他挽住我的胳膊，把我拉出了市场。我们走进昏暗的街道。
“呃，伊万·安德烈耶维奇，”他说，“这真是愉快极了……愉快极了。……你总躲着我，这可不该，咱们这么老的交情。我当然知道你不喜欢我，我想我对*你*的评价也高不到哪儿去，可是呢，咱们彼此该是彼此的趣事。咱们共同的那段经历。……”他打了个小小的寒噪，收住话头，把皮大衣裹紧了些。
我心里只有一件事：脱身。出了市场我们便快步走进彼得格勒几条最寒碜的街。这是陀思妥耶夫斯基的彼得格勒，《穷人》《罪与罚》《被侮辱与被损害的》的彼得格勒。……怪模怪样的公寓楼群黑压压压在我们头顶，渐浓的暮色里，从门洞里溜进溜出的人影，是鬼祟的暗影和幽灵。似乎没有一个人说话；稀疏的惨白灯下你看不清一张脸，只听得见窃窃私语，闻得到腐烂的恶臭，感觉得到脚下又脏又污的雪。……
“你听着，谢苗诺夫，”我从他手底下挣出胳膊，说，“正像你说的。咱们互相不喜欢，又熟到可以把话挑明。你我都不想重提过去，如今又毫无共通之处。”
“毫无共通之处！”他笑了。“那我那两位可爱的外甥女和她们的家庭圈子算什么？你从来就是个不敢直面事实的人，伊万·安德烈耶维奇。你妄想钻进一个可爱家庭的怀抱，就能躲开它的坏处。……没门。俄国家庭里永远有坏处。这个家的坏处就是我。”他又笑了，执意重新挽住我的胳膊。

===Original===

“If you feel so strongly about me, Durward” (when he used my surname he always accented the second syllable very strongly) “all you have to do is to cut my niece Vera out of your visiting list. That, I imagine, is the last thing that you wish. Well, then﻿—”
“Vera Michailovna is my friend,” I said hotly﻿—it was foolish of me to be so easily provoked, but I could not endure his sneering tone. “If you imply﻿—”
“Nonsense,” he answered sharply, “I imply nothing. Do you suppose that I have been more than a month here without discovering the facts? It’s your English friend Lawrence who is in love with Vera﻿—and Vera with him.”
“That is a lie!” I cried.
He laughed. “You English,” he said, “are not so unobservant as you seem, but you hate facts. Vera and your friend Lawrence have been in love with one another since their first meeting, and my dear nephew-in-law Markovitch knows it.”
“That’s impossible,” I cried. “He﻿—”
“No,” Semyonov replied, “I was wrong. He does not know it﻿—he suspects. And my nephew-in-law in a state of suspicion is a delightful study.”
By now we were in a narrow street, so dark that we stumbled at every step. We seemed to be quite alone.
It was I who now caught his arm. “Semyonov!” I said, and my urgency stopped him so that he stood where he was. “Leave them alone! Leave them alone! They’ve done no harm to you, they can offer you nothing, they are not intelligent enough for you nor amusing enough. Even if it is true what you say it will pass﻿—Lawrence will go away. I will see that he does. Only leave them alone! For God’s sake, let them be!”
His face was very close to mine, and, looking at it in the gathering dark, it was as though it were a face of glass behind which other faces passed and repassed. I cannot hope to give any idea of the strange mingling of regret, malice, pride, pain, scorn, and humour that those eyes showed. His red lips parted as though he would speak, for a moment he turned away from me and looked down the black tunnel of the street, then he walked forward again.
“You are wrong, my friend,” he said, “if you imagine that there is no amusement for me in the study of my family. It *is* my family, you know. I have none other. Perhaps it has never occurred to you, Durward, that possibly I am a lonely man.”
As he spoke I heard again the echo of that voice as it vanished into the darkness.﻿ ﻿… “No one?” and the answer: “No one.”

===Chinese===

“你既然对我嫌恶成这样，德沃德”（他叫我姓氏时，总把第二个音节咬得特别重）“那你只消把我外甥女薇拉从你的拜访名单上划掉就是。我料想，这是你最不愿做的事。那么——”
“薇拉·米哈伊洛夫娜是我的朋友。”我激动地说——这么轻易就被激怒，实在愚蠢，可我受不了他那讥诮的腔调。“你要是暗指——”
“胡说，”他尖刻地答，“我什么也不暗指。你以为我来了一个多月，会摸不清事实？是你那位英国朋友劳伦斯爱上了薇拉——薇拉也爱他。”
“这是谎话！”我叫起来。
他笑了。“你们英国人，”他说，“并非像看上去那么不长眼，只是你们恨事实。薇拉和你那位朋友劳伦斯，打头一回见面就相爱了，我那位亲爱的外甥女婿马尔科维奇是知道的。”
“这不可能，”我喊道。“他——”
“不，”谢苗诺夫答，“我说错了。他并不知道——他起疑。而我那位外甥女婿起疑时的样子，可是好看的把戏。”
这时我们已走进一条窄街，黑得步步绊脚。四下里似乎只有我们两个。
这回是我抓住了他的胳膊。“谢苗诺夫！”我喊道，我的急切竟使他站住了。“放过他们吧！放过他们吧！他们没害过你，他们给不了你什么，他们对你来说不够聪明，也不够有趣。就算你说的是真的，也会过去的——劳伦斯会走。我保证他会走。只求你放过他们！看在上帝份上，由他们去吧！”
他的脸离我很近，在渐深的黑暗里望着它，它仿佛是一张玻璃脸，后面有别的脸孔来来去去。那双眼睛里悔恨、恶毒、骄傲、痛苦、轻蔑、谐谑奇异交织的样子，我没法形容出万分之一。他的红唇张开，像要说话，有一瞬他别过脸去，朝那条黑隧道似的街望了一眼，随后又朝前走了。
“你错了，我的朋友，”他说，“你要是以为观察我这一家人对我来说没有乐趣。这*是*我的家，你要明白。我没有别的家。德沃德，也许你从没想过，我可能是个孤独的人。”
他说着，我又听见那个消失在黑暗里的声音的回响。……“没有人？”回答是：“没有人。”

===Original===

﻿ ﻿…
“Don’t imagine,” he continued, “that I am asking for your pity. That indeed would be humorous. I pity no one, and I despise the men who have it to bestow﻿ ﻿… but there are situations in life that are intolerable, Ivan Andreievitch, and any man who *is* a man will see that he escapes from such a thing. May I not find in the bosom of my family such an escape?” He laughed.
“I know nothing about that,” I began hotly. “All I know is﻿—”
But he went on as though he had not heard me.
“Have you ever thought about death since you came away from the Front, Durward? It used to occupy your mind a good deal while you were there, I remember﻿—in a foolish, romantic, sentimental way of course. You’ll forgive my saying that your views of death were those of a secondhand novelist﻿—all the same I’ll do you the justice of acknowledging that you had studied it at first hand. You’re not a coward, you know.”
I was struck most vividly with a sense of his uneasiness. During those other days uneasy was the very last thing that I ever would have said that he was﻿—even after his catastrophe his grip of his soul did not loosen. It was just that loosening that I felt now; he had less control of the beasts that dwelt beneath the ground of his house, and he could hear them snarl and whine, and could feel the floor quiver with the echo of their movements.
I suddenly knew that I was afraid of him no longer.
“Now, see, Alexei Petrovitch,” I said, “it isn’t death that we want to talk about now. It is a much simpler thing. It is, that you shouldn’t for your own amusement simply go in and spoil the lives of some of my friends for nothing at all except your own stupid pride. If that’s your plan I’m going to prevent it.”
“Why, Ivan Andreievitch,” he cried, laughing, “this is a challenge.”
“You can take it as what you please,” I answered gravely.
“But, incorrigible sentimentalist,” he went on, “tell me﻿—are you, English and moralist and believer in a good and righteous God as you are, are you really going to encourage this abominable adultery, this open, ruthless wrecking of a good man’s home? You surprise me; this is a new light on your otherwise rather uninteresting character.”
“Never mind my character,” I answered him; “all you’ve got to do is to leave Vera Michailovna alone. There’ll be no wrecking of homes, unless you are the wrecker.”
He put his hand on my arm again.

===Chinese===

……“别以为，”他接着说，“我是在讨你的怜悯。那才真叫滑稽。我不怜悯任何人，也瞧不起有怜悯可施的人……可生活里有些境况是叫人受不了的，伊万·安德烈耶维奇，凡是真正*算得上*男子汉的，都会想法子逃开这种东西。难道我不可以在自家亲人的怀抱里找到这样一个逃处？”他笑了。
“这个我不知道，”我激动地说。“我只知道——”
可他径直说下去，仿佛没听见我。
“你从前线回来以后，还想过死吗，德沃德？我记得你在前线时，死在你心里占的地方不小——当然，是以那种愚蠢的、罗曼蒂克的、多愁善感的方式。请原谅我直言：你对死亡的见解，是一个二流小说家的见解——不过话说回来，公道地讲，你毕竟亲身研究过它。你并不是懦夫，这你知道。”
我极其鲜明地感到他的不安。从前那些日子里，“不安”二字怎么也轮不到他身上——即使大难之后，他对自己灵魂的钳制也从未松过。此刻我感到的，正是那钳制的松动；他对屋子地底下那群野兽的控制弱了，他听得见它们喍叫哀鸣，感觉得到地板随着它们的骚动而震颤。
我突然明白，我不再怕他了。
“你听着，阿列克谢·彼得罗维奇，”我说，“现在咱们要谈的不是死。是件简单得多的事。就是：你不该拿别人寻开心，不该单单为了你那愚蠢的骄傲，跑去毁掉我几个朋友的生活。你要打的是这个主意，我就要拦你。”
“哎呀，伊万·安德烈耶维奇，”他笑着叫道，“这是下战书啊。”
“你爱当什么当什么。”我正色答道。
“可是，无可救药的感情主义者，”他接着说，“告诉我——你，一个英国人，一个道德家，一个信着良善公义之上帝的人，你当真要怂恿这可憎的通奸，这场对一个好人家庭明目张胆、冷酷无情的破坏？你叫我吃惊；这给你那本来相当无趣的人格添了一道新光。”
“少管我的人格，”我回答他。“你只消别去纠缠薇拉·米哈伊洛夫娜。这个家毁不了——除非毁它的人是你。”
他又把手搭上我的胳膊。

===Original===

“Listen, Durward,” he said, “I’ll tell you a little story. I’m a doctor you know, and many curious things occur within my province. Well, some years ago I knew a man who was very miserable and very proud. His pride resented that he should be miserable, and he was always suspecting that people saw his weakness, and as he despised human nature, and thought his companions fools and deserving of all that they got, and more, he couldn’t bear the thought that they should perceive that he allowed himself to be unhappy. He coveted death. If it meant extinction he could imagine nothing pleasanter than so restful an aloofness, quiet and apart and alone, whilst others hurried and scrambled and pursued the future.﻿ ﻿…
“And if death did not mean extinction then he thought that he might snatch and secure for himself something which in life had eluded him. So he coveted death. But he was too proud to reach it by suicide. That seemed to him a contemptible and cowardly evasion, and such an easy solution would have denied the purpose of all his life. So he looked about him and discovered amongst his friends a man whose character he knew well, a man idealistic and foolish and romantic, like yourself, Ivan Andreievitch, only caring more for ideas, more impulsive and more reckless. He found this man and made him his friend. He played with him as a cat does with a mouse. He enjoyed life for about a year and then he was murdered.﻿ ﻿…”
“Murdered!” I exclaimed.
“Yes﻿—shot by his idealistic friend. I envy him that year. He must have experienced many breathless sensations. When the murderer was tried his only explanation was that he had been irritated and disappointed.
“ ‘Disappointed of what?’ asked the judge.
“ ‘Of everything in which he believed.﻿ ﻿…’ said the man.
“It seemed a poor excuse for a murder; he is still, I have no doubt, in Siberia.
“But I envy my friend. That was a delightful death to die.﻿ ﻿… Good night, Ivan Andreievitch.”
He waved his hand at me and was gone. I was quite alone in the long black street, engulfed by the high, overhanging flats.

===Chinese===

“你听着，德沃德，”他说，“我给你讲个小故事。你知道，我是个医生，我这一行里有许多稀奇事。呃，若干年前我认识一个人，非常痛苦，又非常骄傲。他的骄傲见不得自己痛苦，他总疑心别人看穿了他的软弱；而他既然鄙夷人性，认定身边的人都是蠢货、吃多大亏都活该、还得再多吃些，那么一想到人家会看出他容许自己不快活，他就受不了。他觊觎死亡。倘若死就是寂灭，他想不出比这更惬意的超脱了——安静，离群，独自一人，任凭别人奔忙、争抢、追赶前程。……
“倘若死不意味着寂灭，那么他想，他或许能抢到、抓牢一件生前总也抓不着的东西。于是他觊觎死亡。但他骄傲得不屑于自杀。在他看来那是下贱懦弱的逃避，那样轻巧的解脱，会否定他一生的宗旨。于是他四下寻找，在朋友当中发现了一个他知根知底的人——一个理想主义、愚蠢、罗曼蒂克的人，就像你，伊万·安德烈耶维奇，只是更看重理念，更冲动，更不管不顾。他找到这个人，同他做了朋友。他拿他耍弄，像猫耍老鼠。他痛痛快快活了一年，然后他被谋杀了。……”
“谋杀！”我失声道。
“是啊——被他那位理想主义的朋友开枪打死的。我羡慕他那一年。他一定尝过许多叫人屏息的滋味。凶手受审时，唯一的解释是说他被惹恼了，失望了。
“「对什么失望？」法官问。
“「对他信的一切。……”那人说。
“作为杀人的理由，这借口未免可怜；毫无疑问，他如今还在西伯利亚。
“可我羡慕我那位朋友。那样一种死法，死得漂亮。……晚安，伊万·安德烈耶维奇。”
他向我挥挥手，走了。长街上只剩我一个，被两边高高探出的公寓楼吞没了。

### XXI / 二十一
===Original===

Late on the afternoon of Nina’s birthday, when I was on the point of setting out for the English Prospect, the Rat appeared. I had not seen him for several weeks; but there he was, stepping suddenly out of the shadows of my room, dirty and disreputable and cheerful. He had been, I perceived, drinking furniture polish.
“Good evening, *barin*.”
“Good evening,” I said sternly. “I told you not to come here when you were drunk.”
“I’m not drunk,” he said, offended, “only a little. It’s not much that you can get these days. I want some money, *barin*.”
“I’ve none for you,” I answered.
“It’s only a little﻿—God knows that I wouldn’t ask you for much, but I’m going to be very busy these next days, and it’s work that won’t bring pay quickly. There’ll be pay later, and then I will return it to you.”
“There’s nothing for you tonight,” I said.
He laughed. “You’re a fine man, *barin*. A foreigner is fine﻿—that’s where the poor Russian is unhappy. I love you, *barin*, and I will look after you, and if, as you say, there isn’t any money here, one must pray to God and he will show one the way.”
“What’s this work you’re going to do?” I asked him.
“There’s going to be trouble the other side of the river in a day or two,” he answered, “and I’m going to help.”
“Help what?” I asked.
“Help the trouble,” he answered, smiling.
“Behave like a blackguard, in fact.”
“Ah, blackguard, *barin*!” he protested, using a Russian word that is worse than blackguard. “Why these names?﻿ ﻿… I’m not a good man, God have mercy on my soul, but then I pretend nothing. I am what you see.﻿ ﻿… If there’s going to be trouble in the town I may as well be there. Why not I as well as another? And it is to your advantage, *barin*, that I should be.”
“Why to my advantage?” I asked him.
“Because I am your friend, and we’ll protect you,” he answered.
“I wouldn’t trust you a yard,” I told him.
“Well, perhaps you’re right,” he said. “We are as God made us﻿—I am no better than the rest.”
“No, indeed you’re not,” I answered him. “Why do you think there’ll be trouble?”
“I know.﻿ ﻿… Perhaps a lot of trouble, perhaps only a little. But it will be a fine time for those of us who have nothing to lose.﻿ ﻿… So you have no money for me?”
“Nothing.”
“A mere rouble or so?”
“Nothing.”
“Well, I must be off.﻿ ﻿… I am your friend. Don’t forget,” and he was gone.

===Chinese===

尼娜生日那天傍晚，我正要动身去英国大道，老鼠来了。我好几个星期没见他了；可他就在那儿，突然从我房间的暗处跨出来，邋過，寒磣，乐呵呵。我瞧出他喝的是家具上光蜡。
“晚上好，老爷（barin）。”
“晚上好。”我板着脸说，“我说过，喝醉了不许上这儿来。”
“我没醉，”他受了委屈似的说，“就一点点。如今也弄不着多的。我要点钱，老爷。”
“没你的份。”我回答。
“就一点儿——天知道我不会多要您的，可这几天我要大忙了，那活儿钱回得慢。往后有进项，我再还您。”
“今晚什么也没有。”我说。
他笑了。“您是位好老爷。外国人好——可怜的俄国人就苦在这儿。我爱您，老爷，我会照应您的；既然您说这儿一个钱也没有，那就只好求上帝指条路了。”
“你要去干什么活？”我问他。
“一两天后河对岸要出事，”他答道，“我去帮忙。”
“帮忙干什么？”我问。
“帮那个事。”他笑着答。
“说白了，去当无赖。”
“哎，无赖，老爷！”他抗议道，用了一个俄语词，比无赖难听得多的词，“干吗这么骂人？……我不是好人，上帝可怜我的灵魂，可我从不装假。我就是您看见的这副样子。……城里要出事，我不去凑凑热闹？凭什么别人去得我去不得？再说，我在场对您有利，老爷。”
“为什么对我有利？”
“因为我是您的朋友，我们会保护您。”他答。
“你呀，一寸都不能信。”我对他说。
“嗯，兴许您说得对，”他说，“上帝造我们什么样就是什么样——我不比别人强。”
“是，你是不比谁强。”我答他，“你凭什么觉得要出事？”
“我知道。……兴许是大事，兴许是小事。可对我们这些没什么可输的人来说，是好时候。……这么说，没钱给我？”
“没有。”
“一卢布上下也不行？”
“没有。”
“好吧，我该走了。……我是您朋友。别忘了。”人已经没了。

===Original===

It had been arranged that Nina and Vera, Lawrence and Bohun and I should meet outside the Giniselli at five minutes to eight. I left my little silver box at the flat, paid some other calls, and just as eight o’clock was striking arrived outside the Giniselli. This is Petrograd’s apology for a music-hall﻿—in other words, it is nothing but the good old-fashioned circus.
Then, again, it is not quite the circus of one’s English youth, because it has a very distinct Russian atmosphere of its own. The point really is the enthusiasm of the audience, because it is an enthusiasm that in these sophisticated, twentieth-century days is simply not to be found in any other country in Europe. I am an old-fashioned man and, quite frankly, I adore a circus; and when I can find one with the right sawdust smell, the right clown, and the right enthusiasm, I am happy. The smart night is a Saturday, and then, if you go, you will see, in the little horseboxes close to the arena, beautiful women in jewellery and powder, and young officers, and fat merchants in priceless *shubas*. But tonight was not a Saturday, and therefore the audience was very democratic, screaming catcalls from the misty distances of the gallery, and showering sunflower seeds upon the heads of the bourgeoisie, who were, for the most part, of the smaller shopkeeper kind.
Nina, tonight, was looking very pretty and excited. She was wearing a white silk dress with blue bows, and all her hair was piled on the top of her head in imitation of Vera﻿—but this only had the effect of making her seem incredibly young and naive, as though she had put her hair up just for the evening because there was to be a party. It was explained that Markovitch was working but would be present at supper. Vera was quiet, but looked happier, I thought, than I had seen her for a long time. Bohun was looking after her, and Lawrence was with Nina. I sat behind the four of them, in the back of the little box, like a presiding Benevolence.
Mostly I thought of how lovely Vera was tonight, and why it was, too, that more people did not care for her. I knew that she was not popular, that she was considered proud and reserved and cold.
